one 
New Cooking, Ancient Wisdom
I live with my family in a busy neighborhood in New York City. Tere’s a wealth of food 
available in easy reach. Within a single block of our home there is a bakery, an Australian-style 
café, restaurants serving classic Szechuan, modern Chinese fusion, sushi, artsy ramen, Basque 
tapas, gourmet pizza, casual pizza, northern Italian cuisine, American modern, artisan sand-
wich takeout, a place that sells only pulled pork sandwiches, and a doughnut chain. Walk 
another block and there are more options.  Tere are even two diners open 24 hours, across the 
avenue from each other—if you like, you can have eggs or chicken parm with cherry pie at 2 
a.m.  while looking through the window across traﬃc at someone having eggs or chicken parm 
with apple pie in the other place. Food delivery is also available from a wide range of options 
within minutes. Tere’s a lot of food. 
Tis isn’t unique to my neighborhood. Whether you live in a big city, the suburbs, or the 
countryside, you can probably ﬁnd plenty of convenient and appealing meals that tempt you 
to cook at home less frequently. And more than that, everybody is busy. Life can be fast-paced. 
Leisure time needs to be scheduled, even date night with your special. It’s a tugging rope con-
test between how we live and why we live. And of course, there is always the pull of movies, 
television, news, computers and the ubiquitous phone. It’s no surprise that cooking at home 
is not everyone’s daily priority. 
Everybody eats, but because everyone is diﬀerent, everyone’s diet should be personal. Our diets 
need to be shifting with the seasons, with the stages of life, with our changing schedules, and 
with our aspirations and dreams, whether we are striving forward or regrouping our energies. 
Most importantly, our diets need to adapt to the speciﬁcs of our personal health. 
With this in mind, it’s easy to see why depending too much on even a cornucopia of restau-
rants can’t possibly provide a genuinely health-supportive diet. Professional food is a tough 
business with slim margins; the operating strategy is to entice patrons to order more and to 
return often. It’s no surprise that menus (whether formally printed or just posted over a take-

<!-- p.8 -->
2           New Cooking, Ancient Wisdom
out counter) are trendy and enticing, oﬀering large portions with theatrical ﬂavors. It’s not so 
easy to get the food we really need.
Ten there is all the conﬂicting advice. Tere’s health advice from newspapers, magazines, 
television, radio, government, websites, from family, neighbors, acquaintances. Tere are in-
dependent studies, there are industry-funded studies, there are industry-funded independent 
studies. Health care and agriculture are for-proﬁt businesses, and whether that is best or not, 
this inﬂuences choices and the ﬂow of information without doubt. Te doctors I know are 
frustrated with the business pressure they are under, and while they enthusiastically agree that 
they should train more in diet, each year there is less time to do so.   
In the midst of all this, what is needed is practical guidance on these two points:
 » How to eat for your personal health (seen clearly and comprehensively).
 » How to shop and prepare meals that you will be willing to make and love to eat.
In my practice, I work with an unscientiﬁc sampling of the population, but even so, there are 
some who never cook, some who cook only on holidays, some avid amateur cooks and some 
with professional chef training. People from all walks of life and diﬀerent backgrounds come 
for dietary consults. I’ve seen kitchens where the oven stores old tax returns and there are 
novels piled on the never-used stovetop; others have constantly used kitchen equipment and 
every spice available. Teaching food practice in a group is similarly tricky—there are novices 
and experts, vegetarians and no-carb carnivores. Honestly, however, it’s not as diﬃcult as it 
sounds, because I have no intention of telling anyone what they should do, especially in some-
thing as emotionally charged as what foods to eat. What is important, however, is to introduce 
time-tested knowledge of how to know for yourself the ways your diet is aﬀecting your health 
and some really delicious ways to renew your life. 
Tere is a saying in Chinese medicine: if you want to be healthy, you have to spend two hours 
in the kitchen each day cooking your own food…okay, that may not happen, but let’s say one 
hour. You may not be able to give even one hour, every day, but perhaps you will do some extra 
cooking on your day oﬀ, or have some things you’ve already prepared to add to a quick-cook-
ing dinner. In this book, I’ve included many cook’s tips and suggestions to help you be more

<!-- p.9 -->
New Cooking, Ancient Wisdom           3
eﬃcient in the kitchen. Comfort will grow with practice and experience.
Te recipes included here accompany the ﬁrst volume of Welcoming Food. Tere, we took a tour 
of digestion in order to understand good digestion in practical terms, and what can commonly go 
wrong. If health is perfect, then we eat for the seasons and to celebrate life. But each of us has some 
health issues, large or small, and as we learn how foods work it’s common to have “aha” experienc-
es. “Oh! Now I know when to use chicken soup and when not to!”  Or, “Now I understand why 
I get so tired after lunch….”  Tese points (and so much more) are fundamental to understanding 
diet for ourselves in a way that answers our questions and clariﬁes what we really need to know.
Te recipes I write are very easy and very basic. Tey are teaching templates—once you know 
the principles, the details can change as long as you apply the principles to the foods you 
choose. Unlike the consistency required in restaurant kitchens, the home cook has the ad-
vantage of ﬂexibility. Shop like an artist, look for what inspires. Cook for yourself and others 
today, according to what’s going on for you. As you gain experience in this type of cooking, 
you will gain conﬁdence, eventually becoming a skilled home health director for yourself and 
your family. Many aspects of life and healing are important, but none more so than being able 
to cook for health. 
Here are the keys to kitchen success:
 » Make simple food. Simple food is the highest achievement (and the best to digest). 
 » Good ingredients make good meals. Spend more on ingredients and less in eateries. 
 » Use the special knowledge passed on here and elsewhere to select foods, make dishes 
and create meals.
 » All good cooking is personal. Select and adapt recipes according to what you really 
need, reﬁning this skill as your knowledge deepens. 
Following these basic guidelines, you can make the best tasting medicine in the world. 
It’s often said that food is the best medicine. Basic healthy eating supports general health, but 
with the right knowledge, meals can become real medicine. More than that, we have a great 
advantage over other types of medicine, a secret weapon: personally healthful foods can be

<!-- p.10 -->
4           New Cooking, Ancient Wisdom
wonderful to eat. Consider this: modern drugs all have side eﬀects, acupuncture can be un-
comfortable, herbal medicines can be bitter, meditation and exercise require discipline. But 
with the right knowledge and good ingredients, the most important health practice of all can 
be simply delicious. Tere is a catch, though—we have to be honest and make real changes in 
the food habits that have been hurting us. Healing requires avoiding what harms us as well as 
including what helps. Once that pesky little detail is out of the way, we can understand how a 
personally appropriate diet is truly the best tasting medicine in the world. 
How to Make Food into Meals
Meal planning is where understanding food rises to the advanced level. Taste is an important 
guide but not the only signiﬁcant one—how foods work together in digestion and throughout 
our bodies is extremely important.
Te ﬁrst consideration returns to the idea of clear intention: what is your goal here?   
 » I’m making a quick dinner because I’m going out! 
 » It’s been a long day, I want to relax with something nice. 
 » Tonight the family is all home, we should have a good dinner. 
 » It’s hay fever season soon, let’s adjust our diet now so Jane doesn’t have such 
a tough time. 
 » Jimmy isn’t feeling well—we’ll all share meals that support him until he’s better.
We have multiple ideas when we cook, but one must be the lead. We eat to satisfy hunger, to 
taste what we like, to socialize, to feel better physically or emotionally, for many overlapping 
reasons. When planning a meal, cover the bases while keeping one idea on top. Some hypo-
thetical examples will be helpful. 
We want a tasty meal that is nourishing, but I’ve noticed that:
 » My lips seem dry, so let’s include a soup, avoid hot spices, include zucchini and the 
grain can be steamed rice. Or,

<!-- p.11 -->
New Cooking, Ancient Wisdom           5
 » One of our kids has a nasty, dry cough. Let’s have chicken soup, kale with olive oil 
and sea salt, steamed millet as the grain with sliced almonds on top. Or, 
 » I’ve missed clearing my bowels for a day or two. Let’s have brown rice as the center 
food, with spiced black beans, sautéed chard, steamed carrots dressed with olive oil, 
and no meat because I don’t want meat sitting in there if I have stagnation. And no 
sugar, because sugar binds the intestines. Or, 
 » I’ve had a heavy menstrual period and work’s been tough. I’m exhausted and need 
some me time. I’m going to make a steak at home, with string beans cooked in 
butter and balsamic vinegar. A sweet potato because I love them, and a glass of red 
wine because I get to (and Chinese medicine says a little wine nourishes blood). (If 
vegetarian, substitute adzuki beans for the steak, add a side dish of beets, and have 
some berries for breakfast the next day.)  Or, 
 » I’m always famished before dinner. I’m a teenager and my appetite is insane. I’m go-
ing to take a sandwich to school, made of bread, sliced meat, and cheese. In fact, I’m 
going to take two of them. According to my extensive studies of Chinese medicine 
dietetics, I know that a sandwich combines foods that are tough to digest together, 
and that will keep me full for a long time. To help digestion, I’ll put some good 
French mustard on it, and some lettuce because it’s green. 
As discussed in Book One of Welcoming Food, one or two truly appropriate meals can make a 
world of diﬀerence in how we feel. But if chronic illness is present, the foods you select must 
be eaten long-term to have a medicinal eﬀect (and “avoid” foods must be avoided long-term). 
For most people, however, most meals can be generally health-supportive (rather than thera-
peutic). Tat being said, it is certain that in a life there are times when everyone could beneﬁt 
from a more medicinal approach to food. Te cook’s intention must be ﬂuid between generally 
healthful eating and therapeutic meals. 
Te basics for general meal design follow the idea of feeding all aspects of digestion. A basic meal 
is made of:
 » Grain
 » Green
 » Protein

<!-- p.12 -->
6           New Cooking, Ancient Wisdom
Although each meal can be diﬀerent, when you look at your plate you should see a grain (rice, 
bread, noodles, and potato or sweet potato can be included here as “grain”) some green vegeta-
bles (broccoli, kale, chard, string beans, cabbage, etc.) and a protein food (chicken, beef, lamb, 
ﬁsh, eggs, or beans, tofu, nuts and seeds). To this you can add: 
 » Root vegetables (carrots, beets, parsnips, celery root, etc.)
 » Mushrooms
 » Squashes (zucchini, bitter melon, etc.)  Some squashes, such as butternut, can also 
be considered “grain”, used as the carb of the meal.
As discussed in Book One, spices and kitchen herbs are very important to aid digestion:
 »  Leafy herbs: oregano, basil, rosemary, tarragon, bay leaf, chives, parsley, cilantro, 
etc.
 »  Seed spices: cumin, caraway, cardamom, coriander, fennel seed, star anise, nutmeg, 
allspice, black peppercorn, Szechuan peppercorn, etc.
 » Root herbs: fresh ginger, turmeric
 » Stem and bud spices: cinnamon, clove
You may need a true appetizer: 
 » Olives
 » Artichoke hearts
 » Heart of palm
 » Crunchy things like raw carrots and celery
 » Pickled things
You may need soup. One of the best things we can do for ourselves is to eat more soup, any 
time of year. Along with the rise of fast-food culture has been a decline in soup consumption. 
Soups warm digestion while providing hydration and nourishment. Hydration with food (not 
a drink with a meal but in the food) hydrates the body better than water because it digests 
relatively slowly, rather than running through us.

<!-- p.13 -->
New Cooking, Ancient Wisdom           7
 » Clear soup for warm weather
 » Tick, hearty soup in cool seasons or as a main course
 » Miso soup as a fermented appetizer
Some things should make it into meals rarely: 
 »  Nuts and seeds are best eaten alone as a snack (especially nuts). In certain recipe 
combinations, however, they can be skillfully included (especially seeds). 
 »  Fruit is best eaten between meals as a stand-alone snack, but on occasion, fruits can 
be cooked into dishes according to traditional guidelines (such as apricots or prunes 
cooked with fatty meats to aid digestion). 
 »  Dairy is also best eaten between meals as a snack (if at all). Many cuisines include 
dairy in cooking—have enough to be satisﬁed if your health tolerates it (some reci-
pes here include dairy). Always plan for yourself; substitutes can be used. 
 »  Sugar. Sugar easily interferes with digestion of other carbs and proteins. Eﬀects are 
both short- and long-term. 
Check for roughage. Looking at your stove (or imagining your plate of food), is there ﬁber? 
Are you keeping the microbial army in your gut happy?  If not, switch to a whole grain, or add 
some nuts as an appetizer. Remember, diﬀerent ﬁber feeds diﬀerent microbes; using only one 
type will not do. Eat broadly and keep in mind that when it comes to ﬁber, sudden changes 
catch the microbiotic army oﬀ guard. Cultivate the troops by steady consumption of a wide 
range of dietary ﬁbers. It’s easy to do: 
 » Rotate your foods.
 »  Eat grains as grains (that is, as discernible kernels of farro, rice, millet, bulgur, buck-
wheat, oats, barley, etc., and not just as ingredients in breads, pasta, or processed 
foods). 
 » Eat a variety of vegetables.
 » Include root vegetables.
 » Check for too many ﬁber-free foods (meat, ﬁsh, peeled foods, reﬁned carbs).

<!-- p.14 -->
8           New Cooking, Ancient Wisdom
If generally healthful eating isn’t enough, in other words, if a condition persists that interferes 
with your freedom or worries you for the future, enter into therapeutic eating by ﬁrst commit-
ting to clear meals, following the ideas presented in chapter 7 in Book One of Welcoming Food: 
simplify meals for better digestion, and if needed, separate protein meals from carb meals, have 
plenty of vegetables with either, keep fruits and dairy oﬀ by themselves. 
Remember, for therapeutic eating, consistency is essential and repetition is key. Intention be-
comes a message when speciﬁc foods are consistently present in the diet. Te body will respond. 
Since the world’s ﬁrst written recipe was pressed into a palm-size clay tablet some ﬁve thousand 
years ago (instructions for fermenting barley beer, with more prayers than ingredients), untold 
recipes have been written and forgotten. Nonetheless, there are a number of gaps to ﬁll (or at 
least to address) at the current time: 
 »  Much of what had been taught in families has been let go, things learned in the 
kitchen as deeply as the local accents or way of saying hello. For example, in my 
wife’s family some of the elder women still make fresh bread ﬁrst thing every morn-
ing, every single day. It’s the bread they learned from their grandmothers, no one 
knows when it started. Tis kind of osmosis learning is no longer being passed 
forward. 
 »  Recipes follow trends, and many aim to break the boredom, to introduce new 
things. Tat’s a big contribution, but it’s not the goal here. Here, we are looking at 
simplifying your time in the kitchen while adding insight into the foods you are 
buying, cooking and eating. 
 » Restaurants open and celebrity chefs dazzle us with technique and charisma, but we 
need to cook something every day. What’s for dinner on Tuesday that won’t break 
the bank or exhaust our kitchen patience?  We need to reinvent everyday cooking. 
 »  Many people cook well but are afraid of the ultimate basics, like steaming rice or 
grilling a piece of ﬁsh. Tis is understandable—there is no place to hide with the 
simplest of foods, and, referring to the ﬁrst point, the family traditions of what 
had been daily food staples are not being passed on in complete form. We need to 
turn our attention away from food entertainment as we gradually awaken to the 
function of diet both for our inner world and the realities of farming, commerce, 
environment and socioeconomics in the outer world. Te way to begin is to focus

<!-- p.15 -->
New Cooking, Ancient Wisdom           9
on high-quality, simple food, even if we are good at more advanced cooking. 
 »  It’s good to eat according to your heritage foods, the foods your ancestors adapted 
to over generations, but eating has never been more international than now and 
we need to learn the basics of some traditions we may not have grown up with. 
For some, a wheat intolerance may stimulate us to learn to cook rice and millet, or 
the intellectual understanding of the beneﬁts of fermented foods may inspire us to 
master kimchi or miso. Tere are reasons to explore our comfort foods again as well 
as learn new things. 
 »  And mostly, we need to think about food in a new way, or rather, to be ﬂuent in 
multiple ways of thinking of our foods. We need to understand food in the context 
of its energetics (for lack of a better term), in other words, what eﬀects do our meals 
have on us personally once they have been eaten?  
Remember the principles in the beginning of Book One: 
Diet is deeply individual. Te way foods work inside us diﬀers from person to person, to some 
extent from culture to culture, and even within each of us during diﬀerent times of our lives. 
Most importantly, the way we respond to foods varies according to our personal health. In 
order to understand and use this successfully:
 »  We must develop some information about the properties of the foods we eat, their 
combinations, and the way they are cooked.
 »  We need to have some way of assessing our health status in practical terms, in order 
to know our particular circumstances and tendencies.
In Book One, we toured digestion and the way diﬀerent foods resonate with the various organs 
and essential functions. Here in Book Two are recipes to put that knowledge into practice. 
We’ll begin with basics: some true appetizers, beautifully simple ways to cook grains, a few 
vegetables, some representative cooking methods for ﬁsh, meats and the principal vegetarian 
options, some breakfasts, a few desserts, and some sauces and condiments. We’re ready to look 
at cooking in a profoundly new way.

<!-- p.16 -->
two
True Appetizers
A true appetizer helps digestion welcome food. In contrast to poorly chosen 
appetizers that ﬁll our bellies and slow digestion (bread and cheese are 
both poor appetizers), true appetizers fulﬁll four functions: stimulate sali-
va, clear food stagnation, help food descend, and enliven appetite by gently 
raising stomach ﬁre. 
A quick story. After performing a concert in Cartagena, an ancient port city on the 
Spanish Mediterranean coast, we went for a late meal at a local taverna. Standing at 
the counter bar as is the custom, a smoked ﬁsh caught my eye. I tried to order, mostly 
by smiling and gesturing, but this was met with animated and elaborate Spanish 
beyond my simple abilities. A local gentleman who had just heard our performance 
approached and graciously translated. “Te owner wants you to know that this type 
of smoked ﬁsh is always accompanied by one small slice of a very particular locally 
smoked cheese, steamed fava beans, and bread. And he says that since it all tends to sit 
in the belly in a heavy fashion, he wouldn’t give you any of it unless you have a dish 
of olives ﬁrst, and by the way, the olives he is oﬀering were picked locally and cured by 
his wife in the back of the taverna using the heirloom family recipe.”  Te food was 
so beautiful and the message so inspired that it formed an unforgettable experience. 
Teachers appear in unlikely taverns, one could say.

<!-- p.17 -->
Appetizer Platter          11
Appetizer Platter
In Chinese medicine, the term “kai wei” means to “open the stomach”. Like 
opening the front door of our home to welcome guests, an appetizer opens the 
stomach to welcome food. An easy platter of true appetizers works at more 
than one level to make even a simple meal very successful. 
Ingredients
brined or marinated olives, artichoke hearts, heart of palm 
carrots, celery, radishes
olive oil
sea salt
balsamic or apple cider vinegar
Directions
Arrange these foods as you like, on a presentation platter or simply in bowls. Pour some good 
olive oil into a ﬂat dish for dipping (always use good to very good extra virgin olive oil—for 
lighter oils, choose oils from nuts or seeds rather than “light” olive oil). Next to that, place a 
ﬂat dish with some high-quality ﬂaky salt, and another with tasting vinegar if desired.  
Te olives, artichokes, and heart of palm are already brined or marinated, ready to eat as they are. 
Te carrots and celery can be raw if basic digestion is good, or lightly steamed if raw food is 
not appropriate. Slice into ﬁnger-size pieces. 
Trim oﬀ the green tops of the radishes; slice in half if you like. 
Use your ﬁngers to eat the raw vegetables. Dip the carrots and celery into the olive oil (and bal-
samic vinegar if you wish). Touch the radish lightly to the salt before eating with your ﬁngers, 
as is the custom in France and much of the Mediterranean.

<!-- p.18 -->
12          True Appetizers
A Note on the Food Energetics
Tese are welcoming foods. On the external level, a dish of appetizers welcomes guests: “Come 
in, come in. Have some olives and some veggies while we chat, we’re so glad you’re here!” 
On the internal level, these are true appetizers, fulﬁlling the criteria of opening the stomach 
as described in classical Chinese medicine dietary teachings. Olives and marinated artichoke 
hearts both provide a descending inﬂuence. In general, pickled or brined foods stimulate the 
gallbladder to aid digestion and move things along within the alimentary canal. Heart of palm 
can be a nice accompaniment if desired. 
Crunchy vegetables stimulate digestion by vibrating key stomach meridian acupuncture points 
that lie on the jaw—a message is sent from the jaw, signaling digestion to open and welcome 
food. But even from a laboratory nutrition point of view, carrots are considered more nutri-
tious when cooked, particularly in the presence of oil (the vitamins in carrots are oil-soluble 
and are absorbed much better when carrots are eaten with, for example, olive oil, as described 
here). A small amount of raw carrot very powerfully clears food stagnation from overeating, 
but if digestion is weak it’s better to steam them to one degree or another. Te cook must 
decide whether to present a few things raw for their crunch, lightly steamed to add warmth 
but preserve some crunch, or to cook more fully to give up the crunch in favor of the beneﬁt 
cooking provides to the organs of the belly. Tere is no single answer. 
Celery also oﬀers the crunchy quality that stimulates the stomach through jaw vibration, and 
radishes provide crunch along with their characteristic sharpness (this taste helps digest fats).
Te vinegar helps raise stomach ﬁre to digest the main meal. Use a good balsamic, or an unﬁl-
tered apple cider vinegar (known by devotees simply as ACV). 
Remember, these are just nibbles. We are not eating a large raw salad. We are not trying to 
trick the stomach into feeling full without providing much building food, as in a standard 
weight loss strategy. Rather, these are small appetizers to prepare the internal organs for better 
digestion of a well-chosen meal.

<!-- p.19 -->
Miso Dip with Cucumbers          13
Miso Dip with Cucumbers 
Although complex variations are possible, with good ingredients the simple 
preparations can be best. Miso dip is a perfect example. Have as an appetizer 
or side dish to take advantage of the fermentation of good miso. Snack on a 
few miso-cucumber nibbles while cooking the main meal. Cooks need to look 
after themselves, too. 
Serves: eight
Prep time: 5 minutes
Total time: 5 minutes
Ingredients
organic red miso | 4 Tbsp
toasted sesame oil | 1/2 tsp
unwaxed cucumbers | 12 small, cut in 1 inch chunks
Directions
Place the best quality red miso you can ﬁnd in a bowl. I recommend Hatcho miso if you can 
get it, but any good miso will do.
Mix the toasted sesame oil in roughly (try not to smooth the miso too much). 
Use slender, unwaxed cucumbers, Persian, Japanese, English hothouse or Kirby.
Spoon a generous amount  of miso-sesame oil mix onto the cut end of a 1-inch chunk of cu-
cumber, eat as ﬁnger snack.  
For options, include carrot stick and celery sections for dipping or spreading the miso. Vari-
ations on the dip can include mixing sesame tahini into the miso in a 1:3 ratio (one measure 
tahini: three measures miso).

<!-- p.20 -->
14         True Appetizers
A Note on the Food Energetics
High quality miso requires refrigeration; it’s a living food, full of healthy microbes. To make 
the most of this, avoid heating it. Miso dip is perfect. It’s simple to make and delicious to eat.
Miso eaten this way is a probiotic, providing healthy microbes to the intestines. Once deliv-
ered, these microbes colonize where needed, ideally protecting terrain from “bad” microbes 
(and helping to digest other foods). 
Tere always is the question of whether living probiotic microbes can pass through the stom-
ach (with its acids) to arrive alive where needed. Tink like a detective: 
 »  All babies are born sterile; no microbes reside in a newborn’s intestines. Coloni-
zation begins quickly after birth. So, at least at the beginning of life, every single 
person does gather microbes to their gut. 
 »  Babies have “weak” digestion (by adult standards), with very low stomach acid levels. 
 »  Microbes colonize so successfully in the intestines within the ﬁrst year precisely 
because stomach acids are low (an example of how perfectly the system is designed). 
 » In order to simulate conditions that allow microbes to pass the stomach as safely 
as they do for babies, avoid protein before eating probiotics such as miso (protein 
requires high stomach acid for digestion and therefore stimulates acidic secretions). 
Delivering miso on raw vegetables is quite good, if the raw cucumber can be tolerat-
ed well. For those who should avoid all cold or raw food, miso soup is best (but wait 
10-20 minutes before eating protein such as ﬁsh afterward).

<!-- p.21 -->
Arugala Salad with Paprika Almonds          15 
Arugula Salad with Paprika Almonds
For those with robust digestion, here is a raw appetizer salad for warm 
weather. It is invigorating and energetically descending, just what is needed 
to prepare digestion and prepare appetite. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 10 minutes
Total time: 25 minutes
Ingredients
whole raw almonds | 2 cups
smoked Spanish paprika | 1 tsp
arugula | a handful for each person, about 1/2 pound altogether
For the Dressing
fresh lemon juice | 2 tsp
olive oil | 4 Tbsp
balsamic vinegar | 1 Tbsp
dijon mustard | 1/2 tsp
sea salt | chef’s pinch
Directions
First, prepare the almonds (can be done even several days in advance). Preheat oven to 400˚F. 
Moisten the whole almonds with water (or a splash of olive oil) then toss them with the pa-
prika. Roast in the oven, spreading them out on a sheet so each can “see” the heat, turning 
occasionally, 6-8 minutes only. Remove, allow to cool or use warm. 
Prepare the dressing by whisking together the lemon juice, balsamic vinegar, and the mustard, 
then drizzling in the olive oil while continuing to whisk. Add a pinch of salt. 
Add the dressing to the arugula, toss with clean ﬁngers (cooks should touch their food—the 
modern practice of plastic gloves in professional kitchens is not necessary at home).

<!-- p.22 -->
16          True Appetizers
Taste for salt. Add a quarter handful of paprika almonds to each portion of arugula as you are 
plating. 
Tis well-known arugula salad is usually served with shaved parmesan cheese and cracked 
black pepper. Use your discretion and knowledge of food and health to decide. As an appetizer, 
it’s more eﬀective without cheese. With the parmesan (a sheep cheese that many ﬁnd easier to 
digest than cow cheeses) and perhaps some whole grain berries, it’s a complete meal. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Arugula is bitter and spicy. Some people shouldn’t have any raw food (use the Cooked Salad 
recipe on page 49), but if the weather is warm and digestion strong, raw arugula can be deli-
cious and invigorating.  In Chinese medicine dietetics, arugula is classiﬁed as slightly warming, 
because it invigorates rather than slows digestion (it also supports lung function). It needs little 
seasoning because it is intrinsically spicy (and a bit bitter). Taste your batch—farmers continue 
to select milder varieties, which is somewhat disappointing (if your arugula—or rocket—lacks 
the characteristic bitter and spicy ﬂavor, use a less sweet vinegar than balsamic, such as red 
wine vinegar). Te bitterness of arugula aids descension and relieves abdominal pain. It helps 
clear dangerous toxins from the liver (called ﬁre toxins in Chinese medicine). Its spicy and 
warming quality makes arugula a powerful food to help the lungs recover from common cold 
accompanied by chills. 
Almonds are a “lung and large intestine” nut. Tey are descending, as is arugula, making this 
salad a good appetizer. Almonds also moisten the intestines with their oils and ﬁber. Together, 
almonds and arugula clear food stagnation and promote appetite. 
Paprika is ground from a variety of dried peppers, originally from the Americas but adopted 
long ago from Spain to Eastern Europe. Some are dried over ﬁres to add a smoky ﬂavor. Papri-
ka can be hot (rare) or sweet (more common). Of course, peppers and paprika are nightshades, 
but we are not eating them in large amounts, here it is a dusting, used to invite appetite and 
invigorate the blood, just a little.

<!-- p.23 -->
Soups as Appetizers          17
Soups as Appetizers
Just as a warm smile welcomes guests into your home, a hot soup can be a true appetizer by 
warming digestion, opening the stomach to welcome food.
Te stomach can only work when nicely warm. Cold in the stomach can underlie various 
physical problems.  Lack of appetite, bloating after eating even small portions, undigested 
food in the stool, anemia, loose stool or tendency toward diarrhea, pain in the joints, weak 
bones, and even some common types of infertility can be traced to cold in the stomach. Even 
many cases of acid reﬂux can result from cold, as the stomach works too hard to produce ﬁery 
acid to counteract food and drinks that are physically or energetically cold. 
Te best appetizer soups are relatively thin and digest very easily, such as miso soup or Clear 
Soup (page 88). A puréed carrot ginger soup can also be very good (carrots are descending 
and ginger warms digestion). Since the carrots are cooked and puréed in a blender or with a 
handheld mixer, this appetizer is particularly beneﬁcial for those who aren’t good with raw 
carrots or other raw foods.. 
In any case, appetizer soups should be served in small to moderate amounts. An appetizer 
should not ﬁll the stomach; its job is to open the stomach to welcome food and help digestion 
of the meal that is to come.

<!-- p.24 -->
three
Grains
When well cooked, grains are more than a side dish, they are the founda-
tion of good digestion and good meals. Here are representative recipes for 
white and whole rice, millet, polenta, buckwheat, and antique wheat.

<!-- p.25 -->
Steamed White and Brown Rice          19
Steamed White Rice (and Brown Rice)
More than mere carbs, rice strongly beneﬁts digestion. White rice supports 
hydration and the protective lining of the alimentary canal. Brown rice aids 
peristalsis and good elimination. Both are useful and delicious when cooked 
well. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 5 minutes
Total time: 20 minutes
Ingredients
medium or long grain white rice | 1 cup
water | 1.5 cup (perhaps 1.75 cup)
Directions
Historically, rice needed to be rinsed well, until the running water remained clear. Modern 
commercial rice doesn’t require washing, but many of us rinse it thoroughly nonetheless. If 
you rinse, use a strainer afterward to rid the rice of all the rinsing water, then proceed as below. 
Use between 1.5 and 2 measures cool water to 1 measure of rice (exact amount varies accord-
ing to speciﬁc rice, how dry it seems, weather, and your cooking preference). After experience, 
it is not necessary to measure the rice and water. A traditional method is to add enough water 
so that it comes above the rice by one ﬁnger joint. In other words, with the rice in the pot, 
add enough cool water so that as you touch the top of the rice with your ﬁngertip, the water 
comes up to the ﬁrst joint. 
Bring rice and water to a boil, then immediately reduce to lowest simmer and cover with a 
tight-ﬁtting lid. Do not lift the lid, do not stir the rice. Steam rivulets will form between the 
grains; stirring or ﬂuﬃng will disrupt these channels, trapping moisture below and making a 
mess. 
Turn oﬀ the heat at 15 minutes for white rice (40 minutes for brown rice) but do not disturb 
the lid. Allow it to coast. As the rice gradually cools, the expansive quality within the pot be-

<!-- p.26 -->
20         Grains
gins to reverse and the grains will contract a bit, making a perfect balance of holding-togeth-
er-stickiness and individual grain integrity. 
After 5-10 minutes resting (during which time your other dishes are ﬁnished), serve the rice 
and its accompaniments (vegetables, beans, ﬁsh, meat, mushrooms, whatever you have pre-
pared). Serve as quickly as possible to give everyone the pleasure of steaming food. When 
executed well, home cooking can provide a freshness and immediacy of stove-to-table that is 
impossible for any but the very rarest restaurants to achieve. 
Rice Cooker or Pressure Cooker Option 
Lately I have been using our pressure cooker more. I like it in the winter and tend to ignore 
it in warmer weather. Pressure cooking adds a compactness to grains that is appealing when it 
is cold outside. New programmable pressure cookers are excellent for cooking rice and other 
grains. I am providing basic guidelines here, but be sure to follow the instructions for your 
particular model. Newer brands include a brown rice setting, and the porridge setting works 
well even for congee. (You can ﬁnd a widening selection of programmable pressure cookers 
sold under various brand names such as Instant Pot.)  
To cook white rice in a pressure cooker or instant pot, use slightly less water (because less steam 
escapes), 1.5 cups water for 1 cup white rice. Close the lid, turn on the heat. Bring the pot up 
to pressure, reduce heat to minimum, cook for 12 minutes, then turn oﬀ heat. Te rice will 
continue to cook as the pressure slowly decreases. Allow to rest a few more minutes for perfect 
steamed white rice. Use 1.75 cups water to 1 cup rice for wetter rice. For a programmable 
cooker, simply add the rice and water, close the lid and select the correct button. 
For brown rice, use the same method, but cook under pressure for 20 minutes, then turn oﬀ 
and allow to coast as before. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Basic rice cooking becomes easy with practice. Many are needlessly intimidated. Nicely cooked 
rice is not a bland accompaniment to a meal, it can be a feature. If the grain isn’t skillfully 
cooked and considered as central, the meal will overly focus on meat, stressing our liver and

<!-- p.27 -->
Steamed White and Brown Rice          21
gallbladder while depriving the organs that rice supports—stomach and pancreas-spleen. Tis 
is clearly detectable in the pulses of those who either don’t pay much attention to rice (and other 
non-glutinous grains) or actively avoid it.  
Rice strongly beneﬁts digestion. It can be so beneﬁcial that long grain rice is used in herb-
al medicine as an herb to harmonize the stomach with pancreas-spleen, in other words, to 
balance the necessary descending and ascending directional forces of the complete digestion 
process. White rice nourishes stomach yin (the protective lining of the stomach and duode-
num). Rice also supports hydration, ﬁrst moistening the stomach and from there supporting 
necessary moistness of the lungs, skin, eyes, ears, nose, and mouth. 
Tere are thousands of types of rice. Each has slightly diﬀerent beneﬁts and in turn requires 
slight cooking adjustments. Here are the most basic distinctions:
 »  Brown rice is more nourishing but distinctly harder to digest, unsuitable for the 
young, elderly, or those with weakened digestions. 
 »  Favor brown rice to clear constipation.
 »  Favor white rice to stabilize loose stool or diarrhea. (All white rice begins as brown 
rice, it simply has the bran and endosperm polished oﬀ.)
 »  Long grain rice is the safest for most people, balancing building and clearing very 
well.
 »  Short-grain rice is more warming and is most suitable for cold seasons and is some-
what more building than clearing. Medium-grain white rice balances these qualities 
well and is therefore my frequent choice, using other types for special intentions. 
See also the recipe for Rice Congee (page 180).

<!-- p.28 -->
22         Grains
Steamed Millet
Millet is the most neutral of grains, perfectly balanced in terms of warming 
or cooling, acid or alkaline. Millet strengthens digestion and supports ﬂuids 
and healthy hormones. Many people confess problems when they’ve tried to 
cook millet. Once you get to know it, however, preparing millet becomes easy. 
It’s a personal favorite. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 10 minutes
Total time: 30 minutes
Ingredients
organic millet | 1 cup
water | 1.5 - 2 cups 
Directions
Always wash millet. Use the water ﬂooding method: put the measure of millet in a pot, cover 
with perhaps 3 times as much water, slosh around to ﬂoat any dust from dry storage, then tip 
the water over the side, letting gravity hold the millet to the bottom. Try not to lose any grains. 
Repeat 3-4 times until the water runs clear. Ten, strain the millet to rid excess water.
Cook directly with 1.5 measures water, or dry roast the grains ﬁrst. To cook directly, steam like 
white rice: add the millet and the water, bring to a boil, immediately reduce to simmer and 
cover with a well-ﬁtted lid, cook 15-20 minutes, turn oﬀ, let coast, serve after another 5-10 
minutes. Do not stir millet during steaming or resting. 
To develop ﬂavor and energetics further, dry roast the millet before steaming. After washing 
and straining, roast millet in the cooking pot over medium-high heat without water or oil. 
Stir steadily to evaporate the rinsing water, then continue dry roasting until the millet turns 
slightly tan and oﬀers a pleasant aroma. Ten, add a fat such as butter, olive oil, or seed oil. 
Coat each grain with the oil. Cook then as above—1.5 to 2 measures of water to 1 measure 
of millet, reduce to simmer, cover, don’t stir. Steam 15 minutes, then allow 5 minutes or so

<!-- p.29 -->
Steamed Millet         23
resting time. Serve as base of a meal, with vegetables and a protein. Electric rice cookers or 
pressure pots also work beautifully with millet. 
Millet also makes an excellent porridge (see recipe for Millet Porridge, page 183). Leftover 
millet can be added to any pan-cooked vegetable or meat dish (similar to stir-fried rice dishes) 
or made into croquettes (add some egg, scallion, spices, and form into croquette patties for 
pan cooking). 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Millet strongly supports functions of stomach and pancreas-spleen. Dry roasting develops 
ﬂavor while adding yang qi to assist pancreas-spleen ascension. Millet is considered the most 
neutral of grains, perfectly balanced in terms of warming or cooling, acid or alkaline. Millet, 
therefore, is an important grain for those who ﬁnd problems from meals that combine meat, 
carbs and vegetables. It may be necessary to separate foods into clear meals as described in 
Book 1, but when you are feeling better and ready to try combining again, millet can be the 
best grain to begin with for meals including grain, greens, with ﬁsh or meat. 
Millet is both gentle and a whole grain (with more ﬁber, oils, and minerals than white rice, 
white ﬂour, or pearled barley). Millet hydrates and also clears turbid ﬂuids. It’s anti-inﬂam-
matory and is wonderful for soothing damage done by glutinous grains. Millet is important 
for replenishing hormone deﬁciency; for example, it’s helpful for hot ﬂashes associated with 
menopause. And because millet is high in silica (a major component of bone structure), millet 
is the go-to grain for helping people maintain strong bones or heal a diﬃcult fracture.

<!-- p.30 -->
24         Grains
Corn Grits and Polenta
Polenta and grits are made from tough ﬁeld corns not nearly as sweet as the 
familiar fresh (sweet) corn. Tese types of corn hydrate while encouraging 
clearing of old ﬂuids through urination. Tis is an excellent comfort food to 
help feel restored when tired or stressed.
Serves: four to eight
(serving size varies, make a bunch and keep for next day)
Prep time: 15 minutes
Total time: 45 minutes
Ingredients
dry polenta | 1 cup
butter | 2-3 Tbsp (to taste)
water | 4-5 cups
salt | chef’s pinch (more to taste)
Directions
Traditional polenta technique is to feed dry polenta in briskly boiling salted water while stir-
ring the water to avoid clumping. I prefer to dry roast the polenta grains in a large pot, stirring 
until the aroma ripens slightly, indicating a slight toast, 2-3 minutes. 
Add enough butter or olive oil to coat each grain particle, stirring with a wooden spoon or 
large whisk.  
Add half the water, taking care to avoid spatter. Stir constantly with a spoon or whisk to avoid 
clumping. Add water as needed. Stir steadily to avoid sticking, but after the grain has opened 
to the water, stirring can be strategic rather than constant (while you prepare the rest of the 
meal). 
Polenta is ﬁnished when it isn’t hungry for more water and its consistency is as you like it. 
If not cooked long enough, a slight raw taste will persist in the grain. Polenta is made from 
ground ﬁeld corn, not sweet corn; longer cooking will beneﬁt taste and digestion, but ulti-

<!-- p.31 -->
Corn Grits and Polenta         25
mately cooking time depends upon the size of the grind. Expect at the very least 20 minutes 
of cooking (and stirring), but probably closer to 35-40. 
Serve polenta with cooked greens, seafood, meat or beans. Finish with a bit more butter or 
olive oil, salt if needed, and freshly cracked pepper (or substitute grain of paradise, a peppery 
relative of cardamom seed).
Leftover polenta gelatinizes solidly; slice it the next day for pan frying with olive oil. 
Grits are made by cracking dried ﬁeld corn varieties (rather than grinding as for polenta). By 
cracking them in mills, far more surface area is exposed to the cooking water, vastly speeding 
cooking times compared to cooking the kernels whole. Even so, traditional grits require an 
hour or more of consistent stirring. Medium or quick grits are simply more ﬁnely milled; 
instant grits are mostly precooked and not very good eating. Te term hominy means the ﬁeld 
corn has been soaked in culinary lime to improve taste, digestibility, and vastly improve nu-
tritional value. Freshly milled grits are increasingly available; they are superb but sometimes 
require rinsing oﬀ some corn chaﬀ before cooking (mix raw grits with 3 times as much water, 
carefully pour ﬂoating chaﬀ oﬀ the top without losing the heavier grits). 
 »  Grits can be soaked in cool water to reduce cooking time. Soak overnight for morn-
ing grits. 
 » Bring grits and water to a boil then immediately reduce to a moderate simmer.
 »  Stir constantly until the ﬁrst starch emerges, then regularly but not constantly, as 
you prepare the other parts of the meal. Plan on devoting the ﬁrst 5 minutes to con-
stant stirring. After that, stir frequently to prevent sticking and to develop texture. 
 »  After 20 minutes (double that if not soaked), the grits should be creamy with some 
slightly ﬁrm bits. Grits should have a porridge consistency, wet but holding on a 
spoon. Stir in more water if too thick. 
 »  Just prior to serving, stir in a healthy few pinches of sea salt and about 2 Tbsp butter 
(toasted pumpkin oil or similar if vegan).
Serve with eggs for breakfast. Greens (kale, broccoli, spinach) or seafood (scallops, ﬂounder, 
etc.) are excellent with grits for lunch or dinner. Traditionally, freshly cracked black pepper

<!-- p.32 -->
26         Grains
accompanies grits, providing lift to their earthiness. On the other hand, cheese grits made with 
milk and melted cheese is a popular comfort food, but this combination easily causes dampness 
and weight-gain, negating the health beneﬁts and naturally earthy taste good grits can provide. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Polenta is made from ﬂint corn, a tough ﬁeld corn not nearly as sweet as the familiar fresh 
(sweet) corn. Although corn is generally listed as having a high glycemic index, ﬂint corn 
(polenta) helps regulate blood sugar levels, especially when combined with protein in a meal. 
Corn hydrates and encourages clearing of old ﬂuids through diuresis. It is excellent when 
tired or very stressed. Corn is also excellent for rebuilding strength during convalescence, for 
example, after giving birth, recovering from a ﬂu, or perhaps after having had surgery. Wet 
corn dishes like polenta are excellent for regaining lost weight, but for healthy people it will 
not cause weight gain in normal portions.

<!-- p.33 -->
Buckwheat (and Kasha Varnishkes)         27
Basic Buckwheat (and Kasha Varnishkes)
Buckwheat is an important gluten-free grain. It has a descending inﬂuence 
that’s good for acid reﬂux, indigestion, or constipation, and it is an import-
ant food for helping break up stagnation. Buckwheat has a delicious, warm, 
nutty ﬂavor and deserves to be used much more often. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 5 minutes
Total time: 25 minutes
Ingredients
buckwheat kernels (whole) | 1 cup
olive oil (or duck fat, chicken fat, etc.) | 1 Tbsp
water | 2 cup
Directions
Buckwheat does not need washing, being partially prepared before coming to market. It is 
sold pale or toasted (often called kasha when toasted). Except in hot weather (when I generally 
avoid buckwheat altogether), it is traditional to dry-roast buckwheat prior to cooking. Even 
when sold already toasted as kasha, dry-roasting “wakes” the grains before cooking. Simply 
toss around the grains in a dry pan on medium-high heat until a pleasing roasted aroma rises 
up and the grains turn slightly darker tan. Remove to avoid burning or begin the steaming in 
the same pan (be careful, that pan is very hot). Do not leave the grains unattended in the hot 
pan. 
Add enough oil or fat to coat each grain. Add the water (I sometimes use hot water from the 
tea kettle to move cooking quickly), bring to a boil, reduce to a low simmer, cover with a 
tight-ﬁtting lid, and steam for 12-15 minutes. Without lifting the lid, turn oﬀ heat and allow 
to coast for 5-10 minutes. 
Serve with sautéed mushrooms, greens, beans, meat or ﬁsh. Buckwheat is good as a solo grain, 
but it also combines beautifully with other grains. For example, Japanese soba noodles are

<!-- p.34 -->
28         Grains
buckwheat mixed with wheat—the result holds together well and with the addition of wheat 
is even more robustly warming for damp chilly days. 
Kasha Varnishkes is a traditional comfort food from Eastern Europe that combines cooked 
buckwheat kernels with wheat noodles. In my version, steam 1 cup buckwheat as described 
here, and separately cook 2 cups bow tie pasta (farfalle). In a pan, sauté a full bunch of scal-
lions (coarsely chopped) with 1 cup chopped mushrooms (type of your choice) and 1/2 cup 
pine nuts with 1 Tbsp olive oil or duck fat. When mushrooms have wilted, add about half the 
noodles and about half the cooked buckwheat. Season generously with salt and pepper, add 
more noodles and/or buckwheat to your preference. 
Wheat is binding, buckwheat helps clearing; adjust the balance of the two accordingly. When 
combining grains, always favor one more than the other to give a clear intention to the dish. 
To emphasize the binding quality of wheat, use more noodles than kasha. To emphasize the 
clearing quality of buckwheat, use more kasha than noodles. Both grains are warming, making 
this dish an eﬀective staple for the farmers and villagers of Eastern Europe and a wonderful 
comfort food anyplace that has winter. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Buckwheat provides non-glutinous grain nourishment. It’s an important warming grain, es-
pecially when dry roasted prior to cooking. It can be a staple for those with chronically cold 
hands and feet (buckwheat gently invigorates circulation while strengthening the integrity of 
blood vessels via the nutrient rutin). Because it has a descending energetic inﬂuence, it’s a good 
grain to treat acid reﬂux, indigestion, constipation, or hemorrhoids. 
Buckwheat is especially useful in helping to break up stagnation and conglomerations. When 
planning a meal, think if letting-go or breaking-up is appropriate—if so, buckwheat is a very 
good choice (not mixed with wheat). To amplify the energetics of buckwheat, be sure to roast 
the grain ﬁrst.

<!-- p.35 -->
Rice Combined with Buckwheat         29
Rice Combined with Buckwheat
Blending grains allows for creative tuning of the unique characteristics that 
each grain oﬀers for cuisine and health purposes.
Serves: six to eight
Prep time: 5 minutes
Total time: 25 minutes
Ingredients
white rice (medium or long grain) | 1 cup
buckwheat (whole kernel) | 2/3 cup
olive oil (or butter) | 1 Tbsp
salt | pinch
hot water | 2.5 cups
Directions
Put the rice and buckwheat in a dry cooking pot. Turn heat to high, mix grains with a wooden 
spoon, continue to stir while doing a short toasting to “wake” the grains (1-2 minutes). Add 
the oil, stir to coat, add a pinch of salt (optional), carefully add hot water from the tea kettle 
(using cold water is ﬁne, too). Return (or bring) to a boil, reduce to simmer at lowest setting, 
cover and cook 15 minutes. Do not stir. Turn heat oﬀ, leave covered to rest for 5-10 minutes. 
Serve with steamed greens and a protein dish, or use as basis for stir-fried grain. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
A blended dish like this combines the unique characteristics of each grain in the mix. When 
blending, it’s tempting to combine grains evenly, 50/50, but it’s better to always favor one a 
bit more. Sometimes one can be in very small amount to play a clearly secondary role. Tis is 
common practice with wild rice, often added in small amount to white rice, barley or quinoa. 
All grains are available for creative combining, but it is important to pair grains with consider-

<!-- p.36 -->
30         Grains
ation for their characteristics. Two glutinous grains together are not very useful, for example. 
Rather, pair a non-glutinous grain with a glutinous one, such as buckwheat with wheat, or 
two non-glutinous grains (as the example here). Grains can be combined as whole kernels (the 
example here of rice and buckwheat), in porridge (an example would be congee with some 
corn niblets added in the last 10 minutes of cooking), or by mixing ﬂours (the most common 
way to combine grains). 
From the cook’s point of view, it’s important to pair grains that cook in similar time, for ex-
ample, white rice, buckwheat and millet all cook in 15-20 minutes, brown rice and pearled 
barley both need 40-45 minutes. Wheat berries, wild rice, quinoa, amaranth and teﬀ may be 
best combined after being cooked independently. Creative cooks will ﬁnd their own signature 
combinations and methods.

<!-- p.37 -->
Steamed Farro (and Farro Risotto)         31
Steamed Farro (and Farro Risotto)
Farro can be a very good way to gain the many beneﬁts of wheat without 
the problems associated with modern wheat hybrids. Te best wheats are the 
antique varieties, and farro is the one of the easiest to ﬁnd.
Serves:  four
Prep time: 10 minutes
Total time: 45 minutes
Ingredients
farro | 1 cups
water | 2 cups
salt | pinch
Directions
Farro is available in three sizes. Each size can be pearled or whole (more on this below). Natu-
rally, cooking time is longer for the larger varieties and for whole rather than pearled (partially 
reﬁned) kernels. Te most popular is medium size semi-pearled (farro medio semi-perlato if 
imported from Italy, as is common). 
Rinse the farro and soak for at least an hour. (Soaking overnight is best.)  When ready to cook, 
pour away the soaking water and rinse the farro again. 
Bring farro to a boil with a pinch of salt (twice the water to farro). Immediately reduce to a 
simmer and cover. Steam for 30 minutes, longer if not pearled. One of farro’s great charms is 
that it remains chewy when cooked, do not wait for it to be fully soft. 
Allow grain to rest, covered, for 5-10 minutes before serving with vegetables and some protein, 
or adding to a soup or casserole.

<!-- p.38 -->
32         Grains
Farro Risotto
Farro “risotto” is easier to cook than rice risotto, as farro doesn’t release much starch and there-
fore doesn’t require much stirring. Begin with the white part of one leek, diced, in olive oil 
in a large pot. Add mushrooms (dried or fresh), cook and stir until textures soften and aroma 
develops. Add herbs such as rosemary, oregano, thyme, or seed spices such as caraway, cumin, 
or mustard seed. Add 1.5 cups soaked, drained, uncooked farro, stir. After a few minutes, add 
1/2 cup dry white wine, stir. When the wine is absorbed, continue adding liquid, but this 
time, use vegetable stock (or use the soaking water from the dried mushrooms, careful not to 
include any grit at the bottom). Farro will take longer than arborio rice, but with enough liq-
uid it can be left on its own, covered. Cook for 40-50 minutes, until tender yet chewy. Adjust 
liquid to be moist but not watery. Parmesan cheese is a common addition but is not necessary. 
Farro with mushrooms is wonderful ﬁnished with a scrape of fresh nutmeg and chopped fresh 
parsley.
A Note on the Food Energetics
Farro (and dishes made from the antique wheats spelt, emmer, einkorn and kamut) can be an 
excellent way to gain the beneﬁts of wheat without the problems associated with modern wheat 
hybrids. Wheat keeps us warm in the winter, strengthens muscles, sinews and bones, and calms 
the heart and spirit (and therefore can support good sleep). 
Farro is a collective term for a variety of antique wheat types. Einkorn, emmer and spelt are the 
most common wheats sold as farro. Einkorn is known as farro piccolo, emmer as farro medio, 
and spelt as farro grande. Occasionally, emmer is sold as true farro, but this is a misnomer. Fur-
thermore, farro is sold whole, semi-pearled (semi-perlato), and pearled (perlato). Te most pop-
ular is farro medio semi-perlato, which is semi-reﬁned emmer wheat berries (some of the bran 
left intact). Although emmer is the most common wheat sold with the label “farro”, whole 
einkorn and spelt berries are also easily available. Farro generally refers to the whole or pearled 
kernels (or wheat berries). Once ground into ﬂour, antique wheats are sold by their speciﬁc 
names: emmer, einkorn, or spelt. Although not included as a farro, Khorasan wheat (marketed 
as kamut) is also an excellent antique wheat that is widely available as whole kernel or ﬂour.

<!-- p.39 -->
Steamed Farro (and Farro Risotto)        33
Avoiding Wheat? Why You May Be Able to Enjoy Farro
Many people moderately sensitive to gluten ﬁnd they can eat farro (and other early wheat 
dishes) with good results. Tis can be because:
 »  Einkorn, emmer, spelt (and kamut) are historically early wheats that contain far less 
gluten than modern wheat.
 »  Soaking initiates the germination process, resolving the protective anti-nutrients 
within the grain and releasing enzymes that aid digestion by beginning the trans-
formation of complex carbohydrates.  
 »  By steaming or boiling whole grain kernels (or wheat berries), gluten is not devel-
oped, as occurs when kneading bread. Gluten is not a single chemical, it is created 
by several proteins coming together, a process deliberately cultivated by the knead-
ing process.
 »  Alternative wheats are often grown without modern fertilizers and pesticides, resi-
dues of which can cause many individuals serious problems.

<!-- p.40 -->
34         Grains
Rice of Original Qi
Tis is a rice salad combining black rice with vegetables and red beans to 
support Constitutional Qi in a delicious and ﬂexible manner.
Serves: eight 
(keeps well for several days when refrigerated)
Prep time: 30 minutes
Total time: 60 minutes 
(plus resting time as ﬂavors mix and develop)
Ingredients
black (forbidden) rice | 2 cups
water | 3.5 cups
adzuki beans | 1 cup, cooked (canned or home-cooked)
peas | 1 cup, cooked (frozen okay) 
broccoli ﬂorets | 1 cup, steamed
dried black mushrooms |  1 cup, soaked, drained, sliced (substitute porcini or other 
mushrooms, fresh or dried, if desired)
cashews |  1 cup (substitute sliced walnuts, chestnuts or sunflower seeds, if desired)
arame seaweed | 1/2-1 cup, soaked and drained
scallion | 1/2 cup, finely sliced (raw)
For the Dressing
grapeseed oil | 1 cup
tamari | 1/3 cup
black vinegar |  2 Tbsp (Shanxi black vinegar is a barley-sorghum vinegar from central 
China. Substitute rice vinegar and balsamic, 1 Tbsp each, if Shanxi vin-
egar is hard to find)
toasted sesame oil | 2 Tbsp
sea salt | to taste
parsley or cilantro | 1 bunch, washed, chopped

<!-- p.41 -->
Rice of Original Qi         35
Directions
Cook the black rice using steam absorption method: bring water and rice to a boil, reduce to a 
simmer, cover, cook for 40 minutes (20 in an electric pressure cooker), do not stir. (See pages 
20-22 for rice-cooking tips.)  Allow rice to rest and cool a bit. 
In a large bowl combine the cooked adzuki beans, peas and broccoli ﬂorets with mushrooms, 
cashews, arame and scallions. 
In a smaller bowl, mix the dressing (the oil, tamari, vinegar and toasted sesame oil). Pour the 
dressing over the vegetables, stir to mix. Gently mix in the rice. Taste for balance, adjust as 
needed. 
Stir in the chopped parsley or cilantro, reserving a bit for on-top presentation. 
Serve at room temperature.
A Note on the Food Energetics
Original qi is one translation of the classical Chinese medicine term yuan qi. It’s a collective 
term packed with meanings and connotations, all pointing to: 
 »  Our inherited constitutional strength bequeathed to us in ﬁnite quantity by our 
parents, with the inﬂuence of our gestation, birth, and youngest years
 » Our ancestral genetics
 »  Te status of our deepest health. In other words, how we are managing the strengths 
and weaknesses of our inherited constitutional health. Some squander strong con-
stitutions, others are constitutionally fragile but live very long and meaningful lives. 
Tere is an art to life. 
Te Rice of Original Qi plays with these ideas. It’s a favorite dish of ours to share with others, 
we often bring it to new mothers for postpartum recovery, or even to potluck parties. Make 
changes in the recipe template according to what you know is needed for diﬀerent people. 
Black rice is a wonderful kidney support food. In earlier times the entire crop of black rice 
was reserved for the Imperial Chinese court that resided in the Forbidden City, prized for its

<!-- p.42 -->
36         Grains
value for health and longevity (it is often marketed as “forbidden rice”). It is a whole grain and 
cooks similarly to brown rice.
Adzuki beans also support kidneys, as well as build blood, calm emotions, and aid liver func-
tion. Arame seaweed is cleansing and nourishing; being black and from the sea it is also a 
kidney food. Cashews are one of the nuts used for kidney health, but cashews easily add to 
dampness if digestion is weak (use walnuts or chestnuts in place if you like, or the much more 
easily digested pumpkin seeds…or, if digestion is weak and dampness present, leave nuts and 
seeds out altogether). Cashews are wonderful if you can have them—they even look like little 
kidneys. 
Black mushrooms are also an important kidney-supporting food. Mushrooms help clear 
dampness while supporting original qi and good immune function. Te raw scallions give 
a gentle lift to energize the digestion process. Vinegar stimulates digestion as well, but more 
importantly helps the liver hold the healthy blood that is being made from such good food. 
Toasted sesame seed oil also speaks to the kidneys, said to be the home of original qi. Mustard 
seed or prepared mustard in the dressing is a nice addition—mustard warms the belly for 
good digestion. Make the dish your own, but be careful that your improvisations follow the 
theme—it’s your original qi we’re feeding here, and it’s important to be respectful of what you 
are made of.  
Relaxing the Liver
When the message from our deep health organs is that we have enough of what we need (not 
too much, that’s a diﬀerent issue), then the liver can relax. Vinegar astringes, slightly tightens 
the liver (sour is the ﬂavor that connects most directly to the liver organ, and of course sour 
induces a kind of puckering). Common symptoms of liver excess—manifesting as headaches, 
hypertension, or irritability—are often due to the liver in fact being undersupported. Tis is an 
important point: the liver both stores blood and governs the spreading quality of qi. When 
blood is relatively scarce, the liver tends to tighten, leading to signs and symptoms of excess. 
Te liver can relax when it is given what it is trying so hard to secure. Blood-building foods

<!-- p.43 -->
Rice of Original Qi         37
and good quality vinegar can help here. 
Black Shanxi vinegar is available in Chinatown markets or online, but be sure to read the in-
gredients—the real stuﬀ has no added sugar or coloring and is made from sorghum or millet. 
It’s beautiful—deep, only mildly acidic, and full of ﬂavor. 
Everyone in Shanxi Province seems very particular about their use of vinegar. When I traveled 
there it was common for cooks to mix three types of vinegar with a bit of oil, crushed Szechuan 
peppercorns and fresh cilantro leaves to dress a simple bowl of handmade noodles, even at in-
expensive outdoor food stalls. Flavors were incredible and the commitment to traditional food 
integrity was inspiring, perhaps especially because such beautiful dishes cost only pocket coins.

<!-- p.44 -->
38         Grains
Two Condiments Perfect for Grains, Greens, Bean Dips and more: Za’atar 
and Gomasio
Two simple and delicious sesame seed mixes from two great culinary traditions.
Za’atar is a spiced sesame seed condiment from Middle Eastern cuisine that 
transforms a snack or simple meal into very special eating. Sprinkle it by the 
ﬁnger-pinch or use the olive oil dipping method. 
Gomasio is a balanced condiment of roasted sesame seeds with salt, from 
Japanese cuisine. Add gomasio to grains, greens or ﬁsh for its deep ﬂavor and 
energetic contribution. 
Za’atar
sesame seeds | 2 Tbsp
sumac | 4 Tbsp (if you prefer, 1/4 cup)
thyme | 2 Tbsp 
oregano | 1 Tbsp
marjoram | 2 Tbsp 
salt | 1/2-1 tsp
Directions for Za’atar
Heat the sesame seeds in a dry pan, shaking to manage toasting without burning.   Add the 
other ingredients, continue to shake while heating, only 3-5 minutes in total. 
After you remove a portion for today’s use, allow the extra to cool before storing in an airtight 
container. Za’atar will keep for a month or so in the refrigerator. 
Set out a shallow dish with the za’atar, and another with olive oil. If you’ve also made a bean 
dish—such as the wonderful fava mash known as ful medames—place that in a bowl near the 
two plates. To complete the simple and superb meal, add a plate of warmed pita, fresh bread

<!-- p.45 -->
Za’atar         39
or gluten-free crackers. Take a piece of the bread in your ﬁngers, dip it into the olive oil, touch 
that into the za’atar, eat. If you have ful or something like it, spread that onto the bread with a 
knife, on top of the oil and za’atar. If you prefer, spread some ful on the pita, then sprinkle lib-
erally with za’atar and a drizzle of olive oil. It tastes beautiful and is fortifying for breakfast or 
a snack (in our family, za’atar with ful or mashed chickpeas is breakfast perhaps once each week). 
Za’atar is also delicious when sprinkled on Greek-style yogurt, on eggs, as a rub for grilled 
meats, or on steamed vegetables. It is very versatile. 
Because za’atar is a popular food in the Middle East, recipes vary with family traditions. A 
common variation is to toast the sesame seeds separately. Ten, oﬀer a plate of spice mix and a 
plate of sesame seeds; each person touches pita or other bread into olive oil, then into the za’at-
ar spice mix, then into the seeds. In our home, I usually mix them together, but the Palestinian 
musician friend who introduced me to za’atar years ago urged me to keep them separate, but 
he also adamantly said I should never, ever make za’atar without the wild sumac gathered by 
his elderly aunt near her house in the hills south of Nazareth. In our home we add the sesame 
seeds directly into the za’atar, and we have to “make do” with some ﬁne sumac from a local 
market, far from my friend’s aunt. 
Note: Culinary sumac is a harmless distant cousin to poison sumac. Sumac 
spice is in the large botanical genus Rhus, which includes pistachio, mango, 
pink peppercorns and dozens of other relatives that lack the chemical respon-
sible for the allergic reaction associated with poison sumac, urushiol.  
Premade za’atar is available, but it is so easy to make, so superior in taste (and so much cheap-
er) that it makes little sense to buy it packaged. Buy sumac at any good market in the spice 
department; purchase small amounts—fresh sumac should have a moistness to it and a vivid, 
lemony scent. 
If you’re in a hurry or low on herbs, simplify your za’atar. As long as you have sumac, sesame 
seeds, salt and a green herb, it will be delicious and authentic enough.

<!-- p.46 -->
40        Grains
A Note on the Food Energetics 
Za’atar is a mixture of herbs, seeds, and berries (sumac is a berry that is ground before coming 
to market). It is one answer to the question, “What do I put on my food to make it taste good 
if I’m not used to healthy food?”  It’s an easy and versatile mix that can be used creatively. For 
example: brush olive oil onto a ﬂat bread, sprinkle za’atar on top, and bake in the oven until 
some of the bread is crispy on the outside but still soft within. Or, sprinkle over simply cooked 
ﬁsh, lamb, or adzuki beans. 
Sumac is not an herb that appears in classical Chinese medical compendiums, but knowledge 
is not a closed set; it is intended to be applied. From a Western perspective, sumac is very high 
in antioxidants, is a mild diuretic, and has been seen in a laboratory context to lower blood 
sugar levels. From a Chinese medicine perspective, it is a tart berry that helps the liver manage 
blood, invigorate blood to resolve stagnation, and clear toxins. 
Sesame seeds are the principal seed for supporting the kidneys, an excellent pairing for a mildly 
diuretic berry spice. Tyme, oregano, and marjoram aid digestion (making za’atar an excellent 
choice to help digest fava or other beans). Salt works well with the tart taste of the sumac and 
the kidney association of the sesame seeds. 
 
Gomasio
sesame seeds (unhulled is best) | 1 cup
sea salt | 1/2-1 tsp
Directions for Gomasio
Heat the sesame seeds in a dry pan, shaking to manage toasting without burning.   Add the 
salt. Stir to roast over medium heat, 5-10 minutes. A beautiful toasted sesame seed aroma will 
let you know you can move them to a bowl to cool (leaving them in the pan will allow seeds 
to burn from residual heat). 
Grind the mixture coarsely in a mortar and pestle (or the wonderful Japanese version with

<!-- p.47 -->
Gomasio         41
grooved sides known as a suribachi). 
Use gomasio while warm if you like—sprinkle over broccoli, kale, chard, spinach, or other 
greens. Also use on root vegetables such as roast carrots. A bit of olive oil (or other oil) helps 
the gomasio cling to the vegetables. 
Gomasio will stay fresh on the table for a few days or in the refrigerator for a few weeks. Allow 
to cool before storing in an airtight glass container. 
As with za’atar, homemade gomasio is so easy and so superior that it makes little sense to pur-
chase premade versions, although they are available in most markets near the soy sauce and 
seaweeds.  
A Note on the Food Energetics 
Gomasio is a perfectly simple condiment. It is said that by roasting and then coarsely crushing 
the sesame and salt together, the salt protects the seed oils from spoiling and the sesame keeps 
the salt from making us feel thirsty. Tis may be so, but primarily it is a superbly delicious 
condiment that provides a mild saltiness and an irresistible sesame pleasure. 
Both salt and sesame seeds resonate with the kidneys and the lower area of the torso. Gomasio 
brings that note even into a simple meal. Add gomasio to your cooking playbook (along with 
za’atar). Gomasio raises a simple meal of brown rice, kale and a small piece of ﬁsh (or tofu) to 
perfection.

<!-- p.48 -->
four 
Greens
Eating green vegetables is a part of daily life. It shouldn’t be unusual or dif-
ﬁcult. In our family, every meal but breakfast is full of vegetables, usually 
prepared in simple Mediterranean style: steamed then tossed with good ol-
ive oil and a pinch of good salt. Our kids never avoided greens because they 
never wanted to miss out on what we as adults were enjoying so much. Here 
are recipes that represent important cooking methods: steaming, sautéing, 
European and Asian styles, a cooked salad, and an appetizer salad.

<!-- p.49 -->
Steamed Vegetables with Olive Oil and Sea Salt         43
Steamed Vegetables with Olive Oil and Sea Salt
Tis is a daily method for cooking greens. Broccoli, string beans, kale, chard, 
cabbage, cauliﬂower, asparagus, bok choy, zucchini, even escarole or radic-
chio—greens taste great steamed until just tender and dressed with olive oil 
and salt.
Serves: four
Prep time: 5 minutes
Total time: 10-15 minutes
Ingredients
broccoli (as example) | 2 medium-size heads
olive oil | enough to dress generously
sea salt or ancient land salt | chef’s pinch
Directions
Wash the broccoli under cool water. With a sharp knife trim oﬀ the ﬂorets. Cut oﬀ and discard 
the bottom of the stems. Te length of the stems may be peeled if they are tough, the soft 
stem centers cut to match the ﬂoret size before adding to the cooking pot. Steam the broccoli 
until tender but not fully soft, about 5 minutes, depending on the size of your pieces. Test 
with a fork; it should enter the broccoli easily but still feel some ﬁrmness. Use a steamer if you 
like—what I do is steam them in a lidded pot with 1/4 inch water in the bottom. Begin over 
high heat, but once steaming, reduce to low (steam is very hot, there is no beneﬁt to a higher 
heat setting). Be careful not to evaporate all the water. Perfection is having nearly no water 
left as the greens are perfectly done, but if some does remain, drain it away (add to a soup or 
grain if you can). 
Turn oﬀ heat. Dress generously with nice olive oil; shake or toss with a wooden spoon to en-
sure oil is spread amply. Add a chef’s pinch or two of good quality salt. Serve while steam is 
still rising.

<!-- p.50 -->
44         Greens
A Note on the Food Energetics
Each green vegetable has a distinctly diﬀerent food energetic. Leafy greens are middle burner 
foods that resonate strongly with the liver and its functions. Further diﬀerentiations are as 
follows:
 »  Broccoli, cabbage and Brussels sprouts are compact green vegetables, conferring a 
holding or even tightening eﬀect on the liver. 
 »  Kale, chard and collards are spreading green leafy vegetables and have an opening, 
spreading eﬀect. (Bok choy is somewhat in between, look to see how tightly or 
openly it has grown.)
 »  Bitter vegetables such as dandelion greens, artichokes, spinach, radicchio or broc-
coli rabe have a descending and clearing quality. 
 »  Mustard greens and some others are considered spicy and have a spreading quality 
associated as much with the heart as the liver. Watercress includes bitter, sour, and 
spicy qualities. 
All these greens are easily cooked and dressed with olive oil for everyday eating. As a cook, I 
prefer to merely wilt the bitter greens such as dandelion and radicchio, then dress with oil and 
salt. Because mustard greens get quite bitter if steamed, I prefer to sauté them with a bit of oil 
from the start. 
For a change of pace, cabbage is wonderful simply with butter. Cut a basic green cabbage (or 
savoy, Napa, purple, etc.) into chunks; steam until tender but not fully soft (timing depends 
upon size of the chunks, but perhaps 8-12 minutes). Remove the steaming water, and melt 
some good quality butter on top of the cabbage, stirring gently. Add salt if needed. Cabbage 
loves caraway seeds—toss in 1/8 tsp whole seeds with the butter if desired. And remember to 
cook the vegetables last so they can be served still steaming. 
For another approach, drain the steaming water when a green vegetable is approaching ten-
der. Add a high-quality seed oil (I like grapeseed oil these days), and a splash of tamari to the 
cooking pot. Stir, and ﬁnish with a hint of toasted sesame oil (and some sesame seeds sprinkled 
on top).

<!-- p.51 -->
Steamed Vegetables with Olive Oil and Sea Salt         45
How Much Oil—and What Kind is Best?  
When I eat ultra-simple dishes like these steamed vegetables in Italy, there is a pool of beau-
tifully green and slightly spicy olive oil left on the plate when I am done. Be generous, not all 
the oil will be consumed. Tere should be a sense of plenty. 
In terms of food and cultural traditions, olive oil and butter are more Mediterranean, lighter 
oils and tamari more Asian. Although they can be mixed for fusion purposes, generally it is 
good to be clear which approach you are using and stick with it for that meal.
A Note on Salt
How much salt should I use?  
I am often asked this in dietary consults. My response:  “Not too much, but not none.” 
Salt food to bring it to life, not to make the food taste salty. If you’d like to reduce sodi-
um intake, cook for yourself more often, salting to taste as you do. Most excess sodium 
intake comes from processed foods and restaurant meals. Both fast-food and ﬁne food 
restaurants add more salt than you would ever do when cooking at home. Making your 
own food automatically lowers sodium intake even as you salt your food nicely. 
What about using more herbs instead of salt?
Popular advice to “doctor up” your cooking with lots of herbs and spices while reducing 
salt is a misguided strategy that fails to understand the potent inﬂuence that each dif-
ferent herb and spice exerts when eaten. Herbs and spices should be used each for their 
unique characteristics, not thrown together to mask other problems. For those accus-
tomed to very salty food, a period of recalibration may be necessary. As with sugar, a few 
weeks with nothing salty will allow taste to reset, after which we can experience skillfully 
salted food in a fresh way. 
If excess sodium remains a problem, reduce or eliminate packaged and processed foods, 
reduce or eliminate fast food and overly indulgent restaurant foods, while adding natu-
rally diuretic foods to your diet. Te goal of reduced sodium intake is generally to restore 
balance to ﬂuid metabolism. Barley, bean sprouts, snow peas, string beans, spinach, kale, 
and mushrooms all exert a gentle diuretic inﬂuence that can help with that balance.

<!-- p.52 -->
46         Greens
Vegetables with Ginger and Scallion
Tis method combines steaming with sautéing. Te result is more invigorat-
ing than steaming alone, while retaining the ease of digestion. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 10 minutes
Total time: 20 minutes (or less)
Ingredients
vegetables of your choice |  measure each raw portion in a bowl (use your eye to judge 
a portion)
water | 1/4 inch water (be careful not to let the vegetables burn)
scallions | 1-2 per person (cut into chunks)
fresh ginger | 1 thin slice per person and one more for harmony, slivered
Seed or nut oil (good quality only) | 1-2 Tbsp 
rice wine (optional) | 1/4 cup
tamari (optional) | splash (1 Tbsp)
salt (optional) | pinch
toasted sesame oil (optional) | 1/4 tsp
Directions
Wash and trim the vegetables. Tis can be a solo vegetable (kale, bok choy, broccoli, Chinese 
broccoli, Napa cabbage, mustard greens, string beans, asparagus, spinach, etc.), or a combination. 
Steam the vegetables but remove early, when bright green but still distinctly too ﬁrm (they will 
cook more to ﬁnish in a moment). Restaurants do this ﬁrst part of the cooking by dropping 
the vegetables into boiling water kept on the stove for this purpose. You can do that if you 
like, but I usually steam them in a pot with 1/4 inch water, being careful not to allow water 
to evaporate. 
Remove the vegetables, set aside. In a dry pot or large pan, turn on heat to high, add the cook-

<!-- p.53 -->
Vegetables with Ginger and Scallion         47
ing oil (grapeseed, organic corn oil, organic peanut oil, saﬄower oil, etc.). Add the slivered 
ginger and scallions. Cook for 1 minute, add a shake or two of ﬁve-spice powder if you like 
(it’s good with and without). 
Add the vegetables into the pan, stir to coat.  Add the rice wine and tamari. When the alcohol 
has evaporated (two minutes if heat is high enough), turn heat oﬀ, add a pinch of salt, splash 
just enough toasted sesame oil to add its lovely perfume, and serve with a grain and the rest 
of the meal. 
Here is a simpler variation that only requires one pot. Steam to soften the greens.  Ten, re-
move the veggies and pour away any steaming water that is remaining. Add some grapeseed 
(or other) oil to the empty vegetable pan, throw in the ginger and scallion, pinch of salt, return 
the vegetables, stir. Serve after one minute, during which time all the ﬂavors come together. 
Couldn’t be easier or more delicious. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
By combining these two cooking methods, you get the digestive support from steaming and 
the invigorating aspect of sautéing. (Sautéing stimulates the liver, but in this case, not too 
much.) 
Scallion is a mild onion, spicy but harmonious. Its eﬀect is warming, ascending, and support-
ive of immune defensive qi—helping distribute ﬂuids eﬀectively to the lungs, skin and sense 
organs. Fresh spring onions can be used (slightly stronger) but avoid bulb onions for this dish.
