Fresh ginger is a very important digestive herb.  Ginger warms digestion, helps resolve nausea, 
reduces any abdominal digestive pain and helps clear phlegm. Everyone should keep fresh 
ginger on hand for ready use.
Select vegetables based on their characteristics as outlined in the previous recipe. In making 
your choices, think about what properties might be of beneﬁt to you, your family or your 
guests, while always considering what looks good and fresh at the market.

<!-- p.54 -->
48         Greens
Cooked Salad
Raw vegetables show high nutritional values in a laboratory setting, but 
cooked vegetables are easier to digest. Te cook masters the middle ground. 
Tis “cooked salad” oﬀers a cool, summer dish with the beneﬁts of lightly 
cooked foods. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 15 minutes
Total time: 30 minutes
Ingredients
string beans | about 1/4 handful per person, so 2 handfuls
zucchini | 2-3 small or medium 
cauliﬂower | half a head
carrots | 1-2 
Dressings: See below for various dressing ingredients and instructions.
Directions
Wash and trim the vegetables. Top and tail the string beans. Trim the zucchini ends, then cut 
crosswise either in thick rings or into thirds then quarter the thirds lengthwise. Break or cut 
the cauliﬂower ﬂorets into bite size, and likewise trim and cut the carrots. Place all in a steamer 
(or a pot with ¼-inch water). Steam with a lid until tender but not fully soft (time depends 
on the cuts you prefer). 
Vegetables can be served warm or allowed to cool. 
Other vegetables can be used: broccoli, asparagus, snow peas, common peas, cabbage, kale, 
kohlrabi, etc. For a complete meal, add seeds, grilled ﬁsh or chicken, tofu, black beans—any 
choice of protein—and a choice of carbohydrate such as steamed potato or sweet potato, cel-
lophane noodles or rice noodles.

<!-- p.55 -->
Cooked Salad         49
Choose one of these dressings to toss over the greens: 
 » Fine olive oil and sea salt
 » Classic Dijon vinaigrette. Whisk together vigorously:
1/2 cup good olive oil
2-3 Tbsp red wine vinegar
1/2 tsp Dijon style mustard
chef’s pinch of salt
 » Homemade ponzu sauce for dipping or dressing:
1/2 cup tamari
1/4 cup water
2 Tbsp rice wine vinegar (a low-acid vinegar—if replacing with another vinegar, 
use a bit less)
slivered fresh ginger
juice of 1 lime or 1/2 meyer lemon (the wonderful Japanese yuzu citrus is in-
creasingly available, use that when you have it) 
 » Tahini dressing. Whisk to combine:
3 Tbsp tahini
1 tsp tamari
1 tsp mirin (a gently sweet rice wine for cooking; select a naturally brewed brand)
1 tsp rice vinegar or lemon juice
1 Tbsp toasted sesame seed oil
3 Tbsp warm water (add more gradually if necessary)
optional:  1 tsp honey 
 
          Shanxi vinegar

<!-- p.56 -->
50         Greens
A Note on the Food Energetics
Raw foods, while trendy, can be diﬃcult to digest. Because protecting digestion is paramount, 
cooked food is the best choice most of the time. But many people on a cooked food diet report 
that they miss a green salad. Te cooked salad solves that problem beautifully. 
Cook the vegetables underdone, they will coast as they cool. In summer, shock them in a bowl 
of ice water to stop their cooking precisely in place if you like (and preserve the bright colors 
of perfectly cooked greens). Tis is a classic restaurant technique that is also easy at home. Even 
better, move the food quickly from stove to table so that shocking is not required. 
Each vegetable has unique properties, but any vegetable adds directional help to a meal. Once 
you have some experience with food energetics you will know for yourself how asparagus in-
vigorates upward, cabbage consolidates inward, and so forth.

<!-- p.57 -->
Kale Oshitashi          51
Kale Oshitashi
Tis ultra-simple Japanese classic is very easy to do at home. Traditionally made 
with spinach with sesame seed dressing, oshitashi is a beautiful way to treat any 
leafy green: spinach, kale, chard, or any green leafy edibles you can ﬁnd. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 10 minutes
Total time: 15 minutes
Ingredients
kale (or other leafy green) | 1 large bunch 
tamari | 1 Tbsp
mirin | 1 tsp
toasted sesame oil | 1 Tbsp
sesame seeds | 2 tsp
Directions
Wash the kale, separating leaves from stalks. (You may save the stalks for stock, but do not 
include in this dish.) 
 Steam or blanch the greens (to blanch, bring a large pot of water to boil, add a pinch of salt, 
drop the greens in until just tender, 3-4 minutes for kale, only 1 minute for spinach or chard). 
Remove the greens, run cool water over them to stop cooking and preserve their bright green 
color (this is called shocking). 
Drain the greens. With clean hands, press out as much water as you can. 
Chop the greens on a board with knife strokes about 1/4 inch apart (a coarse chop). 
In a bowl, mix the dressing, add the greens, toss thoroughly. Serve in a small mound on each 
plate, sprinkle sesame seeds  on top. Or, roll into a traditional oshitashi log, then cut into por-
tions with a sharp knife. Garnish with sesame seeds. Serve as an appetizer or as vegetable side 
dish.

<!-- p.58 -->
52         Greens
A Note on the Food Energetics
In choosing which green to use for your oshitashi, consider the diﬀerent properties of spinach, 
kale, and chard:
 » Spinach is mildly diuretic, helps relax the liver and the middle torso, including 
relaxing diaphragmatic tightness. Tis in turn relaxes breathing and releases some 
tension often associated with PMS. Spinach contributes iron, but also is high in 
oxalic acid which functions as an anti-nutrient blocking absorption of calcium. (Ox-
alic acid, or oxalate, is neutralized by healthy gut bacteria—another example of the 
importance of healthy microbiota. Foods high in oxalate are therefore a concern for 
those who have had frequent antibiotics, or a history of kidney stones.)
 » Kale is a coarse green vegetable (spinach is classiﬁed as soft or smooth). It confers a 
more moving energy. Tis helps the stomach and digestion if there is any painful 
stagnation. More than just contributing nutrients, kale helps restore the necessary 
descending directionality for the stomach. Kale also helps build blood.  All kale 
supports blood, but the red and purple kale varieties are blood-building superstars. 
 » Chard also contains oxalates, so it is not a suﬃcient contributor to our calcium 
needs, but it is very soothing and cooling for the liver, making it useful to reduce 
high blood pressure and soothe dizziness or red eyes (all associated with heat in the 
liver). 
Oshitashi—made with spinach, chard, or kale—is nicely descending, soothing, and clearing, 
with special connection to stomach and kidney qi (and if spinach or chard, liver qi as well). Te 
salty note of tamari (wheat-free soy sauce) connects with the kidneys, as do sesame seeds and 
sesame oil. In this way, even a very simple dish has a distinct energetic signature.

<!-- p.59 -->
Oven Roasted Brussels Sprouts         53
Oven-Roasted Brussels Sprouts
Brussels sprouts are a food that many people have an aversion to, perhaps 
based on early experiences of having them overcooked in boiling water. Roast 
them in the oven with loving care and you will be surprised how wonderful 
they are. It’s one of our favorite foods for guests—they pick around them until 
they taste one out of politeness, then a convert is made. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 10-15 minutes
Total time: 35-45 minutes
Ingredients
Brussels sprouts | 1.5 pounds
olive oil | enough to coat generously, 3-5 Tbsp
sea salt | enough to reach loosely throughout
Directions
Preheat oven to 400˚F. 
Rinse the Brussels sprouts, peel away any leaves that aren’t fresh. Trim the bottoms with a sharp 
knife then either cut in half or cut a deep “+” in the stem (to allow heat into the core). 
In a bowl, toss the sprouts with olive oil and enough salt to lightly reach all the sprouts (a 
couple pinches). 
Arrange the oiled sprouts on a sheet of parchment paper in a baking sheet. Ensure that each of 
the sprouts can “see” the heat, in other words, that none are covered by another. 
Use a large spoon to turn the Brussels sprouts once while roasting. 
Roast for 25 minutes or until fork-tender and browned to your liking. 
Tey are ready when a fork enters them easily but some feeling of resistance remains. Timing 
will vary based on multiple factors: size of the sprouts, their density (heavily fertilized vege-

<!-- p.60 -->
54         Greens
tables grow more quickly and are therefore less dense), the way you have scored or cut them, 
when you turn them, and the particulars of your oven. If the sprouts are small or cut in half, 
the roasting time will be much less, perhaps 12-15 minutes.
Other vegetables are also wonderful when oven-roasted with olive oil and ﬁne salt: broccoli, 
cauliﬂower, beets, carrots, parsnips, squash and sweet potatoes (I omit salt with squash, cook-
ing pumpkins, and sweet potatoes). 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Brussels sprouts are in the cabbage family, along with broccoli, cauliﬂower, collards, and kale. 
Tey are compact, grow slowly in relatively cool conditions, and therefore are fairly warming 
(and are traditionally favored in cooler climates). Tey help clear damp heat in the gallbladder 
and liver, helping to clear unhelpful cholesterol and aid peristalsis. Tey help moisten the in-
testines and clear constipation. 
When roasted, Brussels sprouts are full of ﬂavor and appeal even to those who don’t think they 
love this often-maligned vegetable. Roasting consolidates the qi of a food and is the cooking 
method that supports consolidating the qi of the kidneys. I never seem to be able to make 
enough of these, especially in winter months. 
A quick story. A few years ago I was performing as a musician at a charity fundraiser in Los 
Angeles. Tere was a dinner between rehearsal and the event, catered by a very chic farm-to-
table establishment. Nearly all the food was raw. Raw food is a trend I understand, having 
some raw foodie friends. It was visually beautiful food and I found enough to eat, but when 
working through a raw Brussels sprout, I reached my limit. 
Across the table was Jason Alexander, a star of the Seinfeld show and much more. I caught his 
eye and whispered, “Farm to table, yes, but they forgot that it’s supposed to be farm-to-cook-
to-table.”  We melted into hysterics (comedians love to laugh).

<!-- p.61 -->
Zucchini with Goji Berries         55
Zucchini with Goji Berries
Tis is a variation of a classic dish from the Chinese healing foods tradition 
that nourishes blood and stomach yin. It’s gentle and delicious. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 5 minutes 
Total time: 10 minutes
Ingredients
zucchini | 2-3 medium, 4 if small
grapeseed oil (or other seed oil) | 1 Tbsp
fresh ginger | 2 slices, slivered
scallion | 1-2, trimmed, chopped 
goji berries (dried) | a handful (12-24 berries)
mirin or rice wine | 1/4 cup 
sea salt | chef’s pinch
organic cornstarch or kuzu powder  | 1 Tbsp
toasted sesame oil  | splash
Directions
Wash the zucchini, trim the tops and bottoms, cut into three sections, and quarter those sec-
tions lengthwise. 
In a pan or pot, add the oil over medium high heat. Add the ginger and scallions, the goji 
berries, the zucchini pieces, then the rice wine (use mirin, sake, or substitute dry sherry, ver-
mouth, dry white wine, or simply use water). Add a pinch of salt. 
Be careful not to overcook—perhaps 5 minutes is enough, so the zucchini is tender but not 
too soft (time will vary on the size of zukes and heat). 
Meanwhile, mix the cornstarch or kuzu powder in a small cup with just enough water to make

<!-- p.62 -->
56         Greens
a slurry (these thickening agents must never be added to a pan without being mixed with cool 
water ﬁrst). Drizzle gradually into the pan to thicken the pan sauce (all the slurry may not be 
needed). Allow the pan to come to a gentle bubble to activate thickening. If it becomes too 
thick, simply thin it with a bit of water. Te liquid should thicken enough to hang onto the 
zucchini as a sauce without being “gloppy”. 
Add a splash of toasted sesame oil to ﬁnish. 
Serve with rice or other grain as a vegetable side dish. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Zucchini nourishes stomach yin and helps with blurred vision—including ﬂoaters—through 
nourishing blood. Goji berries further nourish blood and—due to their gentle tartness—help 
the liver hold and store enough blood to feel complete and relaxed. Te wine also tunes the 
dish to the blood level (even with all the alcohol evaporated through heat). 
A pinch of salt and the toasted sesame oil both connect with the kidneys. Scallion safely 
stimulates digestion; ginger harmonizes digestion and assists the lungs in diﬀusion (sense of 
openness in breathing and circulation). Kuzu strongly soothes any digestive upset, calming 
excessive stomach acid and soothing the small intestine, an organ that is often overworked by 
poor food combinations and overeating. 
Substitute other gourds or squashes for the zucchini as you like. Serve with a grain and ﬁsh for 
a simple, complete meal, but if acid reﬂux is present or to make a vegetarian clear meal, serve 
with tofu, lentils, or black beans as the protein. (See Welcoming Food Book One, chapter 9, for 
discussion of Clear Meals.)

<!-- p.63 -->
Baked Okra         57
Baked Okra
Okra is a food many people dislike, but it’s a wonderful food to soothe diges-
tion. Give it a chance and it can become a delicacy.   
Serves: four to eight
Prep time: 5 minutes
Total time: 15-20 minutes
Ingredients
fresh okra | 1 pound
olive oil | enough to coat
sea salt | two chef’s pinches (nice and full)
cracked pepper (or grain of paradise) | to taste
Directions
Rinse the okra, leave whole or cut into chunks (I prefer whole). 
Arrange okra on a baking tray lined with parchment sheet.
Sprinkle with olive oil (toss lightly to coat), the sea salt and the grain of paradise (a type of 
black cardamom seed with a long, peppery taste) or freshly cracked black pepper.
Bake in preheated oven at 425˚F for 10-15 minutes.
A Note on the Food Energetics
Many people say they dislike the “sliminess” of okra, but that is a quality that the small intes-
tine absolutely thrives on. Fried okra can be fun, but the fried breading stresses the liver and 
isn’t very health supportive. Baked okra is delicious and far better to digest (okra in stews is 
even better for the intestines). Make this dish for guests—it’s a sly pleasure to convert skeptics 
into okra lovers. Serve as a side dish or appetizer.

<!-- p.64 -->
five 
Legumes
Legumes (beans, in common parlance) are important for nourishment and 
provide unique food energetics.  Here are three representative recipes that 
solve the digestive trouble some have with legumes: simple lentils, spiced 
legumes, and a simple curry.

<!-- p.65 -->
Simple Lentils         59
Simple Lentils 
Lentils are the easiest bean to digest. Tey help clear stagnation, improve 
elimination, settle acid reﬂux, and are a gentle diuretic (which helps clear 
ﬂuid retention). Tey’re an ancient staple food, from the Mediterranean to 
India, working beautifully in many cuisines. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 5 minutes
Total time: 40 minutes
Ingredients
lentils | 1 cup
water | 2-3 cups
olive oil | 2 Tbsp
Directions
Sort all beans carefully for stones, including lentils. Te best way to do this is by pouring a 
quarter cup or so at a time onto a white plate. Sort from one side to the other with dry ﬁngers, 
picking out any stones or stray matter. Rinse the sorted lentils in plenty of running water. 
Lentils do not need soaking before cooking. 
Place lentils in a pot, turn on heat, add some olive oil, stir. Te oil coats the lentils to help them 
stay separate and intact as they cook. 
Add the water, bring to a boil, reduce to a simmer, cover with the lid slightly ajar, cook until 
tender but not mushy. Cooking time varies dramatically for the diﬀerent lentils. 
 »  Red lentils: 5-10 minutes, then add more olive oil, stir gently, season with salt if desired
 » Black “beluga” lentils: 20-25 minutes
 » French lentils: 20-25 minutes
 » Larger brown or green “common” lentils: 30-40 minutes (more if less fresh)

<!-- p.66 -->
60         Legumes
Many other varieties are available in Indian markets, including lentil-like small beans that can 
be treated similarly. Each lentil variety has many uses. 
 »  Red are common for Indian spiced dal (and are good for very quick cooked meals). 
Red lentils cook so quickly and soften nearly into a purée because their outer cas-
ings have been removed. 
 »  Black beluga or French lentils are my favorite to season lightly and serve along with 
rice and vegetables for wonderful vegetarian meals. Tey are so special that they 
make the simplest meal feel noble. Tey are also perfect along with a grain, green, 
and ﬁsh or lamb (season the lentils with more olive oil, salt, pepper and parsley, 
arrange on the plate ﬁrst—beside the grain and green—and place the ﬁsh on top of 
the lentils. If lamb stew, plate that on top of the grain, next to the lentils.)
 »  Basic brown or green lentils are the common choice for Lentil Soup (although any 
lentil can make ﬁne soup, see recipe on page 101).
A Note on the Food Energetics
Lentils are a very important food. Tey nourish stomach yin, supporting a healthy lining of 
the stomach and intestines and encouraging the distribution of ﬂuids. Lentils also help correct 
problems of stomach directionality; the stomach needs to exert a descending directionality 
(lack of descension is called stomach rebellious qi or counterﬂow qi and underlies nausea and 
acid reﬂux conditions). Lentils exert a slight cooling eﬀect, including a calming inﬂuence on 
the emotions and a bit of help in transitioning into sleep after a busy day. Tey are gently 
diuretic as well, part of their clearing and detoxifying capability. High in ﬁber, lentils also aid 
peristalsis and elimination, and are very good for the healthy microbes of the intestines. 
Traditionally, lentils are distinguished by color for localizing their therapeutic qualities. Use 
red lentils for cooling blood and emotions, yellow lentils for nourishing stomach yin, white 
(ivory) lentils for moistening dry cough in the lungs, black lentils to strengthen kidneys and 
constitutional health, and green-brown lentils to help detoxify the liver. Tese are associations 
from the Five Element system as applied to diet. Tere are times such details can be signiﬁcant, 
but most people would simply beneﬁt from more lentils of any type, skillfully cooked and 
rotated for variety.

<!-- p.67 -->
Quick Legumes with Spices         61
Quick Legumes with Spices
Simple food cooked with care is the secret to the best eating. Tis dish is a 
family favorite. It’s fast, inexpensive, and delicious. Te herbs add ﬂavor and 
aid digestion seamlessly. With greens and a grain, legumes provide rich nour-
ishment, a mainstay for vegetarians and omnivores alike. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 5 minutes
Total time: 40 minutes
Ingredients
chana dal, moong dal, or other small legume | 1.25 cups
olive oil | 2 Tbsp to start, more to finish
cumin (whole or ground) | 1/2 tsp
cardamom seed | 1/2 tsp (slightly more if whole seed)
coriander seed | 1/2 tsp (similarly, slightly more if whole)
peppercorns | 1/2 tsp 
sea salt | to taste
fresh cilantro (coriander leaf) | half a bunch
Ingredient Note: Chana dal (split baby chickpeas) or moong dal (split mung beans) are two 
favorite small beans from the Indian food tradition. Tey are widely available in large markets, 
specialty markets or online. Use any small bean you like: lentils, split peas, split yellow peas, 
whole mung beans, pigeon peas, even chickpeas or other bean of your choice. 
Directions
Sort and wash the beans. Soaking beans for two hours in cool water is a good idea but is not 
necessary for small beans. 
In a pot over medium heat, add the oil and the spices (except the salt). Te heat will develop 
the spice notes which will be carried through the dish by the oil.

<!-- p.68 -->
62         Legumes
After 1-2 minutes, add the beans (also known as legumes or pulses), stir to mix them with the 
spices and oil. Add 3-4 cups water (more can be added later). Bring to a boil, then reduce to 
medium heat. Beans tend to foam, so do not cover (the cook can skim foam from the top 
with a spoon to be discarded if needed). Allow legumes to cook 30-40 minutes. Add water if 
needed, or, if too watery, raise ﬂame to reduce liquid gradually. Do not stir during cooking. 
When tender but not mushy, salt to taste, add more olive oil, mix in the chopped fresh cilantro 
and serve with a grain and some greens. Grains, greens and beans together make fully nutri-
tious vegetarian meals (even omnivores should have vegetarian meals regularly). 
Other spices are often used as well. In this context, feel free to add:
 » fresh ginger (sliced, minced or grated)
 » turmeric 
 » mustard seed (not prepared mustard)
 » scallion or shallot
 » citrus peel
 » leaf herbs that aid legume digestion (rosemary, oregano, marjoram, tarragon, sage, etc.) 
Tis recipe makes a very casual dal (soft-cooked small bean dish). For a bit more authenticity, 
use the following method: 
Sort and wash the beans. Add oil but no spices (don’t use olive oil here, use a high-heat oil such 
as ghee, sesame oil, grapeseed oil, avocado oil, etc.). Stir the legumes into the oil, add water, 
bring to boil, reduce to medium. Do not stir. When beans are soft, partially blend by vigorous 
stirring or a very quick spin of a handheld immersion blender. Turn oﬀ. 
In another pan, make an infused oil: Add 2 Tbsp oil or ghee. Allow the oil to get very hot (not 
smoking, however), then reduce heat to medium and add 1 tsp each of cumin seed, mustard 
seed, crushed cardamom pods, ground turmeric and salt. Allow the seed spices to pop brieﬂy. 
Add 2-3 slices of ginger, chopped. Add 3-4 chopped scallions, stir. Add this spice-infused oil 
into the legume pot along with chopped fresh cilantro (or parsley).  Swirl in the infused oil

<!-- p.69 -->
Quick Legumes with Spices         63
loosely, do not mix completely (leave some visual texture). Serve with rice and vegetables for 
a complete meal. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
After eating a well-designed dinner, the belly should feel warm and have a pleasant humming 
feeling, as if a happy purring of the belly is conferring happiness to the whole system. Tis is 
what a good meal should do. Feeling this purring in the belly after a meal of grains and beans 
is a sign that digestion is good, including the complex gut microbiota. 
Te legume varieties recommended for this recipe are classiﬁed as small beans, much easier 
to digest than large beans (such as kidney beans, pinto beans, black beans, lima beans and so 
forth). Small beans aid clearing and are unlikely to contribute to stagnation. Te spices further 
aid digestion and prevent any diﬃculties sometimes associated with beans. As discussed in 
more detail in Book 1, problems digesting beans usually result from disarray of the microbial 
army of the gut.  
Avoid hot peppers and garlic (common additions to this kind of dish), at least most of the 
time. We want to nourish not only protein needs but our ﬂuid system and stomach yin. Hot 
peppers and garlic are strong herbs with strong eﬀects. When used sparingly, they can be useful 
to stimulate appetite and boost yang qi, but regular use tends to dry stomach yin and overheat 
the body.   
In a world of extreme inequality and strained food resources, it can’t be overemphasized that 
vegetarian meals are vastly more sustainable than similarly nutritious meals including meat. 
Grains and legumes (rice and beans, for example) have complementary amino acids that ﬁt 
together like pieces in a puzzle to create complete protein. Together, their protein is equal in 
quality to animal protein (although with a cooler energetic), and the complex ﬁber in such a 
meal cultivates healthy gut microbes in ways that meat cannot.

<!-- p.70 -->
64         Legumes
Vegetable Curry
Curry is a style of cooking with a collection of spices, not a single well-deﬁned 
dish or even one speciﬁc spice blend. Today, “curry” is the national dish of 
Britain, and represents the way much of the world simpliﬁes Indian and 
other Asian cuisine for easy home cooking.
Serves: four
Prep time: 15 minutes
Total time: 30 minutes
Ingredients
carrot | 2 medium, trimmed, not peeled, sliced as you wish
celery | 2-3 medium stalks, trimmed, chopped
shallots | 2 medium, peeled, sliced
garlic | 1 clove, diced
fresh ginger | 5 moderately thin slices, slivered (or equivalent grated)
grapeseed, coconut or other oil | 1 Tbsp
broccoli or cauliﬂower | 1 half head, chopped into small florets
zucchini | 2 medium, cubed
chickpeas | 2 cups, precooked (canned okay)
cumin seed (ground) | 3/4 tsp
cinnamon | 1/2 tsp
cardamom (ground) | 1 tsp
coriander seed | 1/2 tsp
sea salt | as needed
coconut milk (full fat, unsweetened) | 1 can (13-14 oz)
tamarind paste | 2-3 Tbsp
Tai or Vietnamese ﬁsh sauce | 1 Tbsp (use 1Tbsp tamari for vegan option)

<!-- p.71 -->
Vegetable Curry         65
lime | juice of 1 fresh lime
water | 1/2 cup or more
cilantro (coriander leaf) | handful, chopped
Directions
Heat a large pan. Add cool oil to hot pan, add shallots, ginger, garlic, celery, carrot and a pinch 
of sea salt. Stir over medium heat, 1-3 minutes as you gather the spices.
With a wooden spoon, make a clear area in the pan. Add the dry spices and a splash of oil if 
needed. Te aromas and ﬂavors of the dry spices will develop in the hot oil and permeate the 
dish as the seasoned oil spreads throughout. After a minute or so, mix the contents of the pan 
together. 
Add the zucchini and broccoli or cauliﬂower. Improvise with the vegetables: kale, chard, spin-
ach, okra, etc. Add the chickpeas (previously cooked at home or canned). 
Add the ﬁsh sauce, tamarind paste and coconut milk. Stir, bring just to boil, then reduce to a 
gently bubbling simmer. Cover, cook until vegetables are just tender, 7-10 minutes. Add water 
if needed to adjust thickness. If too thin (unlikely), crush some of the zucchini to disappear 
into the sauce. 
Adjust for salt, tartness (add lime), and the funkiness of the ﬁsh sauce (add a small splash if 
needed). Serve over steamed basmati rice, adding a generous amount of fresh cilantro on top 
as garnish. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Te term curry comes from the kari leaf (Murraya koenigii, an aromatic citrus leaf native to In-
dia) that came to represent the enormous complexity and variety of spices used in everyday In-
dian cooking. Te British in India called the many dishes containing kari leaf curries, and the 
curry powder we can purchase today is a mixture of spices meant to approximate the complex 
cooking of the various Indian regional cuisines. Curry powders usually include ground cumin, 
coriander seed, cardamom, mustard seed, dried ginger, black pepper, fenugreek, cinnamon

<!-- p.72 -->
66         Legumes
and clove. (And no kari leaf, though “curry leaf” is increasingly available frozen or refrigerated 
in large or specialty markets.)   Gradually, curry sauces transformed eating in Britain, opening 
the famously bland British palate to an inﬂux of international foods, ultimately transforming 
traditional British fare to surprisingly beautiful levels. 
Because curry is a simpliﬁed adaptation with no “correct” version, you are free to select and 
choose your curry-type dish ingredients for appropriate health inﬂuences. Just as important: 
curries need not be hot or aggressively spiced. 
As you “customize” your curry, work with each element of the dish:
 »  What type of rice or grain: basmati, jasmine, long grain, brown, red, black, or mil-
let, barley, buckwheat (mixed or solo), quinoa, etc. 
 » What oil: grapeseed, sesame, coconut, ghee, etc. 
 » Which vegetables: zucchini, broccoli, cauliﬂower, kale, spinach, etc.
 » Which legumes: chickpeas, lentils, peas, adzuki, etc. 
 » Bulb spices: shallot, leek, scallion, garlic, onion
 » Root spices: ginger, galangal, turmeric, etc.
 » Seed spices: cumin, cardamom, coriander, etc.
 » Leaf spices: curry leaf, lemongrass, cilantro, rosemary, bay leaf, etc.
 » Bark/stalk spices: cinnamon
 » Flower/bud spices: clove
 » Fruit: tamarind (see A Note on Tamarind below)  
Taken together, these ingredient types are a basic template for creating curry dishes. Replace 
any element with one from a similar category and energetic.  For example, if you don’t like 
cumin, use another seed spice such as fenugreek, while retaining cardamom and coriander. 
Chickpeas can switch oﬀ with lentils, or if you don’t like any bean, use chicken, beef, or lamb. 
If you don’t like cilantro, use parsley, fresh basil, or fresh dill. Results are diﬀerent in each case, 
but the bases of the dish remain covered.

<!-- p.73 -->
Vegetable Curry         67
Your preferences and aversions are there to be noticed without undue judgment. It’s okay to 
dislike beans and favor meats, but it oﬀers insight into the state of your digestion if beans cause 
distention even when properly cooked with digestive herbs and spices. Work needs to be done, 
not with the goal of eating more beans but to restore digestion itself. Likewise with cumin and 
parsley; it’s ﬁne to dislike them if you do, but they help relax emotions for expression—is this 
possibly signiﬁcant for you?  
Work with yourself gently but consistently. Our diets can hold us back from our fullness or 
open the way forward. Foods can change minds and hearts. After all, who would possibly have 
guessed that curry would ever become the national dish of England?   
A Note on Tamarind 
Many cooks use sweet and hot peppers in addition to tomato in curries. All peppers 
(also known as capsicum) and tomatoes are fruits of the nightshade family. Nightshades 
have mild but pervasive toxins that heat the blood level and can stoke inﬂammation, 
particularly at the joints. Many people report strong improvements in digestion and 
relief of joint pain when they thoroughly abstain from nightshades. 
Terefore, I recommend tamarind (also a fruit, but not a nightshade) for ﬂavor and 
health inﬂuence. Tart and sweet in taste, tamarind makes a good substitution for toma-
toes and peppers but is very diﬀerent from them energetically. Tamarind cools and clears 
inﬂammation, regulates blood sugar, aids digestion, helps clear abdominal distention, 
and is a mild laxative. Whole dried tamarind can be found in large or specialty markets, 
but tamarind paste is far easier to use and is also available in good markets.

<!-- p.74 -->
six 
More Vegetable Dishes
Root vegetables, squashes, sweet potatoes, mushrooms and fermented vegeta-
bles are all important components of good eating. For some people, these foods 
are mainstays, while for others they appear only occasionally, but all cooks 
should be skilled in preparing them. Here is a sampling of cooking methods 
and dishes to help you and your family enjoy eating more vegetables.

<!-- p.75 -->
Roasted Beets         69
Roasted Beets
In classical Chinese medicine dietetics, beets are classiﬁed as sweet, warming 
and with special connection to kidney, pancreas-spleen and heart. Tey help 
build blood that the liver needs to store, and they help our muscles utilize 
oxygen. Beets help digestion while being nourishing and wonderful to eat. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 15 minutes
Total time: 1 hour 15 minutes
Ingredients
beets | 4 medium-large or 8 small
olive oil | just enough to coat
sea salt | chef’s pinch
Directions
Here are two simple ways to prepare roasted beets:
Preheat oven to 375˚F or 400˚F. Know your oven and know your mood; diﬀerent tempera-
tures yield diﬀerent results and there are times for each. 
1)   Wash beets, leaving skin on, and wrap individually in aluminum foil. Roast for 40-60 min-
utes. When cooked (unwrap one, test with a probe for softness but not mushiness), hold 
the beet under cool running water. Te peel will fall away with gentle rubbing. Te water 
allows you to manage the hot beet, but it won’t cool the inside. 
Gather the peeled beets to a cutting board to slice or chunk. 
Serve warm with drizzle of good olive oil and pinch of good salt. If needed, return them to the 
oven to stay warm while the rest of dinner is underway.  
2)   If you have more prep time, peel the raw beets with a sharp knife. Once peeled, leave small 
beets whole but cut larger ones into quarters or eighths. In a bowl, toss the beets with olive 
oil and salt before roasting on a baking sheet (I like parchment paper under the beets to

<!-- p.76 -->
70         More Vegetable Dishes
ease cleanup). Arrange so that each beet is open to the heat. Beets are done when fork-ten-
der but not mushy, 40-60 minutes depending upon size. 
Beets don’t need to be roasted, of course. For quick vinegar beets, cover raw beets (unpeeled) 
with water, boil until tender, about 30 minutes. When cool enough to handle, cut oﬀ ends and 
peel with a sharp knife or under cool water. Cut into sections, dress with apple cider vinegar 
and olive oil (equal amounts), pinch of sea salt and cracked fresh pepper. 
Beets can be fully pickled as well. Boil 3 pounds beets being careful not to overcook, then 
peel and slice them into uniform shapes of your choice. Place in a crock or jar that ﬁts in your 
refrigerator. In a pot, boil together 2 cups water, 2 cups apple cider vinegar, 1 cup sugar (very 
little of it will be consumed with the beets), 2 shallots, 2 cloves garlic. Reduce and simmer for 5 
minutes. Allow to cool, and then pour over the beets. Stir to release any trapped air, cover, and 
place in refrigerator. Refrigerator pickled beets don’t require sterilized equipment but won’t 
keep as long as formal pickles; eat them within a month or two. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
In Britain and Australia, the term is beetroot; saying simply “beet” will bring blank stares. Tis 
reminds us in America that beets are root vegetables, with a centering energy (round shape and 
sweet taste, both centering signatures) and a descending, root quality. For fun, ask someone 
who says “beetroot” what the beet greens are called…beetroot tops?  Whatever we call them, 
beets are wonderful food, a true food where all parts of the plant are good eating (in contrast 
to tomatoes and potatoes, for example, where the stems and leaves are poisonous). 
Beets are used in Chinese medicine dietetics and modern sports nutrition to build blood, 
lower blood pressure and provide more oxygen throughout the body. For sport performance 
enhancement, beets (often juiced) improve utilization of oxygen by muscles; for healing pur-
poses this quality can be used to protect against or help clear cancers (which tend to grow in 
the relative absence of oxygen). 
Te idea of building blood is very important. We all have enough blood to support life and 
to bleed when cut, but relative blood deﬁciency is common. In simple terms, blood deﬁciency

<!-- p.77 -->
Roasted Beets         71
is the blood aspect behind lassitude or lethargy, often closely related to what Chinese medi-
cine calls qi deﬁciency. In this context, qi is about drive or energy, blood is about resources or 
reserves. 
Blood deﬁciency is easy to note when reading pulses in classical diagnosis from Chinese med-
icine, but if not trained in pulse diagnosis it’s still pretty easy to ﬁnd. A person says, “I would 
like to write a book, or start a business, but I’m working so hard I have no spare time, and I 
have no funds for my startup….”  Tat’s a sign of blood deﬁciency (“I don’t have what I need 
inside….”)  Qi deﬁciency is a bit diﬀerent. With qi deﬁciency a person says, “I have time, I 
have enough money, I buy all the right equipment, but I can’t seem to get going….” 
On a more mechanical level, women of menstruating age are easily prone to blood deﬁciency, 
and the term can also include anyone with a diagnosis of “borderline anemia”. Full anemia is 
deﬁned (in this context) as a deﬁciency of both blood (substance) and qi (energy). Beets help 
build blood as well as help the body utilize oxygen. 
Roasting is the cooking method that resonates with kidney energetics. Te kidneys govern 
very deep health, fertility, genetics, bone and marrow wellness. Good kidney energy feels ﬁrm, 
solid and deep. Roasting any food consolidates its ﬂavors into a smaller space (as water evap-
orates and ﬂavors develop from heat). Tis consolidation is what the kidneys like. In classical 
Chinese dietetics, beets are classiﬁed as sweet, warming and with special connection to kidney, 
spleen-pancreas (they are sweet and nourishing), and heart (their connection to blood). 
In Chinese and Western medicine, blood is seen to be stored in important ways by the liver. 
If the liver is not storing blood well, we can feel depleted. It is not unusual to respond to this 
feeling of blood deﬁciency by craving more food or more material wealth and security. Most of 
us know the feeling that some days we worry about money while other days we don’t, without 
anything real having changed. Liver blood deﬁciency can underlie this sense of insecurity, the 
fear of meeting change, even being unable to stay asleep in the middle of the night. Pickled 
beets will help. Sour is the taste that stimulates the liver to hold and store; pickled beets pro-
vide both blood building and the sourness to hold on to the blood being built.

<!-- p.78 -->
72         More Vegetable Dishes
Roasted Carrots with Dill
Carrots are a great snack raw, but their inﬂuence can be too cooling. When 
carrots are cooked with oil, the nutrients available for assimilation increase 
dramatically (and they’re simply delicious). 
Serves: four to six
Prep time: 10 minutes
Total time: 40 minutes
Ingredients
organic carrots | 4 medium, washed & trimmed (peeling not recommended) (cook 
extra, they keep well in the refrigerator for several days)
olive oil | enough to coat
sea salt | chef’s pinch
fresh dill | 1 bunch
Directions
Cut carrots in ¾-inch chunks, diagonally to preserve the idea of how they grow vertically downward. 
Toss with enough oil to coat, sprinkle with a good bit of salt. 
Roast in a 400˚F oven for 20 minutes, turn or roll, add chopped dill liberally, roast for another 
15-20 minutes. 
Remove before completely soft (depends upon size of carrot and your oven), taste for salt. 
Serve as an appetizer or side dish, or add to a sauté pan with grain and other vegetables. 
Carrots can also be roasted whole for appealing rustic presentation. Serve warm or at room 
temperature. 
As an option, steam rather than roast. Cut carrots as before, steam for 8-10 minutes, remove, 
drain if needed, toss with olive oil, fresh dill, healthy pinch of good quality salt. Or, add a 
splash of balsamic or apple cider vinegar, pumpkin seed oil in place of olive, etc.

<!-- p.79 -->
Roasted Carrots with Dill         73
Parsnips are also wonderful cooked in the same manner, roasted or steamed. Try half carrots 
and half parsnips.   
A Note on the Food Energetics
Carrots are a great snack raw, but their energetic is very cooling. Cook them to contribute 
warmth.  Beta carotene (the famous nutrient named after carrots) isn’t well absorbed if not 
cooked (only 3 percent of their beta carotene can be absorbed when eaten raw, but when 
cooked ten times that amount can be assimilated).  Te  beneﬁt carrots have for digestion 
spreads much further than just digestion of those carrots and their speciﬁc nutrients; carrots 
help restore stomach descension and clear food stagnation, improving digestion overall.
Depending on your digestive needs and your mood, you may cook carrots fully soft or re-
tain some satisfying chewiness. Te crunch or chew in carrots helps stimulate good digestion 
through the way it vibrates the jaw, setting into motion key energetics of digestion (the stom-
ach meridian of acupuncture is stimulated at the jaw). Balance how far to cook carrots based 
on digestive strength or weakness. 
Te vitamins in carrots are of the oil-soluble type (vitamins can also be water soluble). Eating 
carrots with oil improves nutrient assimilation dramatically. Have carrots with olive oil (or 
other healthy oil) whether cooked or raw. 
Dill supports liver and gallbladder, stimulates bile secretion and helps regulate the environ-
ment of the small intestine. 
Te food energetics of carrots with dill are eﬀective and important, but when a hot tray of 
roasted carrots (and parsnips) comes out of your oven, the main thing is that they are abso-
lutely delicious.

<!-- p.80 -->
74         More Vegetable Dishes
Arame with Carrots and Toasted Sesame Oil
Seaweeds contribute important nutrients and functions while bringing a unique 
and deeply ﬂavored note of the sea to the table. Arame is an easy seaweed to enjoy, 
a beginner sea vegetable, if you like. Tis recipe for arame and carrots dressed with 
toasted sesame oil is traditional, easy to make, and irresistible. 
Serves: four to six
Prep time: 15 minutes
Total time: 25 minutes
Ingredients
dried arame | 1 bag, 60 grams
carrots | 2 medium
cooking oil (grapeseed, saﬄower, etc.) | splash, 1-2 Tbsp
tamari | splash, 1 Tbsp
mirin | 1 Tbsp
toasted sesame oil | a splash
sesame seeds | sprinkle, 1-2 Tbsp
Directions
Prep Note: Seaweeds are dried before coming to market. Some can be eaten in that 
dried form (nori, dulse), but others require soaking and some cooking (arame, hijiki). 
Soak arame in warm or hot water to soften. Softening dried seaweeds takes 15-20 minutes. 
(Tis can be done as you begin cooking the meal or you may soak earlier in the day, whichever 
will make cooking more convenient for you.)  Wash the carrots and trim their tops. Cut into 
matchsticks by cutting each carrot into three sections crosswise, then each section lengthwise 
into half, quarters, then eighths. 
Add carrots to a hot pan with just enough cooking oil to coat lightly. After 2-3 minutes, add 
the tamari and mirin.

<!-- p.81 -->
Arame with Carrots and Toasted Sesame Oil         75
Add the softened arame. Lift the arame from its soaking water with tongs, leaving any stray 
bits at the bottom. A bit of this liquid can be added if desired. 
Stir enough to share the oil and ﬂavorings. When the carrots are slightly softened and the 
ﬂavors mixed, turn oﬀ the heat. Perfume with a splash of toasted sesame oil. Garnish with 
sesame seeds.
Serve as a side dish with greens, brown rice, squash, and beans for a macro-bowl, or with 
greens, grain and ﬁsh. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Te ocean is the source of all life and the base of the food chain. Vegetables from the sea—sea-
weed in common jargon—are a powerful and delicious addition to the diet. Seaweeds provide 
a form of seafood for vegetarians or others who don’t eat ﬁsh or crustaceans. Seaweeds are 
also an important supplement for those who do eat traditional seafood while recognizing that 
change is needed as ﬁsh populations are increasingly in crisis. 
Seaweed is rich in protein and minerals. In herbal medicine, seaweed is used to soften hard-
nesses, in other words, to help swell and then dissolve masses that may have formed within the 
body. Seaweed is an important dietary tool for clearing long-standing problems. 
Te art in making a seaweed dish is about aiming that potency. Tis recipe is a traditional one 
for basic health support. Te energy of the sea is sent by the carrots and sesame seeds to the 
lower belly to support constitutional health. 
Arame is an excellent sea vegetable for those who may not already be fans. It is mild, delicious 
and very easy to prepare. Other seaweeds for beginners are nori (familiar as the wrap on sushi 
rolls), wakame (added to miso soup), dulse (a purple seaweed common in Maine and Nova 
Scotia) and kombu (used mostly for making soup stock, see Simple Mushroom Kombu Stock 
on page 86). ‘Advanced’ seaweeds are those richer in mineral taste and thicker in texture. Hijiki 
is an example—it is heavier and cooked in a slightly more involved manner. Energetic diﬀer-
entiation of seaweeds is possible and important, but to begin it is enough to rotate seaweeds 
and to eat them more often.

<!-- p.82 -->
76         More Vegetable Dishes
Basic Sauerkraut
Fermented foods are as old as civilization itself. Originally intended for 
preserving food, fermentation also oﬀers digestive support in multiple ways. 
Start with sauerkraut to begin exploring home fermenting.
  
Serves: ten to twenty servings (2 quarts or liters)
Prep time:  20-30 minutes
Total time:  2 weeks
Ingredients
organic cabbage | 2 medium heads
sea salt | 3-4 Tbsp
caraway seed | 1 tsp
mustard seed | 1 tsp
fennel seed | 1 tsp
Directions
Retain several large outer leaves of cabbage and set aside. Begin ﬁnely slicing the head with a 
sharp knife, avoiding the core. Remove and discard the core. 
Combine the sliced cabbage shreds with the salt in a large mixing bowl, allow to sit 10-15 
minutes, loosely covered with a clean kitchen towel. Liquid will be drawn out of the shredded 
cabbage by the salt.
Now begin vigorous squeezing with clean hands. Cell structures will break, releasing more liq-
uid. Squeeze until the cabbage feels “broken” and spongy, about 10 minutes. Some fermenters 
smash the sliced cabbage with a food hammer or potato masher to save hand eﬀort. 
Mix in the seed spices, then begin packing the soft cabbage into jars. Push it down with your ﬁst—
it is important to squeeze out all air pockets. Te juice will eventually cover the pressed cabbage 
by an inch. Do not ﬁll to the top; leave an inch or two of headroom for fermentation expansion.

<!-- p.83 -->
Basic Sauerkraut         77
Pack the top with the whole leaves that you have set aside. Interestingly, the perfect microbes 
for sauerkraut fermentation are naturally present on the outer leaves of cabbage. Press the 
unbroken leaves down on top of the shredded cabbage, and if possible, down the sides of the 
vessel with a dinner knife. Te juice should come up over the leaves. If it doesn’t, top oﬀ with 
water to cover well. Some cooks weigh the cabbage with clean stones or other weights. It is 
essential to keep all the leaves covered by the brine; exposure to air ruins the sauerkraut. 
Cover the jars loosely, using towel or coﬀee ﬁlter paper, held in place with a rubber band. Be 
sure to allow breathing room for gas expansion. Leave the ﬁlled jars at room temperature in a 
quiet corner of the kitchen (out of direct sunlight or covered with a kitchen towel). Allow to 
ferment 2 weeks or even a month. Longer fermentation will develop more ﬂavor, more micro-
bial beneﬁt, and more tart, sour ﬂavors. 
When it feels done enough to you, cover with a tight lid and move to the refrigerator. Eat 
sauerkraut with meat dishes or as an appetizer, room temperature or warmed in a pan. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Living microbes in fermented foods will help balance the microbiota of the gut. Te sour note 
produced as cabbage properly ferments stimulates the secretions of the liver and gallbladder 
that are essential for neutralizing excess stomach acid and digesting fatty foods. Sauerkraut 
strongly aids digestion, particularly of fatty meats (duck, beef, pork, and especially sausages of 
any type). 
I once sat next to two elegant, slender, expensively dressed elderly ladies in a ﬁne restaurant 
in Paris as they shared an enormous platter of farm-style charcuterie served on a giant bed of 
warm, house-made sauerkraut. Without the sauerkraut, that amount of fatty sausage and pâté 
would be too diﬃcult to digest. Tese two seemed well-practiced in the art of eating. In my 
mind, they have food consumption superhero status. For mere mortals, small amounts of 
sauerkraut add ﬂavor and depth to a meal while helping the gallbladder respond to the fats. By 
all means, have sauerkraut if eating a hot dog—it’s there to help you with what may be fun to 
eat but hard to digest.

<!-- p.84 -->
78         More Vegetable Dishes
Mixed Mushrooms in Balsamic Butter
Mushrooms help clear dampness (retained ﬂuids) and support the deep con-
stitutional level connected to kidney health. Medicinal use of mushrooms is 
thousands of years old; dietary mushrooms bring a bit of that beneﬁt to the 
dining table. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 10 minutes to clean mushrooms
Total time: 15 minutes
Ingredients
mushrooms of your choice | 1 pound
olive oil | 1 Tbsp
butter | 2 Tbsp
balsamic vinegar | 3 Tbsp
red wine (or red wine vinegar) | 1 Tbsp
mixed herbs | 1/2 tsp each if dried, more if fresh
Directions
A note on selecting and washing mushrooms: 
Select mushrooms based on freshness at the market. Start with cremini or 
other basic button type, then add smaller amounts of shiitake, maitake, 
oyster, or enoki. When available, add morels, chanterelles or porcini. Dried 
mushrooms are fantastic on their own, as a base for stocks or soups, and 
combined with fresh mushrooms (soak in warm water to rehydrate before 
trimming and adding to cooking). A bountiful mushroom mix is wonderful, 
but balance your choices so your mélange doesn’t become a mishmash. I usu-
ally select up to three or four types, not more. 
Mushrooms absorb water; true mushroom lovers brush dirt oﬀ their ‘shrooms to clean them 
without washing with water (use a clean brush or simply a paper towel). A quick rinse just pri-

<!-- p.85 -->
Mixed Mushrooms in Balsamic Butter         79
or to cooking is acceptable, but do not wash mushrooms in advance. Trim tough stems or dis-
colored bits with a sharp knife.  Slice the mushrooms coarsely so their identities remain clear. 
Heat a heavy pan, add the olive oil, then the butter. Te oil protects the butter from too much 
heat. 
Add the mushrooms, stir. Keep heat high enough to evaporate the moisture that the mush-
rooms give oﬀ as they cook. 
Add the balsamic vinegar and the red wine (use red wine vinegar if preferred). 
Add herbs if desired, such as rosemary, oregano, basil, chives, thyme. Salt is not necessary—
enough may be included in the butter, and in any case the rich tart and sweet notes of the 
balsamic vinegar are suﬃcient. (If grilling mushrooms with just olive oil, salt generously.)  
If the pan sauce is thin, raise heat to reduce and add another bit of butter to tighten the sauce 
around the mushrooms. 
Serve as a side dish with greens and a protein such as a bean dish, ﬁsh, chicken or meat.  
A Note on the Food Energetics
Mushrooms are part of a vast fungal kingdom. Ten thousand mushroom species are known, 
more are expected to be discovered. Mushrooms comprise complex ecosystems that live con-
tinuously underground, popping into view after rains as if out of nowhere. Mushrooms pos-
itively thrive during the damp season. Energetically, therefore, mushrooms handle dampness. 
Dampness (water weight, accumulations of mucus and phlegm, swellings, etc.) weighs on 
digestion, hampering the transformation of food into nutrients and energy. Anything that 
relieves a problem for digestion automatically strengthens metabolism. In my practice, many 
people tell me they have been instructed not to eat mushrooms because they have dampness 
symptoms (particularly yeast or fungal overgrowth such as candida). I respect the advice of 
other practitioners (we can always steer around the use of any speciﬁc ingredient) but in fact 
mushrooms help clear fungal or yeast overgrowth. Teory and practice converge: mushrooms 
thrive in damp terrain on the lawn or forest ﬂoor; they help manage the damp terrain within.

<!-- p.86 -->
80         More Vegetable Dishes
Mushrooms have two other important functions: they support the constitutional level of 
health (represented by the kidneys) and, because some mushrooms are medicinal or even 
psychoactive, all mushrooms have a subtle aspect of waking the spirit. It is as if dietary mush-
rooms are very polite diplomats from the kingdom of hallucinogens, reminding us that life is 
interconnected beyond our normal vision. Mushrooms, which are in fact vastly interconnect-
ed beneath the surface, are emissaries of their knowledge. 
Te various mushrooms all have diﬀerent speciﬁc beneﬁts. For the cook’s attention at this level 
of health support, it is enough to pick your favorites and mix shapes and colors in order to 
make a nice dish. Te main shapes are the button types, the open types (oyster, chanterelle), 
skinny (enoki), and plump (porcini, king). 
Balsamic vinegar, made from the leftovers of the wine-making process, is a grape vinegar, thus 
useful for building and banking blood. Balsamics range from long-aged expensive artisanal 
bottles, best reserved for direct tasting drop by drop, and commercially produced imitations. 
For cooking, a moderately priced six-year-old choice is more than suﬃcient. Being well-bal-
anced between sour and sweet in taste, balsamic vinegar is useful to help regulate the energetics 
of the middle level, the interplay of liver, gallbladder, pancreas-spleen and stomach. 
Butter supports hormone production, and because hormone excess is so often a response to 
underlying deﬁciency in another part of the endocrine complex, butter exerts a harmonizing 
eﬀect on the hormones. Butter was incorrectly maligned by two generations of conventional 
health thinking; now it is welcomed as a source of healthy oil. Don’t overuse butter, but do 
include it as a healthy dietary fat.

<!-- p.87 -->
Mushrooms with Mustard Cream Sauce          81
Mushrooms with Mustard Cream Sauce
I generally don’t recommend dairy of this type, but with the spices and herbs 
this will digest much better than equal calories and fat from, for example, ice 
cream. I have three reasons to include such a rich and pleasing dish here—see 
below to read them. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 15 minutes
Total time: 25 minutes
Ingredients
cremini or mixed mushrooms | 1 pound, cleaned, trimmed, sliced
shallots | 2 medium, trimmed, sliced
butter | 1 tsp
parsley | half a handful, trimmed and chopped, including stems; reserve leaves
prepared French mustard | 2 Tbsp
heavy cream | 1 cup
nutmeg | several scrapes, perhaps 1/8 tsp
sea salt | pinch
fresh black pepper | a few grinds
Directions
Melt the butter in a large pan, add the shallots and the sliced mushrooms. Mushrooms absorb 
dampness as they grow, often releasing water during cooking. Cook until mushrooms are soft 
and some of the liquid has cooked away. Add the chopped parsley stems, reserving the leaves. 
Add the mustard (grainy or Dijon style) and the heavy cream. Cook hot enough to bubble and 
reduce for 2-3 minutes.

<!-- p.88 -->
82         More Vegetable Dishes
Add the parsley leaves. Add a few scrapes of fresh nutmeg, just enough to notice as a myste-
rious hint of complexity. Test for salt (none will be needed if salted butter was used). Crack a 
few grinds of pepper on top. 
Serve as a bed for ﬁsh or lean meat, or as sauce for rice and lentils.
A Note on the Food Energetics
What?  A dish with cream in a book about health?  I can feel the indignation. But I have three 
strong reasons to include this delicious and easy dish. 
First, many people lose motivation by trying to adhere to a strict diet without suﬃcient rich-
ness. Tey begin with best intentions but don’t feel satisﬁed with their new cooking. It then 
becomes too tempting to rely on restaurants for dishes that are fatty and salty enough to satisfy 
something that is hard to pinpoint. Restaurants are wonderful, but they are best enjoyed for 
cooking styles beyond our home skills or ingredient set, not because we aren’t satisﬁed with the 
food we make at home. Include luxurious dishes like this one occasionally at home, and in the 
balance, you will still be eating far better. 
Second, in the dietary clinic it is common to see people who are trying so hard to eat well 
that they have starved certain aspects of their nutritional needs. I have seen people whose salt 
intake was so low they developed mysterious cramping (no diagnosis from modern medicine 
was found, but cramping disappeared within a day of restoring normal dietary salt). Far more 
common are individuals reducing dietary fat to the point where their hormone levels become 
imbalanced. Hormones are mostly derived from fat in the diet; a strict low-fat diet can confuse 
the body: “I am full, I am not hungry, but I don’t have what I need!”  Cravings often arise. 
Stress hormones are made from fat—a diet too low in fat leaves anyone handling stress unsup-
ported. Too much fatty food can cause problems, but high-quality dietary fats used skillfully 
are health-supportive. 
Te third reason for this recipe is that my son insisted I include this one. When he was twelve, 
we took him and his sister to Paris, where he had a great experience with this dish. Children 
need fat and protein to grow, building hormones, ﬂesh, and bones.

<!-- p.89 -->
Mushrooms with Mustard Cream Sauce          83
As rich as it is, there is balance to this recipe. Te mustard, nutmeg and parsley make the 
cream less likely to cause stagnation. Mushrooms strengthen kidneys and help the body resolve 
dampness, or, in this case, help prevent the dampness that could come from the butter and 
cream. 
I generally don’t recommend dairy or this type of animal fat; however, with the spices and 
herbs it’s easy to understand that the calories and animal fat here will digest much better than 
equal calories and animal fat from, for example, ice cream. More important than discussing 
merits or demerits of a dish is to remember that when indulging occasionally, we must not be 
guided merely by what attracts us (chefs can always make enticing dishes), but also by what is 
appropriate for our good health in speciﬁc terms.

<!-- p.90 -->
seven
Soups and Broths 
One of the biggest losses in modern eating is the disappearance of soups as 
part of nearly every meal. Soups provide hydration and soothe digestion. 
Tey support health by being so easy to digest. Soups are easy to make and 
are transformative for health.

<!-- p.91 -->
Simple Mushroom Kombu Stock         85
Simple Mushroom Kombu Stock
Using well-structured stocks as the base of other dishes is the chef’s secret, more 
important than knife technique or attractive plating. Stocks add depth of 
ﬂavor and nourishment that can change your life. Mushrooms and seaweed 
can bring even a quick stock to the constitutional qi level. Tis stock from 
Japanese cuisine is great for quick cooking. 
Serves: four to eight 
Prep time: 5 minutes
Total time: 10-15 minutes
Ingredients
kombu | 1 strip
dried black mushrooms | 6-8
water | 1-2 quarts
Directions
Drop one dried kombu strip and a handful of dried mushrooms into a pot with 1-2 quarts of 
cool water. Turn heat to high and bring to a boil. Ten reduce to medium and cook for about 
10 minutes. Remove the kombu (it can be discarded, or sliced and sautéed in another dish). 
Allow the mushrooms to continue to gently cook another 10 minutes or so. 
Lift the mushrooms out with a slotted spoon. After they cool enough to handle, remove and 
discard their stems (the stems of these mushrooms will never soften and should not be eaten). 
Slice the mushroom caps for use in a soup or other dish. 
Te stock is ready to be made into miso soup, ﬁsh soup, to sip as a simple broth, or any other 
cooking use. It’s mild but very tasty, full of the subtle depth of mushrooms and the sea. 
Fresh mushrooms can be used, of course, with diﬀerent taste and eﬀect. Experiment. Trow in 
celery if you like, or carrot, to begin to transform a quick stock to a simple soup (see the Clear 
Soup recipe that follows). Sometimes I add seed spices like star anise, fenugreek, peppercorns 
or mustard seed, and sometimes herbs that overlap medicine and kitchen use such as jujube,

<!-- p.92 -->
86         Soups and Broths 
goji berries, bai zhu, huang qi, and dang gui. Spices and herbs ﬁne-tune the broth. Follow your 
own feelings, knowledge and experience. 
Tis stock will keep in the refrigerator for about a week and in the freezer for several months. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Many broths are made from bones (see Beef Bone Broth recipe, page 95; and Fish Bone Broth, 
page 99). Bone broths bring the deepest essence of the animal into the broth, in turn nour-
ishing our deepest level of health: bones, marrow, reproductive essence, and kidneys. To reach 
that depth in a vegetarian stock, it’s important to use seaweed and mushrooms.

<!-- p.93 -->
Clear Soup         87
Clear Soup
Clear soups are light and easy to make. Clearing internal dampness requires 
a strong message of suﬃcient hydration along with a nudge to let go of stag-
nation. Here, diuretic green vegetables are paired with mushrooms to help the 
bladder and kidney with their functions, all while providing plenty of new 
ﬂuids in a delicious, simple soup. Oh, and the greens help relax the liver, too. 
Serves: four
Prep time:  10-15 minutes
Total time: 45 minutes
Ingredients
dried black mushrooms (shiitake type) | 8 (soak in boiled water, discard stem, slice)
water | 7 cups (not counting the mushroom soaking water)
kombu | 1 strip
carrots | 2 medium
green vegetable  
(string beans, snow peas, bok choy, broccoli, etc.) | heaping handful
tamari | 1.5 Tbsp 
scallion | 1 (finely chopped)
cilantro (coriander leaf) | 8 sprigs
sea salt | 1/2 tsp
toasted sesame oil | 1/2 tsp
Directions
In a bowl, cover the dried mushrooms in boiled water to soak. When they soften (15-30 min-
utes depending on their size), cut oﬀ and discard their stems, then slice the caps and return 
them to their soaking water. Fresh mushrooms could be used, of course (omit the soaking 
step).

<!-- p.94 -->
88         Soups and Broths 
In a good size pot, bring the water to boil with the kombu strip (kombu is easily available in 
good markets, near the soy sauce). Simmer for 10 minutes, then remove.   Te seaweed can be 
discarded or sliced and sautéed in other dishes. Its job here is done. 
Add the raw vegetables. Add the mushroom slices and most of the mushroom soaking water 
(be careful to leave any grit at the bottom of the bowl). Simmer until the greens are just barely 
cooked (bright green but still a hint crunchy). 
Add the salt and the tamari (traditionally brewed wheat-free soy sauce). 
Ladle into bowls. Add the scallions, a sprig or two fresh cilantro and a few drops of toasted 
sesame oil to each bowl before serving.
A Note on the Food Energetics
While a thick soup is nourishing, warming and building, a clear soup soothes and helps to 
clear and refresh the ﬂuid system (and of course, also provides nourishment). 
Old ﬂuids tend toward stagnation, a bit like stagnant pond water. Lots of hydration is needed 
to signal to the body that stagnant ﬂuids can be let go. Te body needs to hear the signal: Tere 
are plenty of good ﬂuids arriving. Use snow peas or string beans as the vegetable to renew the ﬂu-
id system (they are gentle diuretics). Other greens have diﬀerent properties but are also good. 
Black mushrooms connect with kidney energetics, also helping clear dampness while supporting 
our ancestral qi, our deep sense of wellness and conﬁdence in our life’s mission. 
If we have conditions relating to excessively thick ﬂuids (such as a chronic cough or sinusitis) 
the addition of aromatic spices and herbs is needed. Te cilantro hints at this, but more will 
be needed. You might use mint, black peppercorn, Szechuan peppercorn, mustard seed, nearly 
raw onion (sliced, added for 5 minutes of cooking) or cardamom. Don’t use everything at 
once; ﬁnd what you like and what helps penetrate the thick turbidity over a period of a few 
days or more.

<!-- p.95 -->
Chicken and Rice Soup         89
Chicken and Rice Soup
Tis soup makes a complete meal. Rice and chicken both connect directly 
to the organs of digestion: stomach and pancreas-spleen. Tis soup helps to 
restore us with very little digestive strain.
Serves: six to eight 
Prep time: 20-30 minutes
Total time: 1.5 hours
Ingredients
chicken |  1 small whole chicken, chopped into breasts, legs, wings (if you purchase 
parts, buy 2 breasts with bones and 2-4 drumsticks)
carrots | 6 medium, diced
leek | 2 medium, whites only, halved and sliced across the length into thin half rings.
celery | 4 stalks, cut thinly 
zucchini | 2, cut lengthwise, lengths cut lengthwise again, diced 
bay leaf | 1
whole peppercorns | 1/2 Tbsp
turmeric | ground, 1 tsp
parsley | 1 bunch 
scallions | 3 finely sliced
salt | 2-3 Tbsp, or to taste
long-grain white rice | 1.5 cups uncooked, or 2.5 cooked rice
Directions
In a large pot, brown the chicken in its skin, and render some of the fat from the skin to the 
pot (use a splash of olive oil to get started if needed). Remove the chicken, set aside, add the 
carrots, leek, celery, zucchini, bay leaf, peppercorns, and turmeric, with a couple chef’s pinches

<!-- p.96 -->
90         Soups and Broths 
salt. Cook the vegetables and spices in the fat left in the pan for 4-5 minutes. (Save the scal-
lions and parsley for the end.)  
Add three inches of water into the pot. Add back the chicken parts and cook for 30 minutes.
With a slotted spoon, take out the chicken and vegetables and set them aside in a bowl. Dis-
card the bay leaf. 
Bring the broth to a boil, add the 1 to 1.5 cups raw rice (depending on desired thickness), 
reduce heat to simmer, cover, cook for 20 minutes. If using already cooked rice, simply add it 
to the pot and bring just up to a boil. 
While the rice is cooking, remove the chicken meat from the bones and shred into large bits. 
It’s best to include the chicken skin in the soup for healthy oils the skin includes, but feel free 
to remove it, if you must, along with any fatty or unappealing bits. Allow the chicken pieces 
to be full bite-size.  
When the rice is cooked, add back the vegetables and the shredded chicken. Add the scallions 
(ﬁnely sliced). Adjust for salt (it will need perhaps 2 tsp more). Diﬀerent size pots, diﬀerent 
heat control, and diﬀerent varieties of rice could change the soup’s thickness; add some boiled 
water to open the soup if necessary. Add the coarsely chopped parsley just before serving. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Grain, vegetables, protein, spices—that’s the template for a basic meal. Chicken and Rice 
Soup is a complete meal in that sense. It can be made with store-bought chicken broth, but it’s 
not diﬃcult to make your own and the result when the soup is fully homemade is very much 
worth it. 
Chicken fat is an age-old ingredient and can be used well. If you are uncomfortable with it, you 
may begin the dish with olive oil and skinless chicken, but that would be a shame. Saturated fat 
is very stable and a necessary precursor to stress hormone production, and therefore good for in-
dividuals with inﬂammation conditions. Maybe some of us should have more of it instead of less. 
Most important is the idea of what a soup really is. Chicken soup is the cook’s way of drawing

<!-- p.97 -->
Chicken and Rice Soup          91
out the essence of the bird, to capture its life force into the pot and from there to the bowl and 
the lucky ones who eat it. Tat’s the point of it, the purpose for the farmer, the grocer, and the 
cook. It’s magical, it’s an alchemical process. Use chicken parts to make it easy, but once in a 
while use a whole chicken and match intention with process to the fullest. 
Chicken and rice pair very well. In classical Chinese medicine dietetics, they both connect 
directly to the organs of digestion, stomach and pancreas-spleen. Tey help us build back 
strength when exhausted, with very little digestive strain. 
If sick or feeling about to go under, this soup provides nourishment and hydration in nearly 
perfect combination. Immunity cannot be well supported if we are not well hydrated; subtle 
dehydration underlies catching colds and ﬂus. More soups, more smiles, and more rest are the 
best preventive medicine. 
If your energy is very low, adding a clove of garlic (crushed) early in the cooking can add a boost, 
but too much garlic too often will contribute to dehydration and no longer strengthen immunity. 
Use garlic wisely. 
Have Chicken and Rice Soup occasionally in cool weather for health maintenance (chicken 
has a very warming energetic), or daily while recovering from a cold, ﬂu, chest infection or 
certain types of digestive distress. It’s a beautiful comfort food. 
Note: Chickens were once very expensive, with golden fat and robust health. Today’s chickens 
are vastly cheaper, but factory farms raise inferior birds who are usually sick and medicated at 
the time of culling. Have chicken less often and at higher quality; buy organic, range-raised 
birds. Good ingredients simply cooked, that’s the secret.

<!-- p.98 -->
92         Soups and Broths 
Egg Drop Soup
Te familiarity of Egg Drop Soup doesn’t do justice to the brilliance of this 
dish. Delicious especially when homemade, it’s also a very easy way to eﬀec-
tively build blood and support digestion.
Serves: one (multiply proportions per person)
Prep time: 10 minutes
Total time: 10 minutes (if you have chicken broth on hand)
Ingredients
chicken broth | 1.5 cups
organic cornstarch or kuzu powder | 1 tsp
egg | 1
fresh chives or scallion | several chives or white of 1 scallion
tamari | a splash (1/2 tsp)
sea salt | pinch
toasted sesame oil | a splash (1/4 tsp)
Directions
In a small bowl, beat the egg. Mix in the tamari, set aside.
In a smaller bowl, mix the cornstarch or kuzu with enough cool water to make a slurry. Tis 
thickens the soup but must always be added wet, not as powder. Also set this aside. 
In a saucepan, heat the chicken broth (homemade is best by far, but use a purchased broth if 
you have one you like). Salt to taste (may need none). Bring just to a boil. Gently stir in the 
cornstarch slurry. Drizzle the beaten egg and tamari while gently stirring the soup. Te cooked 
egg texture is controlled by the stirring speed; practice once and you’ll have it. 
Finish by adding the toasted sesame oil and the chopped chives (or thinly sliced scallion 
whites).

<!-- p.99 -->
Egg Drop Soup         93
A Note on the Food Energetics
Chicken soup provides the essence of the chicken in the easiest possible form for digestion 
and assimilation. Adding the egg ampliﬁes chicken soup into a powerhouse dish that could be 
called “two-way chicken”. Egg Drop Soup helps to build blood and support digestion (see the 
recipe for Roasted Beets on page 70 for more discussion of building blood). 
Te bright yellow color of the soup you may have seen in some Chinese restaurants comes 
from food coloring. Te color pays homage to the color of eggs and the yellow fat of well-
raised chickens, but yellow food dye is widely considered unsafe and is best omitted. Egg Drop 
Soup is a food that we should learn to accept in its real color (dried apricots are another exam-
ple). Addition of 1/4 tsp turmeric powder will brighten the soup’s color if desired. 
All soups are adaptable. Add slivered ginger and a few mushrooms, or for a complete meal, 
serve a larger portion with a few leaves of spinach, kale or other green, sesame seeds sprinkled 
on top, and a bowl of steamed rice.

<!-- p.100 -->
94         Soups and Broths 
Beef Bone Broth
Good food protects our constitutional health, important when our constitu-
tions are strong and essential when they are not. Bone broth is one of the most 
eﬀective ways to reach and support the deepest level of our physical health.
Serves: 4-8 quarts 
(depending on pot capacity)
Prep time: 30 minutes
Total time: two days
Ingredients
fresh or frozen beef knee bones | 3-5 pounds
leek | 1 large or 2 small, white part and some green, chunked
carrots | 3 or more, medium, chunked
celery | 2-3 stalks, trimmed and coarsely chopped
olive oil | 1-2 Tbsp
salt | 1-3 Tbsp
dried mushrooms (black or porcini) |  6-8 whole mushrooms, 1-2 ounces if porcini (or 
combine)
kombu | 2 strips
seed spices |  1 tsp each of mustard seed, black peppercorns, fenugreek seed;  
1/2 tsp allspice,  
3-5 star anise pods,  
6 green cardamom pods
bay leaf | 2
other kitchen herbs |  6 red jujube dates, 1/2 handful goji berries, peel from 1/2 tan-
gerine or mandarin orange (omit if not available)
water | 4-8 quarts, depending upon your pot size
vinegar (apple cider vinegar or red wine vinegar) | 1/4 cup
Shopping Note: Buy knee and ankle bones from a good butcher shop. Tey

<!-- p.101 -->
Beef Bone Broth         95
will quarter them with a table saw to allow best cooking. Marrow bones are 
a delicacy for some but avoid them for making broth. We are looking for the 
collagen in the bone joints, not the marrow of the long bones. 
Directions
Roast the bones on a baking sheet in 350˚F oven for 20-30 minutes. 
While bones are roasting, coarsely chop the leek, carrots, celery and any other root vegetable 
that you may have ready for the stock pot (try daikon, parsnip, etc.)  Toss the vegetables into 
the largest pot you have, add the olive oil, start them over medium-high heat with a good 
pinch of salt. Add the dried mushrooms and the kombu strips. Add the seed spices and kitchen 
herbs.
When the bones have browned noticeably, add them to the stock pot (discard the fat they give 
oﬀ or use for other things). Cover with plenty of water (your pot should be about 3/4 full of 
water). Stock should not boil—boiling can turn the broth cloudy and slightly bitter. When the 
ﬁrst boiling bubbles rise, turn to the lowest setting; the stock should have a slow-rising bubble 
every few seconds, no more. 
Add the vinegar (I often include two or three types of vinegar to add complexity). Te acidity 
of vinegar draws calcium and collagen from the bones into the broth. Te ﬁnished broth will 
not be acidic. 
Simmer, partially covered for one or two days, nonstop. I often let bone stock cook for two 
and a half days. Check periodically that it is hot enough for an occasional bubble but not hot 
enough to reach a true boil. Too cool won’t extract essence from the bones and could at least 
potentially allow some bacteria to grow; too hot and the stock isn’t of highest quality. Add 
water if needed. 
When you’re ready, pour the stock through a strainer into another pot, discard the bones, 
vegetables and herbs. When cool enough, ladle the strained broth into refrigerator containers; 
refrigerate overnight. Fat will separate to the top for easy removal and the stock will show its 
character by congealing through the natural gelatin from the bones. Some may wish to reserve

<!-- p.102 -->
96         Soups and Broths 
the beef fat for other uses, but fat is not needed in the bone broth itself—it’s the essence from 
bones and vegetables rendered through the cooking process that we’re after. 
Warm portions in a saucepan to use. Some people like to sip bone broth straight, others like to 
open its taste by diluting it with some warm water. Salt to taste as desired. For superbly nour-
ishing cooking, use beef bone broth as foundation for soups, braise dishes, sauces and stews. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Bones resonate with our constitutional health, the deepest of the three general levels of health 
(digestive health status is the middle level and immune-defensive health is the upper level). 
As described in Book 1, we are born with certain strengths and resources, bequeathed by ge-
netics and how pregnancy, birthing and very early childhood went for us. Tis is the prenatal 
component. What we eat and digest every day after weaning provides postnatal nourishment. 
Te primary role of diet is to supply postnatal nourishment so successfully that our bodies 
don’t need to tap prenatal resources, as happens, for example, if the diet is too acidic and the 
calcium in our bones is used to neutralize acids in the blood. Eating good food protects our 
constitutional health. 
Is it possible to build back constitutional health when it has been compromised?  Tis has 
long been a subject of debate within the history of Chinese medicine (in modern biomedicine 
the topic doesn’t arise). Certainly, we can conserve and preserve our constitutional health 
with good living habits: good food, good rest, good interpersonal relations and emotions. But 
whether or not constitution can be rebuilt, it can deﬁnitely be supported, and bone broth is 
one of the strongest ways to reach this deepest level.
Strengthen the constitutional resonance of bone broth by including seaweed (all life begins 
in the mysterious sea), dried mushrooms (mysterious plant that grows in the moist darkness), 
and root vegetables (roots anchor sunlight into the earth). Use spices and herbs to ensure all 
this grounding inﬂuence isn’t simply heavy. Spices add motion and lift. Tey tune the broth for 
speciﬁc purposes. (I ﬁne-tune bone broth using spices and herbs for women’s fertility, digestive 
issues, bone breaks, etc.)

<!-- p.103 -->
Beef Bone Broth         97
Bone broths are a current food fad (wonderful), but I am concerned about the health eﬀects of 
bone broths with garlic and hot peppers. Although perhaps tempting, it is best to avoid bone 
broth featuring garlic or hot peppers. When reaching to the deepest health level in the very 
special way bone broth does, it’s important to consolidate, not disperse the constitution with 
overly stimulating spices. 
What about the taste of bone broth?  Some don’t like it. Tis can mean three things:
 » It’s not right for them (their body is sending true signals).
 » Tey are resisting this level of dietary support (their emotional body is sending sig-
nals of resisting change).
 » Or, maybe it’s not a good-tasting bone broth. 
To make a great-tasting bone broth that the body’s wisdom accepts and relishes, be sure to 
follow the instructions above: buy knee or ankle joints rather than marrow bones, roast them 
in the oven, add vegetables, herbs and quality vinegars, cook at just a simmer for a couple days. 
Te results are so good that vegetarians have often asked for a tub of it from the huge pot at 
our place.

<!-- p.104 -->
98         Soups and Broths 
Fish Bone Broth
Fish Bone Broth is much quicker to make than beef bone broth. Fish bones 
are thin and ﬂexible, yielding their essence in much less time.
Serves: six to eight
Prep time: 30 minutes
Total time: 1-8 hours
Ingredients
ﬁsh skeleton | 2-3 skeletons if from small fish, 1/2-1 if from large fish
carrot | 3-4 medium
leek | 1 medium, white part and some green
celery | 2-3 stalks
olive oil | 1-2 Tbsp
salt | 2 tsp or more
dried black mushrooms | 6
kombu | 1 piece
water | 3-4 quarts
white wine vinegar | 1/4 cup
Directions
Shopping Note: Near where I live there is a superb ﬁsh shop. Te ﬁsh mongers 
there were very enthusiastic that some people still cook with ﬁsh bones, and used 
to give me whole skeletons, which they call racks. Ten, they started charging me 
a few dollars per pound. Suddenly, the shop reconﬁgured to sell more premade 
food and the ﬁlleting is done elsewhere, no more beautiful racks from cod or 
salmon. Now I buy small whole ﬁsh (widely available) such as branzini or trout. 
Once scaled and gutted (any shop will do that for you), chop into 3-4 sections 
to expose the bones and proceed with the broth method here (ﬂesh intact), or, 
ﬁllet the ﬁsh for later use before making broth with the skeletons, heads and ﬁns.

<!-- p.105 -->
Fish Bone Broth         99
Begin by roasting the bones on an oven tray at 350˚F for 20 minutes. 
While the bones are roasting, coarsely chop a few carrots, the leek and celery stalks and toss 
them into the stock pot with a splash of olive oil and a generous pinch of salt. 
Also toss in a half dozen dried mushrooms and a single strip of dried kombu. Add an inch of 
water to soften the vegetables, mushrooms and kombu. 
Feel free to improvise with other root vegetables such as daikon, burdock or parsnip, but avoid 
those that will overwhelm the color of the stock (such as beets).
