Add the ﬁsh bones, with ﬁns, some ﬂesh and ﬁsh head if still intact. Use a couple ﬁsh skeletons 
from a small ﬁsh such as branzini, or a half a skeleton if a large ﬁsh such as cod or grouper. 
Cover with cool water. Bring up to nearly boiling but quickly reduce heat to sustain only a 
simmer. Assiduously avoid allowing any stock to reach a rolling boil.  
Add the vinegar to extract calcium from the bones (the calcium of the bones will neutralize the 
acid in the vinegar, the extracted calcium will stay in the broth, which will not taste vinegary). 
Spices and herbs can be added according to taste and intention: rosemary, bay leaf, parsley, 
tarragon, thyme, peppercorns, allspice, etc. 
Cook at a low simmer for 1-8 hours. Strain in a colander over another pot or large bowl, al-
lowing ample time for all the essential goodness to drip from the solids. 
If you like, you may cook the bones longer, there is no harm in it. 
Salt to taste and sip as broth or use in further cooking as stock. 
Fish bone stock stores well in the refrigerator for ﬁve days or in the freezer for two months. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Bones resonate with the constitutional level of health, as do most types of food from the sea. 
Fish stock contains this imprint in an especially delicious way. Use Fish Bone Broth as the base 
for ﬁsh soups or chowders, for exquisite braised ﬁsh and vegetable dishes (see recipe for Braised 
Fish with Lima Beans, page 115), or as a revitalizing bone-broth sipping option.

<!-- p.106 -->
100       Soups and Broths 
Lentil Soup
Lentils are an ancient superfood, used as a daily staple for thousands of years 
from the Mediterranean to India, now in all parts of the world. Tey harmo-
nize the middle and are very well balanced between building and clearing. 
A good lentil soup is equally healthful and tasty. 
Serves: eight
Prep time: 15 minutes
Total time: 90 minutes
Ingredients
scallions | 2-3
carrots | 2 medium or 1 large
celery | 2 stalks
olive oil | 2 Tbsp 
brown lentils | 1 cup
small French lentils | 1/2 cup
red lentils | 1/2 cup
cumin seed | 1/2 tsp
star anise | 3 star pods
green cardamom | 5 pods
ground cinnamon | 1/2 tsp
ground fresh ginger | 1 tsp
parsley | 1 bunch
water | 2 quarts
sea salt | to taste (reserve until end)
lime juice | juice of 1 lime (or 1/2 lemon)
black (Shanxi) vinegar or red wine vinegar | to taste, perhaps 2 Tbsp

<!-- p.107 -->
Lentil Soup        101
Directions
Prep Note: Like all beans, lentils can have stones or grit and need to be sort-
ed and washed before cooking. In batches, pour lentils onto a white plate, 
inspect by moving with dry ﬁngers and picking out any stray matter. Be 
thorough. When all the lentils have been sorted, cover with running water, 
wash well, strain into a colander, repeat. 
Dice the scallions, carrots and celery, add to a large soup pot with a splash of olive oil. Nor-
mally we would salt at this stage, but legumes cook better without salt, so add no salt until the 
soup is nearly ﬁnished. Cook until slightly softened, about 5 minutes. 
Add the washed lentils to the pot, stirring over medium heat. 
Add half of each of the spices. Put the other half in a small saucepan, but do not heat yet. 
After allowing the lentils and spices to warm, add the water, bring to a boil, reduce to a sim-
mer, and cook until lentils are soft, about 45 minutes. Te red lentils will nearly dissolve, 
thickening the soup; if you choose to use only brown lentils, partially smooth the soup with 
a handheld blender. If using a countertop blender, remove 1/3 of the soup to blend before re-
turning it to the main pot to mix together. Te goal is to partially purée the soup while leaving 
plenty of discernible lentils intact.   
Warm the remaining spices in the small pan (no oil is needed), then add them to the soup 
pot. Tis step ensures the spice presence is both deep (the long-cooked spices) and vivid (the 
late-addition spices). 
Chop plenty of parsley, add to the soup. Before serving, add salt, lime juice and vinegar.  
Hearty soups are often tastier when reheated a day or two later, so make enough soup to have 
plenty left over. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
An original Old World bean, lentils resonate with the liver and kidneys. Tey also nourish

<!-- p.108 -->
102        Soups and Broths 
ﬂuids of the stomach and help restore stomach descension in cases of reﬂux or stomach qi re-
belliousness (an issue of confused directionality).  Lentils aid immune strength by helping the 
stomach organize ﬂuid distribution, helping us stay hydrated beyond just drinking liquids. 
All beans are high in protein and ﬁber, engaging the liver in protein synthesis and aiding 
peristalsis. As small beans, lentils are better at clearing liver toxins than larger beans. Tey are 
also mildly diuretic, clearing old ﬂuids as they build new, therefore helping lower high blood 
pressure, clear symptoms of UTI, or assist resolving edema. 
Knowing the value of lentils, select spices to ﬁne-tune your cook’s intention. Some people have 
trouble digesting even the small beans like lentils, adzuki or mung. Cinnamon, ginger, cumin, 
cardamom and nutmeg are warming spices that aid in their digestion and resolve any feelings 
of distention. Turmeric, citrus peel, rosemary, tarragon, oregano and bay leaf also work very 
well. 
Foods Become Medicine Trough Repetition
To gain the beneﬁt of a food, it must be used frequently. With this in mind, varying spices is an 
important cook’s tool to support regular use of important foods while avoiding “taste fatigue” 
(and to adjust with the seasons). 
If, however, a very speciﬁc aspect of lentils’ inﬂuence is needed, rotating spices is not what you 
want. Rather, select spices to focus the meal (e.g., lentils with parsley and orange zest for relax-
ing the liver, or cumin to connect kidneys and heart by way of liver between them). If that is 
the intention, repeat the dish without varying (rotate other foods, of course) in order to elicit 
the desired internal response. 
Te rule of thumb is to repeat focused healing meals three to seven days in a row for a new 
(acute) condition or two to six weeks for long-standing (chronic) conditions. With this guide-
line, you will be able to use your foods for speciﬁc therapeutic success, or, if rotating in normal 
fashion, for general health support.

<!-- p.109 -->
eight
A Sampling of Main Dishes
“After eating a meat dish, your breath should smell mostly of rice.” 
—Confucius (551-479 BCE) 
Te idea of a main dish (“plat principal” in the French culinary tradition) 
can be misleading. Whether we ﬁnd it on the ﬁrst or second page of a menu, 
everything we eat must be digested and everything we eat exerts its inﬂu-
ence within us. 
Imagine this vegetarian meal: steamed Bhutanese red rice, baby chickpeas 
cooked with spices, oven-roasted carrots with dill, and a green such as kale, 
broccoli, bok choy, or chard steamed and dressed with olive oil and a pinch 
of ﬁne salt. In this example, there is no “main”; everything works together 
with equal importance. Tis is a model for meals that include meat or ﬁsh 
as well. Everything works together, nothing is main or side. Yet, for ease of 
understanding, let’s consider a sampling of conventional main dishes: ﬁsh, 
meat, poultry, and tofu. But remember, when it comes to portion size and 
meal balance, even Confucius took time from his day to remind us not to 
overeat these “mains”.  Never eat without thoughtfulness. Meals don’t bal-
ance themselves.

<!-- p.110 -->
104       A Sampling of Main Dishes
Pan-Seared Salmon
Salmon is rich in healthy ﬁsh oils that help balance the omega oil ratio that 
is often out of whack due to poor diet, especially when eating too much com-
mercially fried food. Salmon also helps build blood and if skillfully cooked, 
helps soothe frayed nerves. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 5 minutes
Total time: 15 minutes
Ingredients
salmon | four 4- to 6-ounce fillets, with or without skin
cornmeal (or other ﬂour) | enough to dust, 1/2 cup is more than enough
olive oil | 2 Tbsp
butter | 2 Tbsp
salt & pepper | to taste
Directions
Purchase ﬁsh precut into 4- to 6-ounce ﬁllets, or portion it with a very sharp knife at home. 
Salmon is a high-fat ﬁsh; portions can be smaller than the 6-8 ounces common for lighter ﬁsh. 
Wash the ﬁllets and pat them dry with absorbent paper or clean kitchen towel. While moist 
but not wet, touch the skin side only to the cornmeal that has been seasoned with salt and 
pepper (other spices can be added as well). 
Add the olive oil into a hot, heavy pan. Add the butter (the oil tempers the butter so neither 
will burn), then quickly add the ﬁsh, skin side down. Do not crowd the pan. Cook hot for 3 
minutes. 
At this point, you can carefully ﬂip the ﬁllets over, lower heat by half, and cook the tender side 
another 3-4 minutes for medium (slightly pink center), or 5 minutes to fully cook through, 
depending on the thickness of the ﬁsh. Lightly prod the thickest point to feel how it yields, to

<!-- p.111 -->
Pan-Seared Salmon       105
get a sense for how the center is doing. 
Or, you can cook as restaurants do: once the sear is on the skin-side of the ﬁsh, do not ﬂip. 
Instead, using a pan that can be placed in the oven, move the entire pan from the stovetop to 
a preheated 400˚F oven and cook for 5 minutes. (At home, always be very careful with pans 
that have been in the oven. In my kitchen I leave a towel over any handle that has come from 
the oven to signal that it may be dangerously hot.) 
Good quality salmon likes to be left medium rare in the center, but certainly feel free to cook 
to your own preference. 
A bit of French technique: as you are searing the salmon, skin-side down, use a tablespoon to 
ladle some of the hot cooking oil over the top of the ﬁllets. Tip the pan slightly to gather the 
oils as you do this. You will be rewarded with delicious ﬁnishing of top and bottom of the ﬁsh. 
Tis gives you manual control of the bubbling oil to ﬁne-tune which parts of the ﬁsh you’d 
like to add more sizzle to (in our family we call this “hand-cooking” because you have manual 
control).  Finish the cooking in a hot oven as described just above.  
A Note on the Food Energetics
Salmon is rich in healthy ﬁsh oils. Tese oils help correct the imbalance of omega-3 and ome-
ga-6 common from overconsumption of fried foods cooked in commercial oil. Fish oils are a 
good choice for nerve health as well; healthy nerves are coated in a fatty sheath necessary for 
their proper function. Salmon is especially good at calming nerves and emotions. 
In general, ﬁsh is associated with lung qi functions, but salmon connects more with the liver, 
kidneys, and digestive functions of pancreas-spleen. Use salmon to nourish blood that the liver 
can store. Te neurological connection just mentioned also relates to the liver, and perhaps for 
that reason, it is very important how salmon is cooked. 
Pan-frying is the cooking method that connects energetically with the liver. Amplifying the 
liver connection inherent for salmon with a liver-stimulating cooking method must be done 
with care. If salmon is cooked too long or too hard, the liver can be overstimulated, causing 
diﬃculty sleeping or emotional agitation. If that is a speciﬁc personal concern, it is better to

<!-- p.112 -->
106         A Sampling of Main Dishes
eat salmon poached (see Poached Salmon recipe of page 108). Te cook’s job is to manage this. 
Consider searing only one side, then ﬁnish the cooking in the oven, being careful to leave the 
center slightly soft and bright, in other words, medium rare. More than merely trendy, this 
manages the energetics of salmon very well for most people. 
Salmon is available wild or farmed. Wild ﬁsh has more nature in it, of course, and farmed ﬁsh 
has many problems of quality and ecology. But farmed ﬁsh has the potential to solve problems 
of badly depleted ocean populations. It is a diﬃcult question (vegetarianism, of course, is 
always an option). I believe the answer lies both in more protection for wild ﬁsh and dramat-
ically raising the quality of ﬁsh farming (I do not support genetically modiﬁed ﬁsh farms, as 
it is not possible to contain GMO ﬁsh from escaping and breeding in open waters). It’s a per-
sonal choice with public consequences, but given the large (and increasing) price diﬀerential 
between expensive wild ﬁsh and inexpensive farmed ﬁsh, there seems to be plenty of room for 
the gigantic ﬁsh-farming industry to meet the highest possible standards and still ﬁnd proﬁt.

<!-- p.113 -->
Poached Salmon      107
Poached Salmon
Poaching—cooking in a gently simmering bath of ﬂavored water—protects 
digestion and the fundamentals of metabolism. It’s the best method for bal-
ancing two potentially conﬂicting qualities of salmon: the way it helps calm 
the nervous system and its uplifting nature (think of how salmon so vigorous-
ly leap upstream to spawn).
Serves: four to six
Prep time: 10 minutes
Total time: 25-30 minutes
Ingredients
salmon ﬁllets | 1.5-2 pounds, cut into 4-6 portions
dry white wine (or sake) | 1/2 cup
water | 1/2 cup
butter (or olive oil) | 2 Tbsp
carrot | 1 medium, trimmed and cut into 5-6 pieces 
celery | 1 stalk, chopped similarly to the carrot
leek | 1, white part only, cut to match carrot
bay leaf  | 1
star anise | 1-2 stars
peppercorns | 1/8 tsp
salt | to taste (optional)
parsley, cilantro or dill | half a handful, chopped, to finish
Directions
To begin, bring the water and wine to a gentle boil in a high-sided skillet. Add the carrot, 
celery, leek, bay leaf, peppercorns, star anise and a pinch of salt. 
After 5 minutes, gently lower the salmon portions into the poaching liquid, along with the

<!-- p.114 -->
108       A Sampling of Main Dishes
butter or olive oil. As soon as the liquid returns to a boil, reduce heat to a simmer. Cover, 
cooking until salmon ﬂesh is not quite fully ﬁrm, about 10-15 minutes (depending on size of 
the cut). 
Remove gently with a slotted spatula so the ﬁsh pieces stay intact.
Serve poached salmon with chopped fresh herbs and a drizzle of olive oil, or, if you enjoy 
sauces, add a spoon or two of this dill beurre blanc, a standard sauce from the French kitchen 
tradition: 
In a small saucepan, add 2 minced shallots, 1/4 cup dry white wine, 1/4 
white wine vinegar. Reduce over medium heat until nearly dry. Ten, add 
quite a lot of good quality unsalted butter, 2 Tbsp at a time. Whisk the ﬁrst 
batch into the shallot reduction until nearly melted, then add an additional 
2 Tbsp. Repeat until the sauce is creamy and fragrant. (You will use 10-12 
Tbsp butter for this sauce.)  Add 2 tsp chopped fresh dill, whisk. Taste for 
salt, add if needed. Spoon a bit of beurre blanc over each piece of poached 
ﬁsh. Finish with a few fronds of fresh dill. Te sauce contains a lot of butter, 
but only a spoon or so of the beurre blanc is used per portion.
A Note on the Food Energetics
Salmon is a versatile and popular ﬁsh, and for this reason, it is often incorrectly used as a 
default food. “I can’t decide, I’m not that hungry, I guess I’ll just have the salmon.”  (Or the 
chicken.)  Eating with indecision disrespects the animal who presumably had other plans; it 
also underestimates the strong energetics of foods like salmon or chicken. 
As mentioned in the notes to Pan-Seared Salmon recipe (page 105), salmon resonates with the 
energetics of the liver, kidneys and pancreas-spleen. Poaching—cooking in a gently simmering 
bath of ﬂavored water—is a cooking method that protects and supports pancreas-spleen (and 
therefore the fundamentals of metabolism). Poaching (instead of pan-frying) protects the liver 
from overstimulation. Use poached salmon to calm nerves, provide soothing ﬁsh oils for the 
nervous system’s structures (all nerves are coated in fat, similar to insulation around electrical 
wires, in a sense).

<!-- p.115 -->
Poached Salmon       109
Further, the red quality of salmon must be considered. Feed it to children and you will see—
they become excited, running around far past bedtime. Salmon is uplifting (think of how they 
leap upstream!)  Use it to soothe nerves and lift from mild depression. Select cooking method 
(including sauces and garnishes) to ﬁne-tune the way the food is received. 
Poach in plain water for the easiest and most soothing option. Just water, no wine, vinegar 
or spices. Bring the water to a boil, lower in a portion of salmon, turn heat oﬀ, cover, remove 
gently in 15-20 minutes. Easy and perfect. Serve with parsley and a splash of good olive oil. 
(For best results remove ﬁsh from refrigeration long enough before cooking to be at room 
temperature, or nearly so.)
For a large gathering, I sometimes poach an entire side of salmon—a tribute to the cooking 
my mother taught me to do. I’ll add more aromatics than she would, using the court-bouillon 
method described above (court-bouillon is the use of water, white wine, salt, carrot, leek, celery 
and spices), but the spirit is the same. Adjust amounts of the liquids to cover the ﬁsh complete-
ly. To accommodate the size, I use a baking dish in the oven. 
When poaching in the oven, start by heating plenty of court-bouillon in a large saucepan on 
the stove. Place the uncooked ﬁsh in the baking dish (directly into the pan or on a bed of 
sliced celery and carrots). Cover the ﬁsh with the hot poaching liquid, generously add fresh 
herbs, fashion aluminum foil into a lid, and cook covered in a 325˚F oven until nearly ﬁrm, 
around 20-30 minutes. A discreet probe is acceptable to check for ﬂakiness with a slightly pink 
center. Remember, the ﬁsh will continue to coast after coming from the oven; be careful not 
to overcook.

<!-- p.116 -->
110         A Sampling of Main Dishes
Fish with Ginger and Scallion
Simple and elegant, this is one of my favorite ways to eat ﬁsh. Ginger with 
scallion is a classic ﬂavor combination that functions to warm and invigorate 
digestion. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 15 minutes
Total time: 30 minutes
Ingredients
ﬁsh ﬁllets | 4 portion-size fillets, firm white fish (sea bass or similar)
sake or dry white wine | 3/4 cup (substitute water if you wish)
grapeseed oil (or other quality light oil) | 1 Tbsp
fresh ginger | 3-6 slices, slivered
scallions | 3-5, coarsely chopped
mirin | 1 Tbsp (or 1/2 tsp sweetener)
tamari | 1 tsp
cilantro | handful (half a bunch), coarsely chopped
toasted sesame oil | a few drops (1/2 tsp)
salt and fresh pepper | to taste
Directions
Season ﬁsh on both sides with pinch of salt and few turns of a pepper grinder. If skin is on the 
ﬁllets, use a sharp knife to score an X pattern in the skin to prevent ﬁllets curling as they cook. 
Add the oil to a large, hot pan. Add the ginger and scallions. Add the ﬁsh to the pan. Working 
quickly, add the sake, tamari and mirin. 
Cover, and allow to steam on medium-high heat for 8-10 minutes. 
In the last minute of cooking, add the chopped cilantro and the toasted sesame oil.

<!-- p.117 -->
Fish with Ginger and Scallion         111
Serve when ﬁsh is just barely done, with grain and greens for a complete meal. Spoon the 
cooking liquid over the ﬁsh and rice on the plate. 
Tis is an exquisite and simple dish; the art is in the timing and the balance of ﬂavors. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Steaming is the method associated with stomach and pancreas-spleen. In other words, steam-
ing is the cooking method that most supports digestion itself. Use of a bamboo or metal 
steamer is nice but not necessary. In this home cooking style, the ﬁsh is placed directly into the 
steaming liquid. If you wish to raise the ﬁsh away from direct heat, use sliced carrots or mush-
rooms beneath the ﬁsh (include them when serving) or cabbage leaves (include or discard). If 
you decide to use a steaming basket, make the sauce in a separate pot and spoon liberally over 
the ﬁsh when serving. 
Steamed ﬁsh with vegetables is one of the best recommendations for people with digestive 
woes. It’s easy to digest and easy to make. Tis dish (adapted from my visits to great eateries in 
Taipei) isn’t just a very healthful dish, it’s also one of my favorites.

<!-- p.118 -->
112         A Sampling of Main Dishes
Quick Salt Broiled Fish 
High heat balances the cooling energetic of ﬁsh. Te short duration ensures 
that the ﬁsh is not overcooked (and that dinner is quick and easy for the 
cook). 
Serves: four
Prep time: 12 minutes
Total time: 15-20 minutes
Ingredients
ﬁsh ﬁllet with skin (mackerel, trout, etc.) | 1 fillet per person 
sea salt | enough to cover, then rinse off
seed oil | enough to just coat the fish
Directions
Shopping Note: Tis dish is best with ﬁrm medium-small ﬁsh such as mack-
erel or trout. Mackerel is richer in oils and perfect for this recipe, but if too 
strong, try it with trout or other more delicate-tasting ﬁsh. 
Sprinkle both sides of each ﬁsh ﬁllet with salt and let sit for about 10 minutes, while you pre-
pare the vegetables. (Start your grain just before beginning to prepare the ﬁsh.)
After 10 minutes, rinse oﬀ the salt with running water. Pat the ﬁsh dry with paper towel or 
tea towel. 
Brush the ﬁsh lightly with oil, place skin side down on a baking tray lined with parchment 
paper. Place under high-heat broiler for 2 minutes. (Parchment paper is made for oven use and 
will not burn unless large amounts are left hanging over the sides of the tray while in the oven.) 
Carefully ﬂip the ﬁsh with a thin spatula. Cook the skin side under broiler for 2 minutes, until 
the skin bubbles with a hint of crunchiness. Te cooking is done in under 5 minutes. 
Serve with a grain and vegetables (such as steamed carrots and kale dressed with toasted sesame 
oil and gomasio (see recipe for Gomasio on page 41).

<!-- p.119 -->
Quick Salt Broiled Fish         113
A Note on the Food Energetics
Cooking is a process of transformation, and each method of cooking transforms the raw ingre-
dients in signiﬁcantly diﬀerent ways. 
Here, the choice is to cook a thin, ﬁne ﬁllet of ﬁsh with a short duration of very high heat. Te 
intense heat balances the cooling energetic of the ﬁsh, while the short duration ensures that 
the ﬁsh will not be overcooked. Dry heat such as broiling is appropriate for oilier ﬁsh; ﬁsh that 
tends toward becoming dry is more suited to steaming or braising.
Te quick salting helps the ﬁsh consolidate—thus connecting with our kidney qi, as well as 
helping the ﬁsh hold together through cooking. Remember that the salt is rinsed oﬀ before 
cooking, and that mackerel are full of healthy omega-3 oils (and are abundant in today’s over-
ﬁshed oceans). 
Quick salt broiling is one of the simplest ways to cook fresh ﬁsh. Although perfection may be 
elusive, to make this dish nearly perfect is not hard at all. So often, the simplest preparation is 
the most wonderful.

<!-- p.120 -->
114         A Sampling of Main Dishes
Braised Fish with Lima Beans
Fish Bone Broth (page 99) simmered for hours paired with ﬁsh ﬁllets cooked 
for only minutes make a beautiful combination, restaurant cooking at home. 
All elements of the dish work together: the bone broth supports deep con-
stitutional health, the ﬁsh and lima beans connect to the middle level of 
nourishment, and the spices add lift to support the immune or upper level of 
good health. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 20-30 minutes
Total time: 45 minutes
Ingredients
halibut or other white ﬁsh | 4 servings, about 6 ounces each
ﬁsh broth (see Fish Bone Broth recipe on page 99) | 4-6 cups
leek | 1 medium
baby green lima beans (frozen is ﬁne) | about 24 beans
carrot | 1 medium, cut into short sticks 
parsley | 12 sprigs
olive oil | 1 Tbsp for cooking, additional splash in each bowl
select seed spices (fennel seed, coriander seed, cardamom pod) | 1 tsp each
sea salt | to taste
Directions
Begin by heating the ﬁsh broth in a pot at the back of the stove. 
Braising can be done in the oven, but here directions are for stovetop braising. Working on the 
stove is simpler for people with busy modern lives and it allows the cook to monitor cooking 
more precisely. 
Wash and trim the leek. It is the pale white portion of the leek that is used; this is achieved

<!-- p.121 -->
Braised Fish with Lima Beans        115
on the farm by mounding earth around the growing leeks to shield the lower part of the stalk 
from sunlight thereby keeping it pale and tender. Leeks traditionally require careful cleaning 
to remove soil that seems to permeate through. Recently, leeks have been requiring very little 
cleaning—their whites are sun-blocked in another way. Check your leeks and be ready to wash 
them thoroughly, old-school. Either way, trim oﬀ and discard the small root section and cut 
oﬀ the tough green upper portion. Slice what remains lengthwise in half, rinse, then cut half-
moons across the leek, fairly thinly. Add the leek slices to a heated, large pan with olive oil and 
pinch of sea salt. Cook over medium heat until softened, about 5 minutes. 
In a saucepan, cook the lima beans and carrot until just tender, about 8 minutes. (Tere is no 
need to peel good carrots. Simply wash them, trim their tops and tips, cut into sticks, then 
shorten the carrot sticks to about one inch.)  
In a pan with a lid, braise the ﬁsh portions in ﬁsh stock. For braising, liquid should come up 
to half or three-quarters the height of the ﬁsh. Simmer covered until done, about 4-5 minutes. 
Dry roast the seed spices. Tis can be done earlier, or in the oven, if cooktop burners are scarce. 
It’s very simple and won’t take time: add 1 tsp of large seed spices such as fennel, coriander or 
cardamom pods and 1/2 tsp of small seeds such as mustard seed or cumin. Move constantly in 
a hot pan to bring out their oils and aromas, then transfer to a small dish so they don’t burn 
by sitting in the pan. 
Serve this dish in wide ﬂat bowls. Place a piece of ﬁsh at the center, ladle enough stock to rise 
to half the height of the ﬁsh, more than you would add for sauce but less than would make a 
soup. Te amount depends on your serving bowls. With a cooking spoon, place 4-5 green lima 
beans in each bowl around the ﬁsh; do the same with the carrot and leek pieces. Add a pinch 
of seed spices to each bowl and several sprigs of fresh parsley. Salt if needed. Add a splash of 
good quality olive oil on each and serve. 
Complete the meal with another green and grain, or, for a low-carb clear meal, omit the grain 
and provide slightly larger portions of the main dish and green vegetables, perhaps adding 
sautéed mushrooms to the meal. 
Options: Nearly any ﬁsh works well for this approach, including salmon, monkﬁsh, sea bass,

<!-- p.122 -->
116         A Sampling of Main Dishes
grouper, black cod, or any ﬁsh that is not too thin. A cook may choose to sear the ﬁsh on the 
skin side then braise with the skin side up (uncovered to preserve the crunchiness). Serve the 
same way as before, crunchy skin above the broth. 
Other vegetable and spice combinations are available to the cook. Match what looks good 
in the market with the energetics needed at the time: snow peas, spinach, string beans, Napa 
cabbage, baby bok choy, and so forth. For a more Asian variation, add wakame seaweed and 
use toasted sesame oil in place of olive. Root vegetables beyond leek and carrot can be used. 
Te principle is not to overcrowd the dish, to allow the broth to be clear and the balance of 
elements unobstructed. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Tis dish pairs Fish Bone Broth simmered for hours with ﬁsh ﬁllet cooked for only minutes, 
connecting the constitutional level identiﬁed by the broth with the middle nutritive level of 
the ﬁsh. Greens further support the middle nutritive level, while seed spices connect the con-
stitutional to the surface level that governs immunity and protection. Meanwhile, any broth 
dish is supportive for digestion and hydration. 
Play with the ingredients to make it your own, but be mindful of your tendencies. If you don’t 
like parsley, replace it with something else that is uplifting and opening, perhaps cilantro or 
roasted coriander seeds (cilantro is coriander leaf). It’s diﬃcult to admit the ways our own 
eating cements our problems into place, but it is also a great opportunity. Look into your diet 
(and your cooking) and ask yourself: Do I favor heavy foods and tastes?  Do I avoid bright and 
light tastes, such as lemon, parsley, cilantro, mint, rosemary?  Do I perhaps avoid the spices 
that help emotions move, such as cumin, saﬀron, vanilla?  Or am I on the other end of the 
spectrum, avoiding grounding foods such as grains, animal foods, beans, nuts, bitter vegeta-
bles, balsamic vinegar or dried fruit ﬂavors?  Or perhaps I use grounding foods to subdue my 
emotions, seeking out breads and cakes rich with sugar and dairy?  
Dishes or meals should not be perfectly balanced—each dish and meal should have a person-
ality or tendency. At the same time, we should examine our dietary habits and come to know 
what they mean, how they aﬀect us. Short-term imbalances become features of temperament

<!-- p.123 -->
Braised Fish with Lima Beans        117
and eventually character, if consistently reinforced by dietary habits. 
Our foods should be lively and enjoyable. Daily and seasonal eating should have a rhythm 
to it, or to use a sports analogy, we shouldn’t be calling the same play over and over. Don’t 
be predictable. Personal health is about movement; stagnation can result from habitual taste 
preferences that we follow without understanding.

<!-- p.124 -->
118         A Sampling of Main Dishes
Black Cod with Miso Sauce
Black cod (also known as sable) marinated with miso is a superb-tasting 
dish. Te miso marinade balances the richly nutritive ﬁsh oils in a way that 
represents cooking at its best.
Serves: four
Prep time: 10 minutes
Total time: 1 hour to overnight 
(for marinating)
Ingredients
black cod (or salmon) | four 4- to 6-ounce portions 
sake (rice wine) | 1/2 cup
mirin (sweet rice wine) | 1/2 cup (or just use 1 cup sake)
agave syrup | 1-2 Tbsp
toasted sesame oil | 2 tsp
white or golden miso | 3-4 Tbsp
oil (grapeseed, saﬄower, etc.) | to coat cook surface
Directions
In a saucepan, bring the sake and mirin to a boil then turn oﬀ the heat (if good quality mirin 
is hard to ﬁnd, simply use 1 cup sake or white wine). Mix in the agave syrup and the toasted 
sesame oil. Add the miso, whisk until smooth. Tis is enough marinade for 4-6 servings. 
Marinate modest individual portions of ﬁsh from an hour to overnight. High-oil ﬁsh (like 
black cod or salmon) works best for this preparation; 4- to 6-ounce portions are suﬃcient. 
Cook the ﬁsh with plenty of the marinade on the stovetop in a lidded pan over medium-heat, 
without ﬂipping, until just barely done (time depends on thickness, more than ﬁve minutes 
but less than ten). No extra cooking oil is needed.

<!-- p.125 -->
Black Cod with Miso Sauce        119
Serve with rice or other grain, spooning additional sauce over or around the ﬁsh as desired. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Fish oil has become a popular dietary supplement because it helps restore good balance of 
omega-3 and omega-6 oils. Most people consume too much omega-6, so foods high in ome-
ga-3 are considered very beneﬁcial. Te best way to consume omega-3 is by eating cold-water 
high-fat ﬁsh, such as black cod, salmon, herring, sardines, tuna, trout and mackerel. Te acro-
nym SMASH helps us remember Salmon, Mackerel, Anchovies, Sardines and Herring, or just 
look in the markets for cold-water ﬁsh with good fat content. 
Miso is fermented, helping the digestion of the oils (fermented foods assist the gallbladder in 
its functions, which include handling fatty foods). In this way we are doubly helped: ﬁrst, the 
content of the fatty ﬁsh, and second, assistance in making use of those fats (oils, if you prefer). 
Te miso marinade includes sake or other wine, also fermented. 
Cook’s Note: Don’t use the marinade in the same way as a sauce—it’s a bit 
too strong. Including just what clings to the ﬁsh is suﬃcient for taste. It has 
fulﬁlled its role during marinating and cooking time. Unless, that is, you 
can’t help yourself…. If your ingredients are good and you’ve handled them 
well, the pan sauce may be irresistible.

<!-- p.126 -->
120       A Sampling of Main Dishes
Turmeric and Tamarind Fish
Tis version of a truly ancient dish oﬀers slightly more exotic ingredients for 
special home cooking. Te sauce aims the beneﬁts of the dish in speciﬁc ways, 
each ingredient adding its inﬂuence for health and enjoyment. When shop-
ping for ﬁsh, always select for freshness, but also keep in mind the energetic 
inﬂuences of the diﬀerent types, as described below. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 35 minutes
Total time: 50 minutes 
(including marinating time)
Ingredients
fresh water or ocean ﬁsh | portions for four (more on fish types below)
olive oil | 2 Tbsp
tamarind paste | 2 Tbsp
turmeric powder | 1 tsp
salt | to taste
black pepper | a few fresh grinds
pomegranate syrup (optional) | 1 Tbsp
garlic (optional) | 1 clove, mashed or diced
Directions
Begin by making the marinade. In a bowl, mix the olive oil, tamarind paste, turmeric pow-
der, a generous chef’s pinch of salt and a few grinds of black pepper. Add the garlic clove and 
pomegranate syrup if you choose (this dish is excellent without them, too). Let the marinade 
rest for about ﬁve minutes to develop. Te consistency should be a moist paste.  
Coat your ﬁsh on both sides with the marinade paste and allow to rest for 30 minutes (refrig-
erated if you must). While the ﬁsh is marinating, preheat your oven to 425˚F.

<!-- p.127 -->
Turmeric and Tamarind Fish        121
Place the marinated ﬁsh on a baking sheet lined with oiled aluminum foil or parchment paper. 
Place it in the oven until done, 10-25 minutes depending on the type and size of the ﬁsh. 
Alternatively, place in the preheated oven, skin side up, and broil on high heat for 5 minutes 
to crisp the skin (mind carefully to prevent burning). Flip the ﬁsh and cook on the tender side 
for 2 minutes or until ﬁsh permits just a hint of ﬂaking when probed. 
Serve on a bed of fresh or pan-wilted spinach, or next to steamed grain and steamed broccoli. 
Garnish with chopped parsley and fresh pomegranate seeds, or allow the beautiful golden 
color of the transformed marinade to speak for itself. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Tis recipe is adapted from an ancient dish from Mesopotamia named masgouf, still considered 
the national dish of Iraq. It is a wood-smoked freshwater ﬁsh dish. Most traditionally, river 
ﬁsh are butterﬂied and cooked slowly on sticks surrounding a pit of burning wild brush. (If 
you have a ﬁre cook-pit, the only seasoning needed is salt. Place the salted ﬁsh at the perimeter 
of the ﬁre until nearly done, then touch it directly to the coals when they have burned low 
to crisp the skin.)  As cooking moved indoors, more spices were added to replace the unique 
tastes of open ﬁre cooking. Not surprisingly, each family has its own recipe, and diﬀerences 
are debated with passion. My version is fairly standard, with tamarind, turmeric, garlic, and 
for fun, pomegranate. 
Fish provides high-quality protein, needed by everyone for growth, cell repair, and energy pro-
duction through building hormones and enzymes. But ﬁsh is not protein powder—each type 
of ﬁsh has a character. Seafood provides a dietary link with the sea as the mysterious source of 
life. Tink of ﬁsh as representing a transition stage of evolution, or at the very least, providing 
a link to the base of the food chain. Within that, diﬀerentiations are possible. 
 »  Oily ﬁsh nourish hormones and provide important omega oils and minerals. 
 » White ﬁsh are more cooling, more suitable for pairing with warming herbs. 
 » Bony ﬁsh support yang qi (moving energy). 
 » Fleshy ﬁsh support yin and are less moving in inﬂuence.

<!-- p.128 -->
122        A Sampling of Main Dishes
 »  In terms of the internal organ system and food connections, ﬁsh primarily have a 
lung aﬃnity—their swimming is their breathing. Fish also resonate with kidney, 
liver, and stomach functions. Te energetic connection of ﬁsh with lung qi can be 
further diﬀerentiated:  
 »  Deep sea ﬁsh help anchor deep breathing, very helpful for people with asthma. 
 »  Fresh water ﬁsh don’t swim as deeply as ocean ﬁsh and in diﬀerent quality waters; 
fresh water ﬁsh are more surface ﬁsh. Fresh water ﬁsh help with the energy called 
lung dispersing (they swim-breathe more superﬁcially than what is available to ﬁsh 
in the ocean). 
Tis helps understand how to select ﬁsh. Traditional masgouf is made with bony river ﬁsh 
similar to carp. Fortunately, this recipe is delicious with many types of ﬁsh (carp is not a pop-
ular ﬁsh in Europe or America). Use whole ﬁsh on the bone to be more authentic (and bring 
more yang qi to the table), or ﬂeshy ﬁsh ﬁllets. Trout is a good fresh water choice (as is catﬁsh), 
while red snapper, branzino or sea bass are good ocean choices. Salmon or other oily ﬁsh work 
well here, too (the river ﬁsh of the Tigris are said to be fairly oily, adding to ﬂavor when slow-
cooked around a smoky wood ﬁre). 
Tamarind aids digestion and peristalsis. Turmeric warms the belly, is very good for resolving 
cramping pain and is full of strong antioxidants that scour the blood, clearing free-radicals. 
Pomegranate has an astringent quality (helping loose stool problems or waking to urinate at 
night) and helps with deep hydrating. 
Each aspect contributes something useful; together they make a dish that is as old as civiliza-
tion itself.

<!-- p.129 -->
Fish or Tofu with Fermented Black Beans        123
Fish or Tofu with Fermented Black Beans
With ﬁsh, scallops, or tofu, black bean sauce adds deep ﬂavors and strong 
health support. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 20 minutes 
(including soaking the black beans and dried mushrooms)
Total time: 25 minutes
Ingredients
ﬁsh ﬁllets or tofu |  1.5 pounds fish fillets of your choice, cut into portions  
(or 2 pounds tofu, cut into large cubes)
scallions | 2-3, sliced coarsely
fresh ginger | 3 slices, finely slivered
ﬁve-spice powder | 1/4 tsp
grapeseed oil (or organic peanut oil) | 2 Tbsp
fermented black beans | 2 Tbsp, soaked & rinsed, whole or chopped
dried tree ear mushrooms | 1/4 cup (dry), soaked and chopped coarsely
tamari | 1/4 cup
rice wine | 1 cup
organic corn starch (or kuzu powder) | 1 Tbsp, mixed with cool water
fresh cilantro | a few sprigs per person as garnish
Directions
Soak the dried fermented beans in a bowl of water for 30 minutes or more (this can be done 
well in advance of cooking to save time, even overnight). Soak the tree ear mushrooms in a 
separate bowl. 
Heat a large heavy pan (or wok). Add the grapeseed or other high-heat cooking oil, the ginger 
slivers, the scallions, and the ﬁve-spice powder. Add a pinch of salt (optional). Ten add the

<!-- p.130 -->
124        A Sampling of Main Dishes
soaked fermented black beans (whole, mashed or chopped, as you like, but do not add the 
soaking liquid). Add the tree ear mushrooms, chopped, (without their soaking water) and the 
ﬁsh or tofu. Handle the ﬁsh carefully in the pan or wok to avoid breaking it up. 
After a moment, splash in the tamari and add the cooking wine. As soon as the dish is hot and 
most of the alcohol has evaporated (4-5 minutes), add the dissolved cornstarch (or kuzu) and 
stir gently. Cornstarch thickens when it reaches bubbling temperature. As soon as it does, the 
dish is ready. Adjust with more dissolved cornstarch if thin, or open with a splash of water if 
too thick. Add the cilantro. Serve immediately with steamed rice and green vegetable.
To use scallops in place of ﬁsh or tofu, cook 1.5 pounds scallops in a pan with a splash of oil. 
Turn carefully to put a touch of color on top and bottom, but do not overcook. Set them aside 
on a warm plate, and add the other ingredients to make a black bean sauce. Since you’re not 
braising here, add only 1 Tbsp tamari and 3 Tbsp rice wine. Bring the sauce together with 
the cornstarch or kuzu powder. Plate the scallops, pour the black bean sauce over them. Add 
a bit of the sauce onto the steamed rice and greens, add a few drops toasted sesame oil, some 
chopped cilantro, and serve. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Fermented black beans are the original fermented product of Asian cuisines, the ancestor of 
miso and soy sauce. Tey have been in daily use for over 2,500 years. Black soy beans are fer-
mented with salt to control which microbes will and won’t be involved. Teir taste is salty, a 
bit sweet, and full of complex and pungent ﬂavors that result from the fermentation process. 
(Fermented black beans can be very salty. Soaking them washes oﬀ most of the salt—I always 
taste each batch to know how salty it is, sometimes rinsing multiple times if very salty and 
sometimes adding some of the soaking water into the dish if barely so).
Fermented black beans are also seen as a medicinal herb (called dan dou chi, used to help clear 
common cold that you may catch when run-down and dehydrated). Fermented black beans 
help digestion absorb ﬂuids (a function governed by the stomach) and move them where they 
are needed (to the lungs, lips and skin). Wet dishes like this one help fermented black beans 
fulﬁll their function of nourishing and spreading ﬂuids.

<!-- p.131 -->
Fish or Tofu with Fermented Black Beans       125
Tofu has a cooling energetic—the cook should know this and include something for balance. 
Tofu is often deep-fried to add warmth (yang qi), but I prefer using warming spices, here 
ginger, scallion and a traditional mix called ﬁve-spice powder. Use 1/4 tsp ﬁve-spice powder 
(cinnamon, clove, star anise, fennel and peppercorn) with the ginger and scallion to warm the 
cooling eﬀect of tofu. 
Garlic is often included in black bean sauce dishes, but some care is needed. Fermented black 
beans nourish hydration, harmonize the digestive organs of stomach, pancreas-spleen and 
liver, help calm the spirit and relax chest tightness. Garlic is very useful when a turbo-boost is 
needed, but it is too often overused, exacerbating stomach dryness and emotional irritability. 
(Add one clove garlic—sliced or mashed—if a strong boost is needed to ward oﬀ the beginning 
of a common cold.)
Mushrooms and toasted sesame oil add to the kidney resonance of the salty fermented black 
beans. Serving with steamed rice or millet supports the stomach and pancreas-spleen. Include 
greens (broccoli rabe, kale, chard, bok choy, etc.) to add support for the liver. Te heart and 
lungs, while not the direct focus of this meal, are well included by the way fermented black 
beans (used as a condiment) open and relax the chest, easing our circulation, breathing and 
spirit.

<!-- p.132 -->
126        A Sampling of Main Dishes
Pan-Braised Tofu
High-quality protein in a ﬂavorful sauce. Some people feel an obligation to 
try tofu occasionally, but I absolutely love it. Have it with warming spices to 
balance its cooling energetic. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 20 minutes
Total time: 20 minutes
Ingredients
scallions | 1 bunch, trimmed, sliced coarsely
fresh ginger  | 5 slices, slivered
organic tofu | 1 pound, cut into 1-inch cubes
zucchini | 2 medium zucchini, halved then cut in 1-inch chunks 
organic tamari | splash
mirin | 1 Tbsp (substitute sake, white wine, or rice wine vinegar if desired)
ﬁve-spice powder | 1 tsp
grain of paradise seed | 1 tsp (cracked or whole)
sea salt | chef’s pinch
grapeseed oil | 1 or 2 Tbsp
Directions
Heat a large pan (or wok) quite hot; add the grapeseed oil (“hot wok, cold oil”). Add scallions, 
ginger, pinch salt, ﬁve-spice powder, grain of paradise (or black pepper). Toss around the pan 
to season the oil. 
Add the zucchini and the tofu.
Splash with tamari, splash with mirin, but not so much it cools the cooking. Cover and cook 
until the tofu is just hot and the zucchini is still half-ﬁrm.

<!-- p.133 -->
Pan-Braised Tofu       127
Serve with steamed rice and a cooked green such as kale, broccoli or bok choy as a side dish. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Soy can be diﬃcult to digest for some. Soy has also been suspected of disrupting hormone 
balance, but it is overly processed and hidden soy that has not been skillfully prepared that 
should be of concern. In contrast, making tofu requires soaking soybeans overnight, a pro-
cess similar to sprouting that neutralizes problems and produces time-tested, excellent food. 
Choose organic tofu when possible to avoid pesticides and genetic manipulations. Avoid soy 
isolates and hidden soy in commercial food products. Instead, eat it in honest and traditional 
dishes such as this one.  
In Chinese dietary medicine, tofu is known as a very cooling food, and is often cooked with 
high heat and warming spices to provide balance. Here, the temperature is high, and ﬁve-spice 
powder is used (a mix often containing more than ﬁve ingredients, including cinnamon, cum-
in, star anise, fennel seed and either Szechuan peppercorns, turmeric, cardamom, fenugreek, 
or nutmeg, all warming by nature). 
Seasoned tofu makes a complete meal along with a grain and a green. Improvise combinations, 
adding mushrooms (many types work well, including tree ears), or even crabmeat. I never 
cook this quite the same way twice. A good tofu dish is in our dinner rotation, appearing about 
once each week.

<!-- p.134 -->
128        A Sampling of Main Dishes
Braised Chicken with Soy and Ginger
Tis braised chicken dish supports digestion well. It’s simple for the cook and 
delicious to eat. Chicken can be very warming; braising is a healthier cooking 
method than frying, or, in the case of chicken, even roasting.
Serves: six to eight
Prep time: 15 minutes
Total time: 50 minutes
Ingredients
chicken pieces | 3-4 pounds (thighs, breasts, etc.)
tamari | 1 cup
sake or white wine | 1 cup
barley malt or other sweetener | 1/3 cup
water | 1.5 cups
scallions | 4, cut into 4-6 pieces each
fresh ginger | a chunk the size of your thumb, cut into 5 pieces
star anise | 2-3 pods
allspice | 4-5 whole
peppercorns | up to 1 tsp
organic citrus peel (optional) | 2 strips
Directions
Rinse the chicken parts and pat dry with a paper towel. Brown lightly (with a very small splash 
of cooking oil) in a large cooking pot over medium-high heat. Turn chicken pieces after 2-3 
minutes. After another 2-3 minutes, remove to a plate until later. (Tis step browns the skin 
for ﬂavor and appearance; full cooking will come with the braising.)  
Add the wet ingredients to the pan: the sake, tamari, barley malt, and water. Add the scallions 
and spices, and citrus peel if desired.  Swirl gently to combine ingredients.

<!-- p.135 -->
Braised Chicken with Soy and Ginger       129
Bring the liquids to a boil, then immediately reduce heat to the lowest simmer. Add the 
chicken back into the pan. Spoon the liquid over the chicken occasionally during cooking to 
develop color and ﬂavor. Allow the dish to simmer, partially covered for 30-40 minutes (use 
a lid kept slightly ajar). Setting should be low but with enough heat for occasional bubbling. 
Serve over rice with a generous spoonful of the sauce. Accompany with green vegetables (broc-
coli, kale, bok choy, etc.) and if you have time, a root vegetable such as roast carrots. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Braising is a wet-cooking method where the solid food is covered to half or three-quarters by a 
seasoned cooking liquid. Braising supports digestion (stomach and pancreas-spleen), as do soups 
and porridges. With a soup, it’s the liquid we are aiming at; in pure form we don’t even need to 
eat the vegetables, ﬁsh or meat that formed the broth. Here, we place the chicken over steamed 
rice, ladle some sauce on top, then reserve the leftover sauce in the refrigerator for repeating the 
dish (within a week or two). Traditionally, some families keep a sauce like this going indeﬁnitely, 
recooking and adding liquid as needed (if used in this way, it is called a master sauce). 
Because chicken has aﬃnity with the stomach, braising is a perfect match of cooking method 
with main ingredient. Both the chicken and the braising support digestion and the organs of 
stomach, pancreas-spleen and the small intestine. Chicken can be very warming; by braising, 
you can cook chicken beautifully without adding as much yang qi heat as you would by either 
roasting or frying. 
Turkey cuts, ﬁsh, tofu or vegetables also work very well with this sauce. 
Vary the seasonings to ﬁne-tune the dish for diﬀerent purposes. For example, adding two strips 
of tangerine peel (perhaps each the size of a thumb) will help relax tightness in the chest, relax 
liver, and aid digestion (as well as contributing wonderful citrus ﬂavor notes). 
Sourcing organic products is very important for taste, health, responsible land stewardship and 
humane treatment of farm animals. Chicken and citrus peel are two foods where organic pro-
duction are nearly essential, for diﬀerent reasons. Other common foods where buying organic 
is most important are wheat, corn and soy.

<!-- p.136 -->
130        A Sampling of Main Dishes
Let’s Talk About Red Meat 
To be clear, meat is an option in the human diet, not a necessity. To understand the role animal 
food really plays in our diets it’s useful to think of vegetarianism for a moment. Te question 
for vegetarians is not about getting suﬃcient protein (easy) or suﬃcient micronutrients such as 
B12 (easy unless vegan, then still possible); the question for vegetarians is how to get the yang 
qi energetic that is so abundant in animal food (warming spices are a part of the solution). A 
well-balanced vegetarian diet must not only consider nutrients, it must provide the warming 
and moving yang qi that comes most easily from the energetics of animal food. In other words, 
it’s not just about nutrition needs (which can be met from vegetarian sources), it’s about energy. 
Humans are clearly omnivorous by nature, with tooth and digestive design between those of 
the true carnivores or true herbivores. Terefore, the two health questions for those who do 
eat meat are:
 » How much meat is enough?
 »  Assuming we are not suﬀering from too much meat, what are the energetics and 
functions of eating red meat?
In our culture, the problems people have from meat are overwhelmingly due to eating too 
much of it, often poorly combined with other foods. Tese are problems of excess. To look at 
red meat for its inherent properties, consider having none for extended periods of time then 
trying it again. In this context the true energetics of red meat can be seen, separating its poten-
tial beneﬁts from problems of habitual overconsumption.
Although various good cases can be made for vegetarianism, some meat recipes are included 
here because: 
 » Meat dishes are included in the traditional cuisines of most cultures. 
 » Meat certainly can be part of a healthy diet. 
Tere are of two types of meat recipes here. Some oﬀer ideas to improve assimilation of the 
beneﬁts of meat while others can be used for those who crave a meat ﬁx but aren’t sure how to 
satisfy that craving with their own home cooking.

<!-- p.137 -->
Duck Breast with Balsamic Glaze        131
Duck Breast with Balsamic Glaze
Poultry is an important food category. Chicken is ubiquitous today, turkey, 
too, especially in sandwiches and low-fat burger substitutes. We shouldn’t eat 
so habitually. Duck is rich in taste and surprisingly easy to cook. It nourishes 
the constitutional level of health called kidney-essence, something that neither 
chicken or turkey can do. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 15 minutes
Total time: 30-45 minutes
Ingredients
duck breasts | 4 if small, 2 if large
sea salt | enough to season
for the glaze:  
balsamic vinegar | 3/4 cup
red wine | 1/2 cup
dried prunes or apricots | 3
dried tangerine peel (or orange zest) | 1 medium strip or 1/4 tsp
butter | 1 Tbsp
Directions
Bring the duck breasts out of the refrigerator to come to room temperature before cooking. Long 
Island Ducklings (originally a Chinese breed) are smaller than the Magret duck breasts (a French 
breed). All varieties can be excellent, use what is available and adapt cooking accordingly (the smaller 
types can be prepared whole, while the Magret may be cooked whole or in halves).   
With a sharp chef’s knife, score the fat by making parallel cuts almost to the ﬂesh about 1/2 inch 
spaced, then turning the piece 45 degrees and repeat, producing a cross-cut pattern. Salt the duck gen-
erously on both sides, place fat side down in a heavy pan over medium-high heat.  Do not crowd the 
pieces. Cook until about half the fat has rendered oﬀ and the remaining fat layer has browned nicely.

<!-- p.138 -->
132        A Sampling of Main Dishes
Te duck can be ﬁnished in a 400˚F oven until medium rare (a meat-probe thermometer 
should read 135˚F for medium rare). More often, however, I simply ﬁnish the duck by turning 
it over in the pan and cooking the “naked” side gently with reduced heat. Check for doneness 
by prodding with a ﬁnger or cooking tool; with some experience the perfect balance of pliant 
and ﬁrm becomes familiar. If not certain, cut a discreet probe with a sharp knife into a bit of 
one of the larger pieces. 
As with all meats, remove from the pan to allow the pieces to rest. Expect some cooking to 
continue from internal heat as the juices redistribute. After 5-10 minutes, use a very sharp 
knife to cut serving slices (or serve whole portions to be cut by the diners, steak-style). 
A good sauce simultaneously enhances the ﬂavors and aids digestion of the dish. Tis one, 
adapted from a chef in Modena, meets both criteria beautifully.  
Add the balsamic and wine to a saucepan, bring to a boil, reduce to simmer. Cut the prunes 
into small bits and add to the pan. Add the citrus peel (ﬁnely chopped, or a single piece to 
be removed before serving). Reduce to a syrup consistency (timing depends upon heat—be 
careful to avoid burning!)  Just before serving, whisk in the butter (optional). 
Serve the duck with green vegetables and a choice of carbs (rice, millet, mashed parsnip and celery 
root, etc.)  Spoon a taste of the balsamic reduction over and around the duck. If needed, increase 
the greens and omit the carbs for a clear meal that avoids mixing protein and carbs together.
A Note on the Food Energetics
We all have our prejudices, and one of mine is that people are eating too much chicken and 
not enough duck. Only a few generations ago, chicken was an expensive treat to be eaten for 
deep nourishment, savoring the golden fat that showed it was a “good bird”. Chicken was 
eaten with a special respect that now is reserved for duck. 
All poultry share some food energetic qualities. Tey all lay eggs and either ﬂy well or aspire 
to the air. Chicken boosts qi, connects to the stomach and pancreas-spleen and supports im-
munity (as soup) by bringing ﬂuid and the qi boost together. Chickens don’t ﬂy well; their 
wild ancestors were forest birds in India, similar to quail—ﬂying was limited to reaching low

<!-- p.139 -->
Duck Breast with Balsamic Glaze        133
branches in trees for perching. Tey are said to aspire to ﬂight more than to ﬂy outright, a 
quality that imparts an ascending directional inﬂuence to us. 
Duck is somewhat diﬀerent. Tey are water birds, and very strong ﬂyers, capable of migrating 
long distances with the seasons. Dietarily, chickens are more for the middle level (stomach, pan-
creas-spleen) while duck strongly resonates with the lower level (kidney, easy to remember be-
cause ducks swim in lakes, water connection), the upper level (lungs, easy to remember because 
ducks ﬂy high in the air), as well as the middle (pancreas-spleen, but in the source texts, not 
including stomach as chicken does). Duck is warming, as is naturally raised chicken. (Commer-
cial chickens raised with medicated feed laden with antibiotics have a distinctly cooler energetic.) 
Tis is a strong dose of dietary theory. To be simple, think of duck as nearly a red meat, deeply 
nourishing. Its signature fat layer is supportive of healthy hormone balance. Dietary fat was 
demonized as a cause of obesity and heart disease, a message that was partly too simple and 
partly mistaken. Obesity is caused by metabolic hormones forcing the storage of excess dietary 
sugar into our own fat cells (sugar in the blood is the problem, not fat). Blockage of the arter-
ies is now believed to be caused by internal inﬂammation that damages the internal walls of 
our blood vessels. Tis inﬂammatory damage stimulates layering of insulating deposits that 
attempt repair. It is these attempts at insulating damaged blood vessel linings that can eventu-
ally do great damage. A diet low in sugar and ample in good quality fats is a safe plan for most 
people. Duck fat is a high-quality dietary fat. It is very stable, meaning it can support hormone 
production without oxidizing into free radicals that incite the inﬂammatory response. 
Duck can be in your regular food rotation. Save the liquid duck fat from the pan for other 
cooking (herb roasted potatoes, for example). Duck is comforting and uplifting, passing to us 
their own comfort in the water and the air. 
As mentioned, a good sauce is not only tasty, a good sauce brings a tool kit to help digest the 
dish. While it’s true that the duck’s fat can be an important health support (especially for those 
who have hormonal balance issues partly due to low-fat and altered-fat foods depriving the 
body of resource material for building hormones), it’s also true that duck is a fatty meat, chal-
lenging for some people to digest easily. To resolve potential problems, serve with a well-con-
sidered sauce.

<!-- p.140 -->
134        A Sampling of Main Dishes
 »  Orange peel is a traditional aid for fatty meats, but avoid the sugar of orange juice 
and use only the peel. Te sweetness of orange juice would slow digestion, while the 
bracing aromatic of the peel will help digest the fats properly. 
 »  Some sweetness comes with the addition of a stone fruit, such as apricot or prune. 
Tese are very yang fruits, allowing an exception to the rule of not combining 
sweets with meats. Te yang energy of these fruits boosts digestion, good for fatty 
meats (including beef, lamb, or pork). Use in small amounts to receive their beneﬁt 
without the problem of too much sweetness. 
 » In Chinese restaurants today, “duck sauce” is a neon-colored sugar sauce that should 
be avoided completely. But as with so much in food history, there was an original 
duck sauce that made perfect sense, mixing apricot (a yang pit fruit) with vinegar. 
It’s a mix of sweet and sour, eﬀective for aiding digestion of duck or other fatty 
meats. Vinegar is fermented and sour, strongly helping the gallbladder, the organ 
that stores bile (from the liver) that is needed to digest dietary fats. In this recipe the 
vinegar is balsamic, bringing a note of fruit and wine to the sauce (the original duck 
sauce was made with rice vinegar). Use real balsamic vinegar, but not an expensive 
one. Te chef in Modena who taught me this recipe said that an honest, six-year-old 
balsamic is best for cooking.
 » Lastly, adding red wine gives body to the sauce and softens the bite of the vinegar 
(from a cook’s point of view). Energetically, red wine connects to the blood level. 
Duck does not build blood as well as either chicken or beef without this added 
help (but it does build hormones and what is called kidney essence more than other 
meats).

<!-- p.141 -->
Be Bim Bop        135
Be Bim Bop
Beef is a strong food, invigorating and grounding at once. Balance is key, as 
too much can lead to internal heat, irritation, high blood pressure and poor 
digestion. But in this adaptation of a Korean daily dish with spices, kitchen 
herbs and directional vegetables, beef can be a strong support for vigorous 
health. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 20 minutes
Total time: 30 minutes
Ingredients
tender beef (sirloin, etc.) | 1.5 pounds (can buy already sliced or slice at home)
scallions | 3, trimmed and cut into chunks
fresh ginger | 4-6 thin slices, then slivered
garlic (optional) | 1 clove, sliced or mashed
tamari | 5-6 Tbsp
mirin (or cooking wine substitute) | 2 Tbsp
Shanxi vinegar (or rice wine vinegar) | 1 Tbsp
sweetener (agave or honey) | 1 Tbsp
toasted sesame oil | 1 Tbsp
sesame seeds | 1 Tbsp
carrots | 1 cup, julienned (slivered), about 1-2 carrots
mung bean sprouts | 1.5 cups (when raw)
cilantro (or parsley) | 1/4 cup (about one handful)
steamed white rice |  1 cup measured raw is enough for four servings, make a bit more 
to be generous

<!-- p.142 -->
136        A Sampling of Main Dishes
Directions
First, make the marinade: chop the scallions (whites and greens), peel enough fresh ginger for 
4-6 thin slices, then cut and sliver to make julienned ginger. Put the ginger and scallions into a 
medium-large mixing bowl. Add the tamari, mirin, vinegar, agave (or honey, don’t worry, it’s a 
small amount used for harmonizing ﬂavors), the toasted sesame oil, and the sesame seeds (you 
can crush the seeds with mortar and pestle if desired). Add the crushed garlic if you decide to 
include it (see below). Mix together. 
Slice the beef across the grain as thinly as you can (stores freeze meat ﬁrst, then use machines 
to make extremely thin slices—at home reasonably thin will do). Add the sliced beef to the 
marinade, mix thoroughly. 
While the beef is marinating, steam the rice. Put 1 cup raw rice in a pot with 1.5 cups water 
(or 1.75 cups water if you prefer it moist). Bring to boil, reduce heat immediately to simmer, 
cover with tight lid. Do not uncover and do not stir. After 20 minutes, turn oﬀ heat, but still 
do not open the lid. Best grain cooking allows 5-10 minutes resting time. 
While the rice is cooking, slice the carrots and soften them by cooking for 5 minutes in a pan 
with just enough water to make steam (about 1/4 inch, less if you’re conﬁdent of not letting 
them burn). In another pot, wilt the bean sprouts over medium heat with a splash of water and 
a lid (or a splash of oil if you prefer). Remove carrots and bean sprouts to separate bowls after 
only a few minutes, keep them warm with lids. 
Heat a large heavy pan on the stovetop. Once very hot, add all the meat and marinade. Cook 
as hot and fast as you can, stirring to cook evenly. It will seem dry at ﬁrst, but the meat will 
soon give oﬀ quite a bit of juice. At that point, bring to the table. 
Be Bim Bop means something like mixed-up rice in Korean. Te way to eat this dish is to place 
a good scoop of rice in the middle of a dinner bowl and place a scoop of meat with plenty of 
juice over the rice. Add carrots, bean sprouts, cilantro and more sesame seeds on top of that, 
and then mix it together in your bowl. Te aroma is compelling, and the ﬂavor is too. 
Serve with steamed greens (kale, spinach, broccoli, bok choy, etc.) for a complete lunch or dinner.

<!-- p.143 -->
Be Bim Bop        137
A Note on the Food Energetics
Beef is useful to build blood and support muscle strength. Because it is a strong food, balance 
is key. Eating too much can lead to poor digestion, internal heat, irritation and high blood 
pressure. But incorporating some beef into our diet can help to ground and invigorate us. As 
a side note, vegetarians must ﬁnd a way to build blood and invigorate qi (the use of warming 
spices with suﬃcient vegetable protein is key). 
Te name of this dish, Be Bim Bop, tells us that rice is the principal ingredient, not meat. Tat 
helps us know balance. Te carrots are grounding and the bean sprouts ensure that the beef 
doesn’t overheat us (bean sprouts are cooling, or if you prefer, anti-inﬂammatory). I often hear 
people who eat large amounts of red meat say they are adding “anti-inﬂammatory superfoods” 
to their diet. An easier way to reduce inﬂammation due to diet is to eat relatively less meat and 
to have dramatically more vegetables (of all types). Eating a meat-focused diet while adding 
token anti-oxidants is not a winning strategy. Tis is a rice dish with added meat and vegeta-
bles: a good balance. 
Te spices in this recipe help digest the meal they accompany: scallions prevent stagnation, 
ginger warms digestion, and garlic (if used) provides an overall ascending boost. Garlic, like 
hot peppers and onions, is often overused. Too much raises heat and dries internal ﬂuids. In 
my cooking, I decide about garlic not according to a recipe or merely for its ﬂavor, but accord-
ing to the needs of the people who will eat it. 
Speaking of onion, there is a tradition in Chinese medical cooking to avoid bulb onion when 
eating beef. Sun Si Miao, a great master (and physician to two emperors of China) counseled 
us in his writings from the seventh century CE to avoid combining beef and onions. He did 
not explain his reasoning, however, merely saying that they don’t digest well together, and 
therefore problems will arise over time. Although his reasons remain obscure, he himself lived 
to 101 years of age (1,250 years before the discovery of antibiotics). Be Bim Bop is commonly 
cooked with onions; I leave them out and use the milder scallions (scallions paired with beef 
are okay with Sun Si Miao).

<!-- p.144 -->
138        A Sampling of Main Dishes
Ground Meat Sautéed with Herbs 
(three interchangeable recipes)
Across the world, most meat is consumed not as premium steaks but in dishes 
featuring ground beef, lamb or pork. Ground (or chopped) meat is economi-
cal and more suited for sharing. More than mere economy, ground meat can 
have spices cooked throughout to aid digestion and assimilation. Tese recipes 
are very ﬂexible—swap the types of meat, spices, herbs and condiments.   
Serves: four
Prep time: 5 minutes
Total time: 15 minutes
Ingredients
Common Ingredients: 
 
ground beef, lamb or pork | 1 pound
 
olive oil | 1 Tbsp
Option Set One: 
 
scallions | 3-5, chopped
 
fresh ginger (optional) | 2 slices, slivered
 
garlic (optional) | 1 clove, mashed
 
sumac (or paprika) | 1.5 tsp
 
cumin | 1 tsp
 
cinnamon | 2/3 tsp
 
dried prunes | 8, chopped
 
sea salt | about 1 tsp
 
cilantro | half a handful, chopped
 
parsley | half a handful, chopped

<!-- p.145 -->
Ground Meat Three Styles        139
Option Set Two: 
 
scallions | 3-5
 
rosemary | 1 tsp
 
thyme | 1 tsp
 
oregano | 1 tsp
 
sea salt | 1/2 tsp
 
Option Set Tree: 
 
scallions | 3-5
 
balsamic vinegar | 2 Tbsp
 
tamarind paste | 1 Tbsp
 
tamari | 1 Tbsp
 
cilantro | 1/4 cup, chopped
 
parsley | 1/4 cup, chopped
Directions
Add the splash of olive oil to a hot pan, the chopped scallions and then the ground meat you 
have selected (meats can be combined, for example, 2/3 lamb with 1/3 beef). If cooking with 
more aromatic spices (the cumin, cinnamon, etc.), add them to the oil to “wake” before adding 
the ground meat. 
Tis is very forgiving cooking. Add the ingredients to the pan, stir often enough, add liquid if 
it seems too dry, turn heat higher if it seems too wet. Pork should be cooked through, but beef 
or lamb can be served fully browned or less well-done. Add other spices or liquids. Follow two 
guidelines for success with sautéed ground meat cooking:
 » Use good quality ingredients. Home meals can easily be better than restaurant food 
simply by using better quality ingredients (and still be less expensive).
 » Focus whatever approach you use (avoid too many spices or ﬂavorings at once).

<!-- p.146 -->
140       A Sampling of Main Dishes
In other words, cook with Option Set One this week, Option Set Two next week, 
then Option Set Tree, after that conventional burgers, after that Chinese, Indian, 
Turkish, or Mexican style, then explore Texas chili, and so forth. Te kitchen should 
always be a creative place, but stay focused in your creations. Ground meat dishes 
have been known to include “sloppy” in their names, but the mind-set behind all 
variations should be clear. 
Serve with cooked vegetables, a green salad if weather is hot and health strong, and a grain. 
Make hamburgers if you like, but sautéed ground meat dishes are well paired with rice, buck-
wheat, bulgur, couscous, millet and so forth.  Cooked grains can be sautéed directly with the 
meat and spices if desired, especially when reheating leftovers. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Before food science began ﬂooding the media, the most common health advice in any family 
was,  “Make sure you chew your food well—don’t wolf your food!”  Indeed, good digestion 
is directly stimulated by the chewing process via acupuncture points in the jaw that lie on the 
stomach meridian. Crunchy foods make good appetizers for this reason. On the other hand, 
habitually chewing gum will eventually weaken digestion from overly stimulating the stomach. 
Chewing, of course, helps mix saliva with food (important for carbohydrates but not for pro-
teins) and ensures that stomach acids will have easy access into what we have eaten rather than 
having to ﬁght their way into large pieces (important especially for proteins). But traditional 
family wisdom is correct—most people don’t chew thoroughly enough. Terefore, ground 
meat is easier to digest than steak because stomach juices can far more easily penetrate where 
needed. 
Te second aspect is that spices can be cooked through ground meat. Every culture has spiced 
ground meat dishes, from meatballs or meatloaf to kofta from the Mediterranean or shami ke-
babs from India. Wet-cooked spiced ground meat dishes are even easier to digest than burgers 
or kebabs, for example, meat sauces such as pasta Bolognese or the simple recipes presented 
here. Select spices and herbs that assist the digestion process. 
Green leafy herbs are helpful for digesting meat: parsley, cilantro, rosemary, oregano, thyme,

<!-- p.147 -->
Ground Meat Three Styles        141
tarragon, sage, and so forth. Generally speaking, it is good to avoid mixing meat protein with 
fruit or anything sweet. Te exceptions, however, are the pit fruits (prunes and apricots, pri-
marily). Tese fruits add vitality (yang qi) to the digestive process, helping particularly with 
fatty meats that tend to be diﬃcult to digest. One of the recipes above includes prunes for this 
reason, but many other recipes can be found. 
Orange peel is another traditional herb that aids fatty meat digestion (think of duck á l’orange 
from France of orange-ﬂavor beef invented by a Chinese chef working in Boston). Te distinc-
tion must be made, however, that to aid digestion it is the orange peel that is used, not the 
orange “ﬂesh”, and certainly not orange juice or added sugar (as is common). Adding sugar 
to meat dishes will strongly tax digestion, while adding the peel will strongly help digestion 
of the dish. 
Tese recipes are similar to dishes using chickpeas, lentils or other legumes in place of meat, 
oﬀering an easy conversion to vegetarian options. Traditionally, legumes are added to ground 
meat to provide more food with less expense—a good idea in many ways but be sure to include 
the seed spices that support good digestion of beans (cumin, cardamom, mustard seed, etc.)  
And what about the basic hamburger, America’s iconic ground meat favorite?  At home, herbs 
and spices can be mixed into the ground beef before forming patties, but purists (often chil-
dren) may resist this level of foodie-intervention. No problem. Draw the line at sugary side 
dishes. Sweet desserts or cold milkshakes will slow digestion of meat and eventually cause 
signiﬁcant problems. Instead, help digestion with a good pickle (fermented, if properly made) 
and quality mustard.

<!-- p.148 -->
142        A Sampling of Main Dishes
Marinated Skirt Steak
Skirt steak is a special choice for moderately priced beef. With a simple but 
well-crafted marinade, this makes a wonderful, quick, and hearty main 
course. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 3 minutes
Total time: 15 minutes (not including marinating)
Ingredients
skirt steak (or similar) | 24 ounces
olive oil | 1/4 cup
lime juice | 1/2 lime
Shanxi vinegar (or balsamic) | 2 Tbsp 
tamari | 2 Tbsp
Directions
Allow meat to come to room temperature before cooking. Leave the skirt steak whole or cut 
it into 4 portions at this point. 
Te marinade comes together very simply. Mix the olive oil, lime juice, vinegar and tamari in a 
bowl or tray, then add the meat, being careful that each piece is coated amply. Allow the steak 
to marinate from 30 minutes to several hours. 
Heat a heavy iron pan to quite hot, and then add the steak portions. Cook 3-4 minutes per 
side. Depending on the thickness of the cut (skirt steak varies from very thin at the end to 
about an inch in the middle), your steak will now be medium rare or a bit more on the thin 
edges. Assess by gentle prodding with a utensil or your clean ﬁngers. If you are unsure, use a 
knife to discreetly peek inside. 
Remove from the pan and allow to rest on a board or plate; some coasting will always take 
place as the meat rests.

<!-- p.149 -->
Marinated Skirt Steak       143
Serve skirt steak medium rare, with green vegetables and so forth. Slice across the grain for 
presentation.   
A Note on the Food Energetics
Red meat provides a deeply invigorating bump of yang qi. Fast-and-hot cooking makes the 
most of that. Leave the center medium rare so it doesn’t get tough, add seasonings to aid di-
gestion. Skip grains for a clear meal, or include rice, millet, potatoes or other healthy carbs for 
slow, steady and satisfying assimilation. 
Of course, the varying types of red meat are diﬀerentiated from each other: beef is warming, 
lamb even more so (reserve for cool weather), while pork is closer to neutral. Further diﬀer-
entiations are possible by the speciﬁc cut: a shoulder or leg muscle was very strong and active 
for the animal, a belly cut less so. Skirt steak is a belly cut, from the diaphragm of the cow. Its 
unique muscle type and fat content make it an excellent choice for moderately priced beef.

<!-- p.150 -->
144       A Sampling of Main Dishes
Strip Steak or Ribeye
Premium steaks are more aﬀordable at home than in steakhouses, and with 
a bit of practice, even more satisfying. If you have cravings for seared meat, 
be honest and include dishes to satisfy them in your home cooking. And when 
having a steak at home, choices for vegetables extend beyond creamed spinach 
or other steak palace fare. Master vegetarian and meat cooking as part of the 
full spectrum of eating for health. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 3 minutes
Total time: 10 to 12 minutes
Ingredients
strip steak or ribeye | 2 steaks, 1.5 to 2 pounds total
grapeseed oil | enough to coat pan
sea salt | enough to sprinkle over steak
Directions
First, allow steaks to warm to room temperature before cooking; remove from refrigerator an 
hour before cooking. 
Salt the steaks well, including the sides, including the fat. Press the salt into the meat with 
clean ﬁngers (massaging the salt into the meat). 
Place a heavy pan over medium-high heat until thoroughly hot (cast iron or enamel coated 
iron is best). Add a splash of oil with a high smoke point, allow the oil to heat for a few sec-
onds, then add the steaks. Te meat should sizzle, but the oil should not smoke. Oils create 
active carcinogens when they reach their smoke points, something that should be consistently 
avoided. 
Te goal is to sear both sides of the steak while preserving tender medium rare within. Two 
methods work well for home cooking:

<!-- p.151 -->
Strip Steak or Ribeye       145
 » Place steaks in the nearly smoking pan with a gap for heat to circulate between them 
(do not crowd the pan when searing meat). Do not move them for four minutes, 
then turn them over and do not move them for three minutes. Ten, remove to a 
plate and allow to rest for 5 minutes before slicing. 
 » Place steaks in the nearly smoking pan with a suﬃcient gap between them. Turn 
them over after one minute. Repeat four times so that each side gets four one-min-
ute turns touching the heat. Ten, remove to a plate and allow to rest for 5 minutes 
before slicing.
Both methods work well, preference is personal. If you like, also give the fat a turn at the heat 
by holding the steaks fat-edge down in the pan with tongs to sear the edges. 
Experienced steak cooks always emphasize the resting period. It’s similar to cooking rice. Foods 
expand when heated; that expansion reverses when allowed to coast and cool slightly, drawing 
moisture back in. 
Serve with plenty of green vegetables, with or without carbs.   
A Note on the Food Energetics
Strip steak (a.k.a. New York strip) and ribeye are two of the more popular premium beef cuts. 
In the clinic, I commonly hear confessions about wanting to eat “healthy foods” at home but 
succumbing to meat cravings in restaurants. It’s also common to hear that when one spouse 
is urging mostly vegetarian fare at home, the other routinely orders steak when out. Tis isn’t 
good; families should eat together, sharing food if possible. Te most direct solution to these 
problems is to cook what you honestly want to eat. Indulgences like ﬁne steak are healthier 
and cheaper at home (and your home can be more honestly happy).  
Simple is best: good quality steaks with good quality salt, seared just below smoking tempera-
ture. Grass-fed beef is the best choice for a variety of reasons. It is also a bit more intense and 
therefore satisfying in smaller portions. 
Te basics energetics of meat have been described above. Eating meat is a dramatic participa-
tion in the circle of life and should be done as responsibly as possible.

<!-- p.152 -->
146       A Sampling of Main Dishes
Another Sauce: 
Tamari-Mirin Glaze 
A good sauce aids digestion of the foods it accompanies. It can also raise 
home cooking to restaurant-quality, satisfying the craving to go out for meals 
that you can easily cook at home.  Tamari-mirin glaze has three fermented 
ingredients and fresh ginger, all digestive aids. It’s versatile, simple to make, 
and makes very simple food very special. Serve with grilled ﬁsh, steak, or tofu.
Serves: sauce for four to six servings
Prep time: 3 minutes
Total time: 5-10 minutes
Ingredients
tamari (or soy sauce) | 1/4 cup
mirin | 2 Tbsp
rice vinegar | 1 Tbsp
fresh ginger | 2 slices, slivered
Directions
In a saucepan, bring ingredients to a boil then quickly lower to a simmer. Cook to reduce to 
about half, or until consistency is thickened almost to a syrup (about 5 minutes). 
Serve as a sauce at the table, or as a glaze while grilling meat, ﬁsh, shrimp or tofu. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Matching or exceeding restaurant quality cooking is not diﬃcult with some practice, especially 
when we can use better quality ingredients than many restaurants are willing to purchase. Tis 
is a standard glaze from Japanese cuisine, easy to make at home. 
Tamari is soy sauce made without the usual addition of wheat. Originally, soy sauce was a 
byproduct of miso production. Miso was the goal, soy sauce a happy accident. During fer-

<!-- p.153 -->
Another Sauce: Tamari-Mirin Glaze       147
mentation of the soybeans, a separation takes place: the solid paste below becomes miso and 
the dark ﬂavorful liquid that rises to the top is soy sauce. Miso is usually made with soy mixed 
with a grain; tamari is soy sauce when no grain is used. 
Today, soy sauce is far more popular than miso, and chemical shortcuts to produce soy sauce 
without miso are widely used (the soy solids left over are sold as cattle feed). Tis soy sauce is 
inferior for taste and health. Pay a bit more for traditionally brewed tamari, it is well worth 
it. High quality traditionally made soy sauce is also available that includes wheat as a major 
ingredient, often sold under its Japanese name, shoyu. 
Good quality tamari is fermented, oﬀering many beneﬁts and a classic umami ﬂavor. Umami
