(a term invented by a Japanese scientist in 1908) is the primordially compelling ﬂavor set of 
cooked or transformed protein. Protein is necessary for all of us daily, and we are hardwired to 
seek it. When proteins are broken down into amino acids through cooking or fermentation, 
the umami sensation results (especially if sizzling heat releases the amino aromas into the air as 
steaks do when cooked outdoors over wood coals, taunting vegetarians with umami aromas). 
Te word umami is an onomatopoeic coinage; if Professor Ikeda had been American, he might 
have called this sensation oh yummy (or, perhaps more primordially, ooh, mommy!). Tis helps 
us remember what umami is—the yummy protein sensation. (Umami is a ﬂavor set, not a 
fundamental taste such as sweet, sour, salty, bitter and sharp/spicy/pungent.)  Soy sauce or 
tamari oﬀer the umami sensation along with salty taste. (Miso is fermented in the presence of 
salt, most of which is removed. Well used, tamari does not exceed healthy salt usage.) 
Mirin—gently sweetened rice wine—is also fermented. Tere are sugary industrial versions 
that are not properly fermented, but for good eating pay a bit more for real mirin (the price 
diﬀerence seems signiﬁcant per bottle but is slight per use). Mirin does have added sugar, so if 
even a very small amount of sugar is an issue for your current health, use sake, dry white wine 
or even dry vermouth (vermouth has the beneﬁt of long shelf life, meaning a bottle will not 
spoil if used very rarely). 
Rice vinegar is made from fermented rice. Again, select a truly brewed product and ignore the 
cheaper industrial ones (read ingredient labels carefully). Also ignore ﬂavored vinegars. Rice

<!-- p.154 -->
148       A Sampling of Main Dishes
vinegar is very low in acid; if substituting another—sherry vinegar, perhaps—use a bit less. 
Tis glaze is cooked and therefore will not provide live microbial support, as miso soup does 
(which is served warm but should never be boiled). Even so, cooked fermented foods stimulate 
liver and gallbladder in beneﬁcial ways and aid digestion. Fresh ginger further aids digestion 
through its warming, clearing and soothing eﬀects. 
Afternote on Cooking Red Meat:
everyone should be skilled in vegetarian cooking 
As mentioned earlier in this chapter, animal food is part of traditional cuisines throughout the 
world and certainly can be included in a healthy diet. Chinese medicine, as part of Chinese 
culture overall, is completely comfortable with all kinds of animal foods. Te key is balance. 
Problems of too much meat have been the focus in the media, but too little can leave a person 
potentially undernourished, something that I see regularly in the dietary clinic with individ-
uals who may have bone fractures that are slow to heal, diﬃculty staying warm, borderline 
anemia, and so forth. Balance is key.
Vegetarianism is usually seen as a restricted diet. Vegetarians, in this view, eat only some of 
what others can eat. But stop to think of all that can be eaten—a wide array of grains, leafy 
vegetables, root vegetables, legumes, fruits, pods, berries, seeds, nuts, sea vegetables, mush-
rooms, spices, and animal foods from the sea, land, dairy farm, and air (poultry). No one eats 
everything. 
Tose who focus on meat tend to eat a small subset of the food that is available. If you were 
to expand your personal food habits (with real foods, not packaged foodstuﬀs), you would 
undoubtedly ﬁnd more grains, vegetables, and fruits on your table. Seen this way, we live in 
a vast expanse of mostly vegetable food, in which lies a subset of animal food. Remembering 
that farm animals are fed grains, grasses, and other vegetable feed, the image of animal food as 
existing within a context of vegetable food is complete.
With this in mind, it’s easy to see another view on vegetarian meals: all of us should be eating 
vegetarian-type meals that can also include (or occasionally feature) animal foods (dairy, sea-

<!-- p.155 -->
Afternote on Cooking Red Meat       149
food, poultry, red meat, and even honey). Being comfortable cooking (and eating) vegetarian 
meals will ensure plenty of antioxidants for anti-inﬂammatory eating. It will come closer to 
satisfying the main points of vegetarian theory as well: 
 »  Most people would eat less meat if they knew the truth of how animals are raised, 
slaughtered and prepared for market.
 »  We can’t feed the world’s population or protect a viable ecosystem with everyone 
eating modern amounts of animal food.
 »  Current norms of meat consumption are too much for good health.
In case any reader is interested, I personally have been both vegetarian and omnivore. I advo-
cate neither, only saying to our omnivores that we all need to consider what might be called 
the limits of meat consumption, and saying to our vegetarians that balancing health is easier 
with the complete food spectrum available.

<!-- p.156 -->
nine
Snacks and Desserts
A complete kitchen practice includes room for our indulgences. If sugar or 
reﬁned carbs have already damaged metabolism, it’s best to strictly avoid 
sweets, but if not, something sweet can be a delight once in a while.
  
Let’s Talk About Snacks and Desserts
“What in the world can I have for a snack, or for a sweet?  Can I ever have dessert?”
Tese are great questions, and I hear them often. Even with the best of dietary in-
tentions, if we’re not truly happy with the way we’re eating, we’re likely to give up, to 
throw in the towel. We need to make room for the occasional treat. Yet we must tread 
lightly. For many (including too many children), snacks provide a huge part of the 
daily food. Snacks can disrupt metabolism if eaten on top of a meal which has not 
yet fully digested, something I call “snack damage”. Overly sweet snacks also disrupt 
metabolism (more snack damage). We need to be honest: Are we having snacks in a 
positive way or just giving in to convenience, marketing, or old patterns?  
Tere are times for strict discipline—when overcoming serious illness or to change 
directions in life. But for many, a moderate indulgence can be part of a healthy strat-
egy—it takes the place of a conventional sugar hit that will deﬁnitely cause damage if 
repeated regularly. Keeping this balance in mind, here is a sampling of healthy snacks 
and desserts. 
Some people like black and white instructions, “Just tell me what to do!”  But just as 
the weather is always shifting based on reasons large and small, diet needs to be ﬂexi-
ble, to be adaptive. Let’s call these indulgences “advanced practice” with a wink, and 
promise not to lose our way.

<!-- p.157 -->
Chia Pudding        151
Chia Pudding
Tis easy-to-make snack or dessert is dairy- and gluten-free. Since there is no 
cooking involved, it’s a safe introduction to the kitchen for anyone old enough 
to hold a spoon. Try it for breakfast, too. 
Serves: six
Prep time: 10 minutes
Total time: 1 to 1.5 hours (no cooking, only soaking time)
Ingredients
organic chia seeds | 1/2 cup
coconut milk (canned, full fat, no added sugar) | 2 cups
water | as needed, usually about 1-2 cups
vanilla extract | 1/2 tsp
maple syrup | 1/4 cup (or less)
ground cinnamon | 3/4 tsp
cardamom powder | 1/2 tsp
fresh nutmeg | just enough scrapes to perfume the dish, or 1/8 tsp
Directions
In a bowl large enough to allow for the chia seeds to expand, mix the coconut milk with the 
spices and sweetener. 
Add the chia seeds, stir to mix thoroughly, allow to stand for about an hour at room tempera-
ture. Stir again, adding water if needed (I usually add one cup or even two, depending on 
humidity and my cooking mood that day). 
Allow the chia to absorb as much ﬂuid as they will. Ten it’s ready to eat, or refrigerate for 
later. Sprinkle with a bit more ground cinnamon to serve. 
Options are wide open; try adding a squeeze of lime juice and chopped mint or shiso just 
before serving.

<!-- p.158 -->
152        Snacks and Desserts
A Note on the Food Energetics
Chia is gelatinous and descending, a perfect food for the delicate lining of the small intestine. 
It is high in protein, ﬁber and healthy oils as well. 
Coconut was once warned against (the fats are saturated, similar to the fats in egg yolks), then 
it was championed for lowering risk of obesity and heart disease. A middle course makes the 
most sense: current science oﬀers no reason to avoid coconut or its fats, nor can it claim that 
coconut oil will work magic within us. But it is very satisfying, meaning we won’t need too 
much. And if heart disease is caused by inﬂammation, as is becoming widely accepted, saturat-
ed fats can lower circulatory problems because they are far more stable than polyunsaturated 
fats (which are chemically wide open for oxidation, thus raising internal inﬂammation). 
From the food energy point of view, remember that as the fruit of a tropical tree, coconut 
resonates with the wei qi/protective qi level, explaining why some people have allergic reactions 
or autoimmune responses to coconut. If you tend to react very easily to almost anything you 
eat, it’s wise to avoid wei qi stimulating foods (including many fruits, dairy, nuts, eggs, shellﬁsh 
and even chicken). You can still have chia pudding—just use water in place of the coconut 
milk.

<!-- p.159 -->
Oatmeal Raisin Cookies        153
Oatmeal Raisin Cookies
Tese cookies are old-school, and yet so full of real ingredients that you can 
pretend they aren’t cookies at all (until you taste them). Easy to make, too. 
Serves: twelve to sixteen
Prep time: 20 minutes
Total time: 40 minutes
Ingredients
dry oatmeal (Scottish or old-fashioned rolled) | 1.5 cups
unbleached ﬂour (or gluten-free ﬂour) | 1.5 cups
baking powder | 1/2 tsp
salt | 1/4 tsp
cinnamon | 1/4 tsp
nutmeg | several scrapes fresh nutmeg
dried currants (raisins if you like) | 1/2 cup
unsalted butter | 6-8 Tbsp (allowed to warm)
egg | 1
vanilla extract | 1/2 tsp
honey | 1/4 cup
barley malt | 1/2 cup
hazelnuts | 1 per cookie (optional)
water | add some if needed
Directions
Preheat oven to 375˚F. Line a baking sheet with parchment paper for easy baking and cleanup. 
Mix the dry oats (I prefer Scottish or old-fashioned rolled oats here for their thicker texture, 
but standard rolled oats will do) with the ﬂour (for gluten-free cookies, combine 1 cup millet

<!-- p.160 -->
154        Snacks and Desserts
ﬂour or sorghum ﬂour with 1/2 cup rice ﬂour). Add the baking powder and salt (a pinch of 
salt softens the chemical taste of the baking powder). Add the cinnamon and nutmeg. Add 
the dried fruit (I prefer the smaller currants to the traditional raisin). Mix together by hand. 
In a separate, larger bowl mix the butter (that has been allowed to warm, or substitute 5 Tbsp 
good olive oil), the egg, vanilla, honey and barley malt. A whisk works well to combine the wet 
ingredients, no electric mixer necessary. 
Mix the dry into the wet, allow it to sit a few minutes. Work in a splash of water if needed, then 
roll the dough into balls, place on the parchment sheet, press slightly to ﬂatten, then bake for 12-15 
minutes depending upon size. Allow to cool on a wire rack while another batch goes into the oven. 
Ingredient Note: Oats do not contain gluten, but they are often grown near 
wheat or packaged close enough to wheat to be unusable for those with celiac 
disease. Gluten-free oats are available that avoid such exposure. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
With desserts or sweet snacks, the goal is to provide a treat without causing any trouble. 
It’s extremely important to understand that we respond to food diﬀerently when healthy or ill. 
No food is good or bad for everyone, or for anyone at all times. An oatmeal cookie can be a 
nourishing snack if digestion is strong but a chewy mineﬁeld for someone with diabetes, SIBO 
(microbial dysfunction of small intestine) or some degree of metabolic weakness. Many people 
are somewhere between robust health and digestive trouble. It’s common sense that our health 
status should inform our food choices, but less often realized that the foods themselves will 
have diﬀerent eﬀects on diﬀerent people. 
As early as the sixth century CE, Chinese medical texts clearly diﬀerentiated foods (and med-
icines) for three loosely deﬁned groups of people: 
 » Tose who are well and healthy.
 » Tose who need some therapeutic support but are solidly on their way back to health. 
 » Tose who are ill or in compromised health.

<!-- p.161 -->
Oatmeal Raisin Cookies        155
We needn’t be permanently in one or another category—even very healthy people sometimes 
get sick and should temporarily treat themselves as ill or convalescing. Sometimes this is slow, 
year by year, or it can be short-term. For example, if you get a cold or ﬂu with fever and chills, 
you are actively ill (all food selected should be good medicine for you at such time). Once the 
fever breaks and chills are gone, you know you’ll be ﬁne but you’re not fully well yet, you are 
convalescing (eat to restore appetite and complete your recovery). After a week or two, you 
will be fully recovered and probably feel better than before you got sick (now, eat to support 
what’s coming next in your life, with the renewed vitality that comes with full recovery). Mis-
understanding this process is a major reason so many people have recurrences or linger in a 
gray zone between illness and health. 
With these principles in mind, decide for yourself when to make cookies or when to have 
meals without desserts. It’s not up to anyone else to tell you how healthy you wish to be. When 
you do indulge, quality should be good and portions appropriate. 
Tese cookies are sweet but in a signiﬁcantly diﬀerent way than what many people eat every 
day, multiple times through the day. Barley malt contains maltose rather than fructose—it 
is made by allowing natural enzymes in barley to convert the carbs to maltose through the 
sprouting process. Barley malt tastes like old-fashioned candy and has multiple uses in herbal 
medicine: it clears food stagnation, calms digestive distress between liver and pancreas-spleen, 
promotes appetite, soothes ulcers of stomach or intestines, calms diarrhea, and perhaps most 
famously, promotes growth for children who are not thriving. Honey adds a sharper type of 
sweetness. Combining both creates a deep and complex sweetness (while providing intellectual 
rationalization that eating these cookies may even be good for you). 
We used to be told to be afraid of butter as a saturated fat, but no longer. Butter supports 
proper hormone production and is satisfying enough that one or two cookies feels complete. 
If butter is not on your food map, use olive oil. (A secret from Moroccan cuisine is that olive 
oil can be used very successfully in baking.) 
And of course, the use of oats brings their beneﬁts to snack food (as long as the sugar is not 
dominant). Oats soothe digestion even while they add ﬁber and bulk to the diet, aiding peri-
stalsis and preventing a sweet indulgence from causing food stagnation.

<!-- p.162 -->
156        Snacks and Desserts
Pear Juice Kanten
Another gelatinous food that is soothing for the intestines is the vegetarian 
“gelatin” made from kanten or agar-agar. Cooling when eaten chilled or 
warming when just made, pear juice kanten is far easier to make than con-
ventional jelled desserts or snacks.
Serves: eight
Prep time: 5 minutes
Total time: 15 minutes
Ingredients
organic pear juice | 32 ounces
agar-agar (kanten) ﬂakes | 3 Tbsp
Directions
Place the juice in a saucepan over medium heat. 
Add the agar-agar ﬂakes. Tey will dissolve as you stir. Bring just to a boil, careful to avoid 
boil-over. Reduce heat, and simmer 5 minutes, stirring occasionally. 
Pour into serving bowls (we use thick water glasses), and allow to cool. 
Serve still somewhat warm, or chilled. Unlike gelatin, kanten will set whether warm or cool. 
Options: Use any good quality juice you like, apple, apricot, etc. Kuzu powder can be substi-
tuted for the agar-agar. Sesame tahini can be mixed into the juice while warming for a beauti-
ful ﬂavor and a shift of focus. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Agar (agar-agar or kanten) is a neutral vegetarian “gelatin” made from seaweed (or more pre-
cisely, a type of red algae). It is available as small ﬂakes or in feather-light bars. Te name 
agar-agar is from the southeast Malaysian language; kanten is the Japanese word for the same.

<!-- p.163 -->
Pear Juice Kanten       157
First used in the 1600s, agar has widespread modern food uses, including thickening inexpen-
sive ice cream, but don’t think of it as a chemical additive. It has excellent properties as a food. 
Some Westerners ﬁnd seaweed slightly constipating (lacking a gene or the trained microﬂora 
to digest it). Agar, in contrast, is nearly all cell wall ﬁber so it helps peristalsis with a mild lax-
ative eﬀect (making a good balance with mild sweets, which slow digestion). Agar has found 
its way all over the world over the last three hundred years and is permitted in certiﬁed organic 
products. 
Pears and apples are fruits that beneﬁt lung function. Tey are the natural fruits of autumn, 
when many people begin to suﬀer colds and seasonal respiratory problems. Kanten is a use-
ful snack or dessert during autumn and winter, if served warm and not too sweet. In winter 
weather, addition of sesame tahini can further support warming (sesame seeds also connect 
with kidney energetics, moisten the intestines with their oils to clear constipation, and, as 
seeds, have a descending directionality). 
Fruit juice may seem healthy, but drinking a lot of juice means you are consuming too much 
sugar. It’s much easier to have a small amount when thickened as kanten. In modest portions, 
fruit juices can be used for their true characteristics. 
Agar-agar is in many ways superior to gelatin (or pectin used to thicken jams). It melts easily 
and does not require refrigeration to set. It adapts well to juices of diﬀerent colors, can hold 
fruit chunks in suspension, and once set, it can be precisely sliced for artistic presentations. In 
our family, however, it makes a simple and healthy comfort food that kids and adults love to 
eat—and it couldn’t be easier for the cook to prepare.

<!-- p.164 -->
158        Snacks and Desserts
Rice Pudding
Rice pudding is a wonderful comfort food, a meal and dessert in one. I’m 
nearly always disappointed by too much added sugar in restaurant versions. 
Make it at home to enjoy it most fully. 
Serves: four to six
Prep time: 10 minutes
Total time: 40 minutes
Ingredients
medium grain white rice | 3/4 cup
water | 2 cups (more may be needed)
whole milk (organic), or substitutes (see below) | 3 cups
vanilla extract | 1 tsp
cinnamon | 1 tsp
cardamom pods | 3-4 pods (1/4 tsp if ground)
citrus peel | 1/2 tsp dried, more if fresh
nutmeg | 1/4 tsp, best freshly ground 
honey | 1/4 to 1/3  cup
Directions
Bring the rice to boil in 2 cups water. Lower the heat to simmer, cover with a lid. 
Cook for 15 minutes, then add the milk (see below for a note on cows’ milk alternatives), 
honey, cinnamon, cardamom (pods or ground), and citrus peel. 
Return to a very gentle boil, stirring to prevent any scorching at the bottom of the pot. To pre-
serve more whole rice, stir less vigorously using a wooden paddle; to make a creamier pudding, 
stir more vigorously using a large wire whisk. 
Continue cooking over low heat for 15-30 minutes. Keep covered when you are not stirring.

<!-- p.165 -->
Rice Pudding       159
Alternatively, the pudding can be moved to a 375˚F oven to ﬁnish for 30 minutes. 
When rice is tender but still discernible as individual grains, add the vanilla extract and the 
nutmeg. Taste for sweetness and balance. If necessary for proper texture, add more water.  Re-
move from the heat. 
You may serve rice pudding warm, at room temperature, or cooled. (Allow to cool in the refrig-
erator in the cooking pot, or in individual serving dishes.)  Serve with more cinnamon on top. 
Ingredient Notes 
Rice: Diﬀerent rice types will make very diﬀerent rice puddings. Short grains will be creamier 
(and require nearly double the amount of water to reach a consistency that is not too tight). 
My preference is for a medium grain white rice of Asian type, but by adjusting stirring time 
and amount of water, any type can be used. Experiment to see what you like best.
Wonderful rice pudding can also be made with black “forbidden” rice. Begin with 4 cups water 
and simmer or bake 45-60 minutes. Here, my preference is to use canned full-fat unsweetened 
coconut milk, and I like to add lime juice and a pinch of salt to the seasoned “milk”. Warm 
some additional coconut milk in a saucepan with cinnamon, honey and cardamom to pour 
over the rice pudding when serving, as desired. 
Milk: Most nondairy milks work well in rice pudding, especially coconut, almond, hemp seed 
or oat. Be aware that commercial nondairy milks are usually sweetened; omit the honey from 
the recipe.  If using homemade nut milks (easy to make and wonderful), some sweetener will 
probably be welcome. (Of course, rice milk can be used, but for me that doubles up on rice in 
a way that doesn’t feel right—better to add another ingredient.)
A Note on the Food Energetics
For best health, have this dish warm or at room temperature and avoid over-sweetening. Soft-
cooked rice with a gentle sweetness is soothing to digestion, nourishes stomach yin and soothes 
the upper intestines. Te use of milk makes it a classic, deep comfort food, appropriate for 
convalescence or anytime a dietary hug feels right.

<!-- p.166 -->
160        Snacks and Desserts
Rice pudding often includes a dried fruit. Select the fruit for its properties beyond adding 
sweetness. Raisins and goji berries nourish blood and support liver function, ﬁgs and prunes aid 
peristalsis. Add dried fruit during the middle of the cooking process or on top when serving. 
Let’s Talk About Milk
Most people would do well to reduce the dairy in their diet (or avoid it completely). For many, 
avoiding cheese and other dairy would be the strongest single change for health other than 
avoiding reﬁned sugar. Milk should deﬁnitely be avoided for those with serious illness or any-
one who wishes to lose weight. Yet, no food is always good or always bad. What, then, are the 
potential beneﬁts of milk?  Milk appears as a medicinal substance in the oldest materia medica, 
known as the Shen Nong Ben Cao (Divine Farmer’s Medicinal Manual) collated and reﬁned by 
the Daoist master Tao Hong-Jing (456-536 CE). Te assessment of milk in that classic text 
still holds true. Used well, milk can aid convalescence, helping to build back strength and body 
tissue. It is moistening (but too much produces phlegm). Knowing this, consider the following 
story from the life of Buddha: 
Te prince Gautama Siddhartha had left home and exchanged his ﬁne clothes for a simple 
monk’s robe. He wandered for several years, studying with the greatest spiritual teachers of 
his time. Dissatisﬁed with that formal training, he spent more years meditating in the forests 
with a small group of ascetics. Tis, still, didn’t provide the breakthrough the monk sought. 
He walked to the bank of a river, and sat alone beneath a giant tree. He vowed he would not 
rise until he achieved the breakthrough that he sensed was near or died. It’s the well-known 
enlightenment story of one of the world’s great traditions, and we know how it turned out: 
Siddhartha had his ultimate realization just before dawn. Ten, starved and exhausted, he 
passed out. 
Now comes the rice pudding part. After the sun rose, a shepherd girl was guiding one or two 
water buﬀalo along the riverbank and happened upon the collapsed monk. Was he sleeping? 
Was he dead?  He was nothing but skin and bones. She approached haltingly—the rules of 
society didn’t allow women to approach men. She was of the very lowest caste and he was a 
holy man, possibly deceased. She called to him, then again. Maybe she nudged him with her

<!-- p.167 -->
Rice Pudding        161
shepherd’s staﬀ. He woke—still in a state of complete realization—and saw her.  “Monk, are 
you alive?  I am sorry to disturb you, but you look in need, can I help you?”  Te Buddha 
spoke to her very directly, against all custom.  “Do you come by this river spot each day?” 
“Yes, Monk!”   “If it’s no trouble, would you bring a bit of rice tomorrow?”  Such food clearly 
broke the vows of asceticism that Siddhartha had been following in the forests. Te next 
day, the shepherdess arrived with a wooden bowl of rice cooked in water buﬀalo milk. What 
decadence!  It was food that would have been given to babies as they weaned. (Buﬀalo milk 
is thinner than that of modern cows, it is, in fact, very close to human mother’s milk.)  So, 
what happened in the story?  
Buddha gratefully received a single bowl of primordial rice pudding from the shepherd girl. 
He didn’t eat too much, nor too little—just enough—that morning and every morning after-
ward, until he had regained his health. 
Te shepherdess is a crucial part of a great story. From the very ﬁrst, food and compassion ex-
emplify two aspects of what would become Buddhist teaching: respect and kindness to all and 
the idea of moderation—not too much nor too little—the Middle Way. It was extraordinarily 
normal. 
I always think of this story when I make rice pudding. Actually, it’s because of this story that 
I make rice pudding at all. 
To simulate the milk of a skinny water buﬀalo from 500 BCE, mix cow’s milk with water. Add 
enough sweetness to remind you of the kindness exchanged between the shepherd girl and 
the Buddha. A little cinnamon, a cardamom pod or two, perhaps an occasional piece of dried 
fruit. It reminds us that no food is just food, and that everything has a story.

<!-- p.168 -->
162        Snacks and Desserts
Stewed Dried Fruit
My grandmother used to make this when she wasn’t feeling well, an old-
school kitchen remedy. But that stigmatizes a great dish. Tink of it like this: 
it’s nice to have an understanding friend to phone when you need a shoulder 
to lean on, but if that’s the only time you call, how is that friend supposed 
to feel?  Enjoy stewed fruit even when nothing’s wrong, it’s a luxurious and 
healthful dessert. 
Serves: eight
Prep time: 10-15 minutes
Total time: 1 hour
Ingredients
prunes | 1/2 cup
ﬁgs | 1/2 cup
apricots | 1/2 cup
dried apples | 1/2 cup
cinnamon | 1 tsp 
cloves | three whole, or 1/2 tsp ground 
star anise | 3 whole 
red wine | 1 cup 
vinegar (balsamic or apple cider vinegar) | 1/4 cup
honey | 1 Tbsp
fresh or dried (organic) citrus peel | approximate size of a postage stamp
water | 1-2 cups 
Directions
Place all the ingredients in a saucepan and bring to a simmer, stirring occasionally. Continue 
cooking until the fruits have softened, the alcohol is evaporated from the wine and a gentle

<!-- p.169 -->
Stewed Dried Fruit        163
syrup has developed between the fruits. Add more water if too thick, or cook further to reduce 
if too wet. Stirring will avoid sticking. 
Serve warm. 
Have as a snack or an hour after dinner as dessert, or serve with Greek (strained) yogurt and 
chopped almonds for a complete meal.  
A Note on the Food Energetics
It may seem counterintuitive to dry fruits only to rehydrate and cook them. Fruits contain 
more nutrients when raw (from a laboratory analysis point of view) but that must be balanced 
by the needs of digestion. Raw fruit tends to be very cooling, and digestion only works well 
when warm. Drying fruits adds warmth to them, as does the gentle, relatively long stewing. 
Cinnamon and clove are warming spices. 
Prunes are famous for aiding peristalsis and clearing constipation, but ﬁgs are just as good or 
better. If digestion and elimination are working well, there is no need to fear eating prunes or 
ﬁgs; but if food stagnation or constipation is present, they can be therapeutic foods (along with 
suﬃcient oils and warm water in the diet). 
Almonds are descending and directly support descension and the necessary contribution of 
lung qi for healthy elimination. 
Apples and pears also support lung qi descension, useful for clearing food stagnation and help-
ing the lungs during the cold and ﬂu season. 
Wine and vinegar are fermented products; wine further adds a resonance with the blood level, 
helping build rich blood and robust immunity. 
Citrus peel adds lightness to a fairly heavy fruit dish while helping to relax the liver for free 
and easy breathing. 
Snacking on a mixture of fruit and nuts can be excellent, but stewing dried fruits transforms 
their eﬀect, making fruit far more health-supportive in wintertime or any time of year when

<!-- p.170 -->
164        Snacks and Desserts
extra digestive support would be helpful. 
Note: since this dish restores movement of peristalsis and helps clear consti-
pation, avoid if chronic diarrhea is present. For healthy individuals, however, 
this dish can simply be a very pleasant snack or dessert.

<!-- p.171 -->
Poached Pears       165
Poached Pears
As a dessert, snack or complete meal, poached pears are unbeatable. Pears 
connect with the lungs, helping them stay moist to function well. Te spices 
relax breathing and warm the emotions for lingering, settled conversation 
after dinner. 
Serves: four to six
Prep time: ten minutes
Total time: one hour
Ingredients
organic pears (Bosc, Anjou, etc.) | 4-6 whole pears
red wine | 1-2 cups
balsamic vinegar | 1/4 cup
water | 1 cup
organic citrus peel |  use zest or several thumb-size strips of dried peel  (organic is 
important to avoid sprays that linger on the peel)
cinnamon | 1 tsp
star anise | 3 stars
clove | 2-4 whole cloves
cardamom | 3 whole pods or 1/2 tsp ground
vanilla | 1 whole vanilla bean or 1/2 tsp vanilla extract
honey | 2 Tbsp
Directions
Wash the pears; peel if you like (I leave the skins on). Cut a thin slice oﬀ the bottom to allow 
them to stand upright in the cooking pot. 
Place all the ingredients in a large pot. Bring to boil. Ten, reduce to a gentlemanly simmer 
and cook 45-60 minutes.

<!-- p.172 -->
166        Snacks and Desserts
Remove pears from liquid to cool. 
Raise the heat on the cooking liquid to reduce it nearly to a syrup.  Drizzle over pears. 
Serve warm or at room temperature, alone or with Greek-style yogurt. For a complete meal, 
add almonds and dried ﬁgs (remove the stems and with a sharp knife cut each ﬁg into 4-5 
slices).
A Note on the Food Energetics
Pears are a beautiful tree fruit, excellent for autumn or winter eating. While many fruits are 
semitropical (bananas, mangoes, dates, citrus, and so forth), pears and apples are the classic 
temperate zone fruits. Pears actively support the lungs (as do apples). 
Add warming spices (cinnamon, star anise, clove, etc.), and this lovely dessert dish can help 
prepare us for the winter season and its association with common cold or ﬂu. 
Vanilla is a ﬂoral spice; its use (even in small amounts) helps uplift a long-cooked dish. Vanilla 
relaxes the heart, gently freeing the emotions. Citrus peel relaxes the diaphragm, allowing 
breathing to relax. Te wine (with alcohol cooked away) connects with the blood level, nour-
ishing blood and its relation to the liver. Finishing a dinner with poached pear, conversation 
will be more open and settled, and when it’s time, sleep should be a little bit easier and a little 
bit deeper.

<!-- p.173 -->
Butternut Squash Pie       167
Butternut Squash Pie
It’s important for the health-conscious cook to avoid joining the Food Po-
lice—the pay is weak and you don’t even get to issue tickets to oﬀenders. But 
this pie is so naturally good that someone might call the Food Police on you, 
which is a welcome change of role. Don’t worry, the judge will let you go as 
soon as the healthy ingredients are revealed and the last of the evidence is 
eaten. 
Serves: eight
Prep time: 40 minutes
Total time: 1.5 hours
Ingredients
To make the crust:   
rolled oats | 1 cup
oat ﬂour (or other ﬂour) | 1 cup
almond meal | 1/2 cup
sunﬂower seeds | 1/2 cup
cinnamon | 1/2 tsp
salt | pinch
oil (grapeseed, walnut oil, etc.) | 1/2 cup
maple syrup or barley malt | 2 Tbsp
water | 1-3 Tbsp, if needed
To make the ﬁlling:
butternut squash | 2 medium size 
maple syrup | 1/4 cup
barley malt | 1/4 cup (or 1/2 cup all maple syrup)
tahini | 2 Tbsp

<!-- p.174 -->
168        Snacks and Desserts
cinnamon | 1 tsp  
nutmeg | 1/2 tsp
freshly grated ginger | 1/2 tsp
sea salt | 1/2 tsp
milk or nondairy milk | 1 cup
kuzu powder | 4 Tbsp 
Directions
Begin by baking the butternut squash. Preheat oven to 400˚F. Cut squash lengthwise, remove 
seeds with a spoon (rinse and roast the seeds for snacking, if desired). Place the squash face 
down on a baking sheet covered with parchment paper. Bake until very soft, about 50-60 
minutes. 
While the squash is baking, make the crust. 
Whisk wet ingredients for the crust until emulsiﬁed. Ten, mix the dry ingredients together. 
Add the wet ingredients into the dry, combine with spoon. 
Oil a deep pie plate. Use your ﬁngers if you choose to use butter, or a bunched paper towel if 
you choose coconut oil, grapeseed oil, walnut oil, etc.
Place the crust mixture in the middle of the oiled pie plate. Use a small bowl of water next to 
your workspace to moisten your hands before pressing the crust from the middle outward. Re-
wet hands as needed. If the crust seems too dry to form, add 1-3 Tbsp water. 
To make the edges, press into the corners with a thumb while ﬁngers of your other hand guide 
the dough up along the sides. Make the corners tight, as the crust tends to droop a bit as it 
bakes. 
When the squash is baked and can be removed from the oven to cool, blind bake the crust 
until barely brown, about 15 minutes with the oven set to 375˚F. 
While the crust is baking, ﬁnish making the ﬁlling. Scoop the baked squash from the skins

<!-- p.175 -->
Butternut Squash Pie       169
and place in a blender. Puree the squash with the barley malt, maple syrup, tahini, pinch of 
salt and spices. 
Dissolve the kuzu powder in the milk (or nondairy milk of your choice). Add that to the 
blender. 
Pour the ﬁlling into the baked crust, shake it gently to spread evenly. 
Return to the oven at 375˚F for 25-30 minutes, until the ﬁlling is set and has just slightly 
browned. 
Cool before slicing. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Many assume that a healthy eating practice isn’t fun or lacks foods for special occasions. “Can 
I cheat sometimes?” is a common question I hear from clients. Te answer depends on health 
status and where an individual is in their personal progress. At ﬁrst, many people want to get 
healthier without changing a thing in their lifestyle (“Isn’t there just something I can take that 
will ﬁx me?”)  In the next stage, a person wants to get better and is willing to focus on making 
that happen, at least some of the time (“back and forth” syndrome). After a few more stages, 
special occasions such as birthdays are exactly when you want to feel best and truly nurture 
your life. Luckily, this butternut squash pie can work during all of those stages. 
Butternut squash is a food that directly aids digestion. It’s grounding, mildly sweet, and is 
soothing to the stomach and small intestines. It aids metabolism (including pancreas-spleen) 
and includes minerals and ﬁber for peristalsis. It’s a great food. (Other cooking squashes and 
pumpkins share these soothing qualities.)  
Barley malt prevents or clears food stagnation (most desserts easily add to stagnation). Te oats 
also clear stagnation and stimulate peristalsis, while supporting stomach, pancreas-spleen and 
the intestines. Te spices warm and harmonize digestion. 
Eating too much of even a mild sweet will eventually cause problems, but this pie, made with 
dairy or without, is real food and can be a safe and delicious treat.

<!-- p.176 -->
170        Snacks and Desserts
Holiday Honey Cake
Cake is not known for being especially healthy, but it’s nice to have an easy 
cake in your cooking repertoire for holidays or birthdays. Tis one—adapted 
from a traditional holiday spice cake—is a go-to for our family birthdays. 
When our daughter was in grade school, a birthday party was a stressful rite 
of passage: Would the school girls eat the cake or “be like, Health food! Yuck!”  
Tis cake always disappeared, the girls having two or three pieces each. We 
would make one with the ﬁrst recipe and another that was without gluten or 
eggs, and some years that one was the favorite. 
Serves: eight
Prep time: 20 minutes
Total time: 1 hour
Ingredients
all-purpose ﬂour | 3.5 cups
baking powder | 1 Tbsp
baking soda | 1 tsp
salt | 1/2 tsp
cinnamon (ground) | 2 tsp
cardamom (ground) | 1/2 tsp
cloves (ground) | 1/2 tsp
allspice (ground) | 1/2 tsp
olive oil | 1 cup
honey | 1/2 cup
barley malt | 1 cup
eggs | 3
vanilla extract | 1 tsp
Puer tea (black tea as option) | 1 cup

<!-- p.177 -->
Holiday Honey Cake        171
fresh orange juice | 1/2 cup
rum or brandy (optional) | 1/4 cup
slivered almonds or whole pepita | 1/2 cup
Directions
Preheat oven to 375˚F. 
In a large bowl, combine the ﬂour, baking powder, baking soda, salt, and ground spices. Stir 
to combine. 
In a smaller bowl, mix the eggs, oil, honey, barley malt, orange juice, tea, vanilla extract and 
optional alcohol. Stir together. 
Pour the wet ingredients into the dry. Mix, but only enough—beating wheat will toughen its 
gluten, preventing full rising. Let batter sit to allow the ﬂour particles to fully absorb the mois-
ture of the wet ingredients. Add water (a tablespoon at a time) if needed to achieve the classic 
cake batter consistency: sticky-runny oﬀ the mixing spoon. 
Butter a 9 x 9-inch cake pan (or larger). Pour in the batter, shake to settle, and arrange the 
slivered almonds or whole pepitas evenly across the top. 
Bake for 40-50 minutes (less if wider pan), until top browns and a probe exits cleanly (or very 
nearly so).  
 
A Note on the Food Energetics
It’s nice to have a few cake recipes that you like to make for birthdays and special occasions. 
When you make it yourself you have control over what’s in it, adjusting the sweetness and 
spices, or swapping out ingredients to accommodate allergies or sensitivities. Here is a guide 
for adapting any recipe (including this one) to be gluten-free and egg-free.
Wheat Flour
Wheat is a strong and very useful food for those who have no gluten problems, but it is im-

<!-- p.178 -->
172        Snacks and Desserts
portant to select organic wheat ﬂour. Te organic farming movement isn’t perfect, but the dif-
ference is signiﬁcant. GMO wheat is designed to tolerate high amounts of herbicide (usually 
glyphosate, originally sold as Roundup). Glyphosate residues in wheat easily pass through the 
intestine wall, causing chemically induced leaky gut syndrome. Tis signiﬁcant health prob-
lem will persist at least as long as American commercial farming tolerates late-application of 
glyphosate to wheat crops (European farms are forbidden from using glyphosate in this way). 
Gluten-Free
 To substitute non-glutinous ﬂours for the 3.5 cups wheat ﬂour, use:
 » 1 cup millet ﬂour
 » 1 cup ﬁne cornmeal
 » 1 cup buckwheat ﬂour
 » 1/2 cup white rice ﬂour
Tis will taste very good and hold together successfully. Commercial gluten-free ﬂour mixes 
are readily available, but it’s easy to design your own. Since gluten provides stickiness and 
rising qualities, mix your non-glutinous ﬂours accordingly. Millet and corn are not sticky, but 
buckwheat and rice ﬂour strongly hold together.  Tis mix will work very well for cakes, pan-
cakes, and muﬃns. If any of these non-glutinous ﬂours don’t work for you (such as cornmeal), 
simply substitute another. Other “ﬂours” that can be used in your own mix include almond 
meal or coconut ﬂour. Include some sticky and non-sticky ﬂours or your cake will be too 
crumbly or too heavy. (If you’re in a hurry, commercial gluten-free ﬂour mixes are acceptable.) 
Egg-Free
 Once rare, egg allergies are on the rise. Te standard substitute per egg is:
 » 3/4 tsp potato starch 
 » 3/4 tsp tapioca ﬂour (tapioca is powdered cassava root)
 » 2 Tbsp water
Arrowroot or kuzu powder can be used in place of tapioca ﬂour. Tese are very easy starches to

<!-- p.179 -->
Holiday Honey Cake       173
digest (and can be used as healing herbs to soothe digestion, particularly beneﬁting stomach 
and upper small intestine). 
When substituting ingredients, remember that every food or ingredient has characteristics and 
properties. Use this to advantage—with a little practice you can make a gluten-free cake with 
kuzu for egg that anyone will love. Tat’s the goal.   
When it’s a birthday or a holiday and you want to make a cake, buy organic, use barley malt 
and honey instead of white sugar, and adjust the recipe to suit the people you are cooking for. 
Know what all your ingredients are. Tis recipe takes an hour to make. It’s easy, the kitchen 
smells great while you’re cooking, and the taste is beautiful. 
If cake frosting or icing is requested, avoid sugary options and simply add homemade whipped 
cream as a dollop on the side of a slice.

<!-- p.180 -->
174        Snacks and Desserts
Honey-Roasted Almonds, Pecans, and Walnuts
At their best, sweet foods remind us of the sweetness of life. Tis rich dessert 
seems like pure indulgence, but it can contribute to healthful eating  if used 
with care and understanding. 
Serves: eight
Prep time: 5-10 minutes
Total time: 10-15 minutes
Ingredients
almonds (unsalted) | 1 cup
pecans | 1 cup
walnuts | 1 cup
currants or raisins | 1/2 cup
honey or maple syrup | enough to coat (up to 1/4 cup)
unsalted butter | 4 oz
vanilla extract | a splash (1/2 tsp)
cinnamon (ground) | 1/2 tsp
sea salt | small pinch (optional)
Directions
With a sharp knife on a cutting board, slice the nuts, each into 2 or 3 pieces, but do not dec-
imate (or, if you prefer, leave them whole). 
Add nuts to a heavy pan over medium heat, along with the butter, honey (see below for a Note 
on sweetener choice), vanilla extract and pinch of salt (omit salt if using salted butter).   
Heat the mixture until it bubbles, stirring to avoid any burning, 3-5 minutes. Add the currants 
in the last minute or so. 
Dust lightly with the ground cinnamon and serve warm. Tis mixture is delicious on its own

<!-- p.181 -->
Honey-Roasted Almonds, Pecans, and Walnuts       175
or with some plain yogurt.
As an option, mix ingredients together and bake in a 350˚F oven for 15 minutes on a parch-
ment paper-lined baking tray. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Tis is another richly satisfying dessert included not for daily fare but to show that healthy 
eating can include beautiful indulgences on occasion. People too often eat their “diet” then 
switch gears and “cheat”. What we need is a truly satisfying diet that allows us to have a sweet 
treat while remaining integrated with our knowledge and food plan.   
Almonds promote descension (most nuts do) and include protein and ﬁber. Tey support 
lungs and large intestine and therefore help avoid stagnation when included in something 
sweet. 
Other nuts can be used, of course: hazelnuts, for example, or branch out to pumpkin or sun-
ﬂower seeds, with raisins, ﬁgs, chopped dates, or coconut ﬂakes. Or, use just one nut, such as 
almond or walnuts. I select which nuts to use balancing two considerations: 
 » What are the freshest nuts I can get today? 
 » Which nut would be most suited for the people I’m cooking for?  
Each nut has somewhat diﬀerent energetics. Said most brieﬂy: use almonds to support lungs 
and for clearing constipation, hazelnuts or macadamias for ease of digestion, pecans for liver 
considerations including easing sore ligaments, walnuts for kidney support.

<!-- p.182 -->
176        Snacks and Desserts
Not All Sweeteners Are Created Equal
In modern medical terms, sugar is sugar, high in calories. Or, diﬀerent types of sugar can be 
compared in a glycemic index: the measure of how quickly and how far blood sugar will rise 
when a substance is eaten alone (inaccurate because context inﬂuences absorption). 
For me, however, the relative levels of fructose are the most important nutrition aspect (fruc-
tose causes major problems for the liver, over time). Table sugar is half fructose, honey and 
agave syrup about the same, maple syrup a bit less, trendy sugars like that extracted from 
coconut sap are slightly lower than that. Molasses is lower still (and very high in minerals), 
and barley malt or rice malt syrup have almost no fructose (they are maltose). In the kitchen, 
this variation means that mixing sweeteners can help control the fructose in your desserts. For 
example, if barley malt is mixed with honey or agave syrup, the fructose in your recipe can 
drop by half. If a food company discovered a table sugar that had half the fructose, it would 
be the dietary sensation of the decade. Te informed cook can make this happen very easily.
It’s important to note these nutritional factors and act on them, but we’re also interested in 
where the sweetener comes from, what signature it brings with it. What is its poetry?  Honey 
is an insect product, it arouses protective wei qi and can be useful for reducing immune issues 
like pollen allergies (hay fever). Maple syrup is concentrated sap of tall trees, it spent its life 
reaching up through a noble trunk to the branching leaves (in classical language, aspiring to 
heaven)—use maple syrup to feel inspired. Agave syrup is concentrated juice of a desert cactus 
that spent its life holding on to any moisture that could be gathered—use agave to help nour-
ish hydration (all sugars will retain water, by the way). Many new sweeteners are arriving: co-
conut syrup (made from coconut palm, not coconut fruit), date sugar, pomegranate molasses. 
And of course, there are the ubiquitous white or brown sugars from sugarcane and sugar beets. 
When it comes to sugar, the main thing to keep in mind is that any sugar used daily will im-
pact not only your weight but the health of your blood and all that depends upon blood. Any 
sugar can get us into trouble, including artiﬁcial sweeteners. Learn to enjoy the subtle tastes of 
foods without added sugar, and when all’s clear, your taste will be calibrated back to normal so 
that the sweetness of an occasional dessert or snack treat comes through honestly and strongly.

<!-- p.183 -->
ten
Breakfast Foods
Te early masters of Chinese medicine identiﬁed the morning as the best 
time to eat grain (carbohydrates). Tis is the time the stomach and met-
abolic regulators (pancreas-spleen) are naturally at their peak. Tat’s the 
theory, but breakfast must be as individual as any other meal. Te sam-
pling of breakfasts here oﬀers options for a variety of needs and tastes.

<!-- p.184 -->
178        Breakfast Foods
Let’s Talk About Breakfast 
Breakfast can be tough. Mornings are rushed, sleep incomplete, the shower 
isn’t wet enough and there’s no time for breakfast.  “I don’t get grumpy—it’s 
everybody else!”  Ten there’s the bickering in the media about whether the 
ﬁrst meal of the day is truly the most important or makes no diﬀerence if 
skipped altogether—about as interesting as the footnotes in a study about 
the optimal number of times to chew bread. In my dietary work, I have had 
people ask me if the milk in their take-out coﬀee counts as protein (does it?). 
And of course, there is the quick-grab sugar ﬁx (of your choice) that sabotages 
so much of the dietary work we are all trying to do. 
Permit me a word to calm the waters. Breakfast is a part of it, a part of our to-
tal life; it can’t be considered alone. Tat’s why all those importance-of-break-
fast studies are inconclusive—they are short-term and isolated, two things 
that life shouldn’t be. Often, my clients who are most ill have become dis-
oriented from the sun; they have near-permanent jet-lag without actually 
traveling—up at night, asleep until noon. Tis pattern exhausts kidney qi, 
depleting adrenals and undermining good digestion (ﬁnally the digestive 
connection). As described in Book One, kidney qi underlies metabolism. Di-
gestive strength (and therefore the foods we should eat) varies not only with 
being well-rested or not, but with the time of our sleep relative to the cycle 
of day and night. Appetite for breakfast in the morning is a very good sign of 
an important health status. Breakfast is important in ways that are subtle and 
hard to study (surprise).

<!-- p.185 -->
Congee (Rice Porridge)      179
Congee (rice porridge, or Jook)
Congee provides what’s needed to support the stomach’s protective lining, and 
it’s so easy to digest that it eﬀectively rests and resets digestion. Further beneﬁts 
spread out from this central pivot.
Serves: four
Prep time: 15 minutes
Total time: 60 minutes
Ingredients
medium or long grain white rice | 1 cup
grapeseed or other light oil | 2 Tbsp
good salt | two generous pinches or more
water | 8-12 cups
various condiments | medium amounts, see below
Directions
Cook’s Tip: Heat 8-12 cups water in a separate pot. Having hot water on 
hand streamlines the cooking process. 
Put one cup uncooked medium or long grain white rice in large, empty pot. Turn heat to high, 
add 1-2 Tbsp grapeseed oil (or other light oil). Stir, coating each grain. Add two generous 
pinches salt. 
After a minute or two, add just enough boiling water to cover the rice, perhaps a cup (no need 
to measure). Stir steadily as the grains absorb the ﬁrst water. When almost absorbed, add more 
boiling water and keep stirring. After 3 or 4 rounds of adding water and stirring (about 10-15 
minutes), the grain will have released starch into the water (this is called ﬁrst starch in congee 
jargon). 
Ten, add 4-6 cups more water and turn the ﬂame down to a gentlemanly simmer for about 
45 minutes, now stirring only occasionally. Don’t let the grains settle and stick to the bottom

<!-- p.186 -->
180        Breakfast Foods
of the pot. Add more hot water as needed to achieve the special congee consistency.  Add 
more salt as desired (restaurant congee can be startlingly salty). Cooking congee is surprising 
at ﬁrst—the grains absorb more and more water. Walk away for a few minutes and you’ll 
be amazed that it needs even more water. Te congee is ready when it’s stable—milky water 
between discernible rice grains, very well cooked. All this water in the soft rice can then be 
assimilated during digestion. 
Congee is only served plain if digestion is very weak (for example, as an infant’s ﬁrst food or 
an adult’s ﬁrst food after surgery). Other than such exceptions, congee is always served with 
condiments and extras. Every cook has their favorites. I invariably oﬀer a splash of tamari, 
some drops of toasted sesame oil, sliced chive or scallion, and very thinly slivered ginger. Tey 
are added to each bowl when serving. Congee should also have a protein. Use three slices of 
leftover meat from last night’s dinner to be traditional (roast pork, chicken, duck, etc.)  I prefer 
poached or soft-boiled eggs (1 or 2 per person) or sliced ﬁsh ﬁllet cooked simply in a pan with 
ginger, scallion, tamari and sesame oil (easy, you already have them out). Fish ﬁllet with boiled 
peanuts is very traditional (ﬁsh and peanuts are deeply nourishing constitutional level foods, 
as are eggs). Another favorite is dried scallops (a Chinatown ingredient that keeps well in the 
cupboard). Dried scallops oﬀer a very ﬂavorful and nourishing addition to the congee. Add 
them to the back pot of simmering water so their broth is cooked into the congee, then spoon 
three scallops into each bowl. 
If ﬁsh is too strong for you in the morning, used mashed sweet potato, butternut squash or 
corn niblets; mix 1/4 cup into each bowl of congee. 
Find your own favorite. It’s not a quick-cook breakfast, but it isn’t diﬃcult. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Congee is a very important dish. It’s good to become familiar with it and to learn how to adapt 
it with condiments and proteins. As a rice dish, its principal function is to soothe stomach and 
all of digestion (upper small intestine as well as pancreas-spleen). Congee provides support to 
the stomach’s protective lining (stomach yin), while it rests and resets pancreas-spleen function. 
Because all the water in congee is gradually absorbed, congee is considered more beneﬁcial to

<!-- p.187 -->
Congee (Rice Porridge)      181
restoring hydration than drinking plain water. Chronic dehydration that isn’t noticed under-
lies many deep health problems. Te stomach is the central hub of all ﬂuids and their distri-
bution throughout the body. Congee and the congee cooking method is especially important 
for restoring deep hydration.
Congee is a template to be used in many ways. Use the condiments and extras to tune what 
your congee supports. For the kidneys and constitutional health, use eggs, ﬁsh and peanuts, 
or sliced pork. Butternut squash or mashed corn niblets speciﬁcally aid a weak metabolism. 
Pickled vegetables (another Chinatown ingredient) are traditionally added to communicate 
with the liver. Add adzuki beans to connect the heart above with the kidneys below (while 
strengthening digestive functions of the middle as well). Seaweed and/or mushrooms are often 
added (both resonate with kidney and bladder). A discussion of congee wouldn’t be complete 
without noting that congee is the go-to hangover cure in China and Hong Kong. 
Add congee to your breakfast rotation. Once you get to know it, its gentleness becomes its 
beauty. Have it when feeling under the weather, or have it every day for health maintenance. 
It can be a staple food. 
If digestion is very compromised, congee may be too easy to digest, causing symptoms of 
blood sugar spiking or distention from gassiness in the middle torso. Tis may be a sign of 
SIBO, overgrowth of yeast or bacteria in the upper small intestine along with weakened pan-
creas-spleen function. Healing will require another strategy. For most, however, congee is the 
gentlest comfort food possible, a beautiful anchor to a day.

<!-- p.188 -->
182        Breakfast Foods
Millet Porridge
When I traveled in China, my breakfasts alternated between rice congee 
and millet porridge. Millet has been a staple grain for thousands of years, 
particularly in northern cities like Beijing. It makes a stunningly simple and 
delicious breakfast, nourishing and very easy to digest. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 5 minutes
Total time: 45 minutes
Ingredients
millet (organic if possible) | 1 cup
grapeseed or other oil | 2 Tbsp
sea salt | 1/2 tsp
water (can be heated to speed cooking) | 5-8 cups
Directions
As mentioned in chapter 3, we need to wash millet before cooking. To do so, put the measure 
of millet in a pot, ﬂood with two or three times as much water, let the grains settle to the bot-
tom, slowly tip the water over the side. Repeat three times, or until the water runs clear. Ten, 
pour through a strainer to prepare for dry-roasting the millet (optional). 
Add the washed millet back into the pot, turn heat to medium-high, stir until dry. Continue 
roasting the dry millet over the heat until the grains turn slightly darker tan and emit a gentle 
and appealing toasty aroma. When you’re new to dry-roasting millet, keep a small bowl of raw 
millet on the cooking counter for color comparison. Te diﬀerence is slight but noticeable. If 
you’re in a hurry, it is ﬁne to skip the dry-roasting step.
Next, add the oil, stirring so each grain is coated. Add the pinch of salt. Carefully add enough 
hot water to cover the grain (like the congee method). Stir as the grains absorb the ﬁrst water. 
Add more water. Reduce heat to a simmer. Millet porridge is more forgiving than rice con-
gee—constant stirring is not required. Occasional stirring with the addition of more cooking

<!-- p.189 -->
Millet Porridge        183
water will do. As with congee, it is done when there is starchy water between fully soft-cooked 
grains and the millet won’t absorb any more water. Te exact amount of water to use is hard to 
say, as some evaporates during cooking. Steamed millet takes only 15-20 minutes, but it takes 
45 minutes for millet to develop into porridge (even more cooking time won’t hurt). Te pot 
can simmer while you do other things; give it a stir once in a while to check on it. 
Serve with condiments and additions in the same way as congee. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Some people object to rice congee, noting that it is mostly water and the grain is white rice 
instead of whole grain brown rice. (It’s true that congee is not nutrient-dense. Rather, its value 
is in the way it beneﬁts the organs of digestion.)  Millet porridge answers that objection beau-
tifully. Millet porridge is a whole grain dish, and therefore includes more protein, minerals, 
vitamins and ﬁber. Te accompaniments to millet porridge can be simpler—perhaps poached 
egg, some sliced scallion and slivered ginger. Follow your own tastes and intuition. 
In northern China, a huge pot of millet porridge often sits next to an equally huge pot of 
congee in the entryway to casual breakfast joints. Te intense steam and fantastic aroma are 
irresistibly inviting. You select one or the other, point to some condiments and sit down for a 
great (and amazingly inexpensive) breakfast. Formal congee joints oﬀer dozens of options, and 
at hotel breakfasts you can pick your own fancy little things to go on top. But nothing really 
improves on a simple bowl of congee or millet porridge with a little of this or that thrown in 
to aim the beneﬁts and to provide a bit of protein.

<!-- p.190 -->
184        Breakfast Foods
Basic Morning Oats
Oatmeal porridge is the familiar“wet breakfast” in the West, as rice congee 
and sometimes millet porridge are in Asia. Oats have achieved food-fad sta-
tus for reducing cholesterol through promoting elimination, but they also help 
metabolic issues and calm the spirit.
Serves: four
Prep time: 10 minutes
Total time: 20-40 minutes
Ingredients
steel-cut or rolled oats | 1 cup  
water | 3 cups for steel-cut oats, 2 cups for rolled oats
salt | pinch
Ingredient Note: Steel-cut oats are whole oat kernels that are cut across the 
grain to allow faster cooking. For my taste, this treatment provides more 
ﬂavor and a better texture than rolled oat porridge. Rolled oats are whole 
oats that have been steamed and pressed between steel rollers to expose more 
surface area for even quicker cooking.  Old-fashioned rolled oats are whole 
oats that are put through slightly wider rollers, leaving a chewy texture.  
Quick-cooking oats are whole oats that have been steamed longer and pressed 
more thinly.  Instant oats are not whole oats—they have been reﬁned for fast 
cooking and should be avoided. 
Directions
Cook 1 cup steel-cut oats with 3 cups water and a pinch of salt. (You can adapt the portions, 
knowing that you want a 3:1 ration of water to steel-cut oats.) 
Bring to a boil, reduce to simmer, stirring occasionally to prevent sticking. Cook 30-40 min-
utes, uncovered or loosely lidded to avoid boil-over. Flavor and digestion beneﬁts improve 
with somewhat longer cooking.

<!-- p.191 -->
Basic Morning Oats       185
To save time and make oatmeal even easier to digest, you may soak steel-cut oats overnight 
in cool water and cook in the morning in the soaking water or fresh water (refrigeration not 
necessary except in very hot weather). Pre-soaked steel-cut oats will cook in about 20 minutes. 
Cook old-fashioned rolled oats for about 10-15 minutes in two measures water to one of oats, 
although a bit more water and a bit longer cooking time improves the porridge. Quick-cook-
ing oats (not recommended) require only 5-10 minutes cooking. 
Serve oats with fresh or dried fruit depending on the season. Cinnamon is a warming spice, 
wonderful in cool weather or to energize morning digestion in any season. With raisins or 
currants and cinnamon, the desire for sweetener will be lessened. Almond milk adds moistness 
and gentle sweetness. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Like rice and millet, oats harmonize the stomach with the pancreas-spleen, strongly aiding di-
gestion. Oats relieve indigestion, bloating, and constipation. Tey treat diarrhea, too—perhaps 
counterintuitive, but often the intestines need to be cleared of what is oﬀending them in order to 
end a cycle of diarrhea. Oats provide the ﬁber to do this while also being gelatinous and sooth-
ing. For best health, use more water with oatmeal porridge—most people make it too thick. 
Until the 1970s, oats were sold in apothecaries or drug stores more than food markets. Ten 
they achieved superfood status for the way their soluble ﬁber binds with cholesterol to carry it 
out through the intestines. Perhaps more importantly, oats improve the way insulin is utilized 
(improving metabolism) and can help calm the spirit (shen in Chinese medical language, a 
combination of heart, emotions and sense of identity). 
Oatmeal porridge is commonly served with sweeteners such as brown sugar, honey or maple 
syrup. Te cook’s attention should be focused on bringing the beneﬁts of oats into the diet 
without adding so much sweetener that those beneﬁts are undone. By understanding clearly 
what these beneﬁts are—soothing digestion, regulating insulin levels, calming the spirit, pro-
moting peristalsis—we can enjoy more simply prepared oatmeal breakfasts rather than eating 
it as merely a platform for morning sugar delivery.

<!-- p.192 -->
186        Breakfast Foods
Homemade Granola
Nuts, fruits and grains roasted together have been found in archeological cook 
sites around the Mediterranean and in North America. Granola is so easy to 
make and so superior when fresh that there is no need to buy it from a store.
Serves: ten
Prep time: 10-15 minutes
Total time: 40-50 minutes
Ingredients
old-fashioned rolled oats | 3 cups
almonds | 1 cup (whole or sliced)
sunﬂower seeds | 3/4 cup
pumpkin seeds (pepita) | 3/4 cup
cinnamon | 1.5 tsp
nutmeg | several scrapes
salt | 1/2 tsp
grapeseed oil | 2/3 cup
maple syrup (or honey) | 1/4 cup
barley malt syrup (or rice malt syrup) | 2/3 cup
vanilla extract | 1-2 tsp
dried currants (or raisins) | 3/4 cup
Directions
In a large bowl, mix the dry ingredients, except the currants or raisins. Hold them for later.
In a smaller bowl, whisk together the wet ingredients.
Pour the wet mixture over the dry and combine so each grain and seed is well coated. 
Spread the mix on parchment paper in a baking pan, bake at 325˚F until brown. Turn with

<!-- p.193 -->
Homemade Granola       187
a spoon, and continue baking until the newly turned bits have also browned, perhaps 30-45 
minutes all told. A hotter oven can be used—up to 375˚F—but be sure to turn more often to 
prevent burning. In the last 10 minutes of cooking (or after cooking) mix in the currants or 
raisins. 
For crunchier granola, allow to cool and bake brieﬂy again for ten minutes, stirring once. 
Allow granola to cool fully before storing in airtight containers. Homemade granola stays fresh 
for at least two weeks. 
Use as breakfast cereal with milk or nut-milk, as a ﬁnger snack, or over yogurt. 
To make a granola bark that holds together more like a granola bar, use honey as the sweetener 
and butter as the fat. Mix the ingredients in the evening, ﬂatten ﬁrmly into the parchment 
lined baking pan. Refrigerate. Bake in the morning slower and longer than basic granola (use 
250˚F for 60-75 minutes, until ﬁrm and golden). Allow to cool fully. Rather than cutting into 
neat rectangles resembling commercial granola bars, it’s nicer to break this bark irregularly. If 
you prefer regularity, lightly score rectangles into the granola before baking. Carry in sealed 
bags for snacking, if it makes it that far. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Granola—whole grains roasted with nuts, fruits, honey, spices and oil—emerged as an iconic 
food of the 1960s but has roots in food practice from the beginning of civilization both around 
the Mediterranean and in the Americas. Granola straddles foraging and farming, nuts and 
grains. 
Even healthy sweet snacks like granola can be problematic if made too sweet and eaten in 
excess, but in moderation, homemade granola can be a healthful treat for anyone who can 
eat grain. Because the sweetness is accompanied by oats and nuts, peristalsis is well supported 
and stagnation won’t arise. Avoid if digestion/metabolism is too taxed to handle any carbs, for 
example, in cases of SIBO or some versions of hypoglycemia. 
 Once you understand the food energetics, substitutions can be made to customize the dish for

<!-- p.194 -->
188        Breakfast Foods
taste and health purposes. Almonds connect with lungs and large intestine, good for wheezing 
and other respiratory issues. Sunﬂower seeds and pumpkin seeds relate more directly to diges-
tion, they connect with stomach and pancreas-spleen. Currants or raisins add a liver energetic 
note. Honey and barley malt syrup are fairly safe sweeteners; barley malt clears food stagnation 
and functions as a sweet that contains no liver-taxing fructose. Oats themselves connect with 
stomach and pancreas-spleen, soothe digestion and also strongly support peristalsis and elimi-
nation. Overall, this version of granola harmonizes digestion and helps things move properly. 
But when you bake it at home, all you will  think about is the enticing aroma and beautiful 
taste.

<!-- p.195 -->
Skillet Cornbread       189
Skillet Cornbread (and muffins)
Cornbread is an especially deep comfort food. If you have roots in the Ameri-
can South, you know it is also an iconic heritage dish. With great ingredients 
and fresh from the oven, the appeal of skillet cornbread is universal. Tis 
recipe ignores the trend toward sweet, cake-like cornbread, featuring instead 
the rustic taste and texture of the artisanal coarse cornmeals that are coming 
to market again. 
Serves: eight
Prep time: 10 minutes
Total time: 30 minutes
Ingredients
cornmeal (coarse grind) | 2.5 cups
baking powder | 1.5 tsp
salt | 3/4 tsp
egg | 1
whole milk | 1.5 cups
butter (unsalted) | 4 Tbsp, melted, plus 1 Tbsp cold butter
Directions
Preheat oven to 400˚F. Use a cast iron (or heavy enamel iron) pan if possible (medium size). 
Preheat the pan on the stovetop over medium heat. 
In one bowl, mix the dry ingredients: cornmeal, baking powder and salt. 
In a separate bowl, whisk together the wet ingredients: egg, milk and melted butter. 
Whisk the wet ingredients into the dry. Allow the cornmeal to absorb wet ingredients by let-
ting it rest for ten minutes or more. 
Ten, working quickly, add enough cold butter to the hot skillet to coat it well. Immediately 
pour in the batter. Carefully move the skillet directly into the hot oven. Bake about 20 min-

<!-- p.196 -->
190        Breakfast Foods
utes; the cornbread is done when the top is browned and a probe exits cleanly. Do not over-
cook, as true cornbread can get dry rather easily. 
Remove and let cool enough to handle. Invert the skillet onto a plate for cutting. Slice the 
cornbread into wedges while still steaming, and eat right away. Adding butter is traditional, 
but it’s wonderful without. Pair cornbread with a good quality tea. 
To make Corn Muﬃns, adapt the recipe as follows: 
1.5 cups cornmeal
1 cup organic wheat ﬂour (or non-glutinous substitute)
1.5 tsp baking powder
1/2 tsp salt
2 eggs
1/8 cup barley malt (equivalent to 2 Tbsp)
1/8 cup honey or maple syrup
1 cup milk or non-dairy ‘milk’
1/4 cup yogurt
1 Tbsp olive oil
Mix together the dry ingredients, and separately mix the wet. Combine wet into dry (do not 
over mix). Fill batter into oiled muﬃn wells or paper baking cups, leaving 1/3 of each cup 
empty for rising. Bake in preheated 400˚F oven for 15 minutes or until a probe exits cleanly. 
Cool slightly, serve as is or with butter. Makes 12 medium-size muﬃns. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Polenta and grits are examples of wet-cooked corn dishes (and excellent for breakfast, too—
have polenta or grits with eggs for an excellent wet corn breakfast). Te wet-cooking methods 
are more supportive to the organs of digestion and their good functions (stomach, pancre-
as-spleen, and small intestine). Cornbread is baked, a dry-cooking method that changes the 
focus. Corn still supports the belly, but the cooking method is more associated with the lungs.

<!-- p.197 -->
Skillet Cornbread        191
Lungs need support during the cold and ﬂu time of year (and before the hay-fever season). 
Skillet cornbread ﬁts well into autumn and winter eating, for breakfast, snack or appetizer. 
Corn is native to the Americas but spread incredibly quickly after Columbus brought kernels 
to Europe after his second voyage. Corn was well known as far away as China only seventy-ﬁve 
years after that. Te masters of Chinese medicine recognized the beneﬁts of corn for digestion, 
the stomach and the metabolic glands of pancreas-spleen. Tey also noted a special connection 
to the kidneys and the association with the reproductive system, what we would call today 
genetics and fertility, including the idea of ancestral lineage. 
Many artisanal or heritage corn types are being grown and milled now, often locally. Search 
them out, it is more than worth the eﬀort. Recipe proportions may need slight adjustment 
based on corn type. And one ﬁnal note: most modern corn is genetically modiﬁed and can be 
subject to particularly heavy pesticide use. Terefore, choosing organically grown corn is very 
important. (Buying organic is also especially important for wheat and soy.)

<!-- p.198 -->
192        Breakfast Foods
GF Pancakes (gluten-free)
I’m often asked how to make tasty gluten-free pancakes, something for a re-
laxed weekend morning. Te secret is not to imitate pancake house fare, but 
to make something better. Tese aren’t about being gluten-free, they’re just 
good. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 10 minutes
Total time: 30 minutes
Ingredients
buckwheat ﬂour | 1.5 cups
cornmeal (ﬂour) | 1.5 cups
cinnamon (ground) | 1/2 tsp
baking powder | 1 tsp
salt | 1/2 tsp
coconut oil | 1/2 cup
eggs (optional) | 1 or 2 medium 
honey | 2 Tbsp
vanilla extract | 1/2 tsp
water | 1.75 cups, more as needed
Directions
Mix the dry ingredients in a large bowl. Mix the wet ingredients in a smaller bowl. 
Make a well in the dry mix; add the wet into the well, and gradually mix the sides down into 
the wet until the batter is well-combined. 
Allow the pan to heat thoroughly. When a drop of water tossed from your ﬁngers dances 
when ﬂicked onto the pan, it is ready for the batter. (Many use nonstick pans; much better 
is cast iron or enamel-coated iron. Stainless steel pans include nickel and chromium to ward

<!-- p.199 -->
Gluten-Free Pancakes        193
oﬀ rusting, but the presence of nickel allows oil to stick, causing poor pancake performance.) 
Add enough oil just to coat. I like coconut oil for cooking pancakes, but grapeseed or other 
similar oil works well too. Put a spoon of coconut oil in the pan, spread it around, then ladle 
in a medium size scoop of batter for each pancake. Do not overcrowd the pan. 
Allow the edges of each cake to ﬁrm and some bubbles to show in the middle before ﬂipping. 
Timing will vary according to batter speciﬁcs. Experiment at ﬁrst to get your rhythm. 
Serve with eggs, or sliced fruit, or leftover Stewed Dried Fruit (recipe on page 163). 
Cook’s Note on Baking Pancakes   
Since ﬂour is constantly changing with humidity, mixing a batter requires some impro-
visation. For pancakes, use a thin batter compared to the consistency needed for cookies, 
breads or cakes. Pancake batter should pour oﬀ a spoon or ladle with a certain viscosity, 
more or less the way honey pours. If the batter is too thick, the pancakes won’t cook well. 
Baking is often considered to be a fussy aspect of cooking. Tere is chemistry involved 
and precision is needed, a sense that we must follow instructions to the letter. Why, for 
example, do some recipes call for mixing the dry into the wet and others the wet into the 
dry?  What esoteric witchcraft is involved here?  Shall we murmur incantations as we stir 
13 times to the left during the waxing moon but 11 times to the right when the moon 
wanes?
It’s not so complicated as that, alas. Te dry and wet ingredients are best mixed separately 
before combining to ensure that the baking powder (for example) spreads evenly through 
the mixture. Te purpose of mixing wet into dry or vice versa is merely to avoid lumps, 
without the need to work the batter too much (which would prevent rising by overdevel-
oping the gluten, not a problem here, as there is none). For these pancakes, I sometimes 
do throw everything into one bowl, cowboy style, then bring it together by whisking 
the wet ﬁrst then incorporating the dry, but best practice remains separate mixing as 
described above. Let the batter rest a few minutes so the moisture can be fully absorbed 
(refrigeration is often recommended but is not required for busy people).

<!-- p.200 -->
194       Breakfast Foods
A Note on the Food Energetics
Pancakes are not an important food, but the question comes up again and again from those 
who avoid gluten,  “Can you tell me how to make gluten-free pancakes so I can feel normal, 
once in a while, please?”  Te answer is yes, and they can be wonderful, but they’ll always be 
a bit diﬀerent than common wheat pancakes. With good ingredients and some care, they can 
be better. 
Standard pancakes, of course, are made with wheat ﬂour. Wheat is a strengthening, warming 
grain. Some people can’t eat any gluten, and many more have some degree of intolerance to it, 
or simply avoid it because it often increases phlegm and a strong sense of tiredness. Do a little 
detective work to see if wheat bothers you or not. Tose with only slight symptoms may do 
well with strictly organic wheat or the antique wheats (spelt, kamut, emmer or einkorn, easily 
available as ground ﬂour, ready for pancake experiments). To include antique wheat ﬂour in 
these pancakes, substitute it for either the buckwheat or cornmeal in the recipe here. Other 
grains will have diﬀerent attributes. 
Corn and buckwheat (the combination in this recipe) work well together. Cornmeal can be 
a bit dry when baked, but the buckwheat moistens and holds the pancake together very well 
(even as it helps break up stuck areas internally).
To avoid corn (a fairly common allergen), substitute millet ﬂour. 
To avoid eggs, omit them and add 1/4 cup rice ﬂour and an equal amount extra water. 
We make GF pancakes occasionally on a weekend morning. Sliced fruit and a sprinkle of 
cinnamon makes them sweet enough. Tere is a bit of honey in the batter to help them brown 
appealingly as they cook, but if more is needed, a very moderate drip of maple syrup or honey 
will do. Adding protein slows the quick absorption of that sugar which might otherwise cause 
blood sugar spikes and crashes. Eating protein with breakfast eﬀectively lowers the glycemic 
index of the pancakes. Common breakfast proteins include eggs, sausage or bacon, smoked 
salmon, plain yogurt or even tofu. Adding any protein slows the metabolic stress of consuming 
sugar, but strains digestion in other ways for exactly the same reason. For too many people, 
pancakes are little more than chewy delivery vehicles for morning sugar. It doesn’t have to be

<!-- p.201 -->
Gluten-Free Pancakes        195
so; savory pancakes are common in Japan and Korea, made with scallion, ginger, shrimp and 
squid. But in the typical American breakfast pancake, there is sugar in the batter and often 
syrup on top. Imitation syrup (labeled “pancake syrup”) is largely high fructose corn syrup 
(renamed as fructose or corn sugar to sound more wholesome). Tis is a toxic processed food 
product that should be avoided by everyone. Look at it this way—if you really love sugar, eat it 
moderately to protect your pancreas-spleen health so that you can continue to enjoy the sweet 
life. What this means for pancakes is to use real maple syrup or honey but only in moderation. 
Pancakes, even if gluten-free, are a comfort food treat. By redeﬁning them as a way to eat 
quality grains rather than a boat to deliver sugar to our blood, pancakes can be a part of very 
good eating.

<!-- p.202 -->
196        Breakfast Foods
Buckwheat Crepes
Crepes are an elegant pancake thin enough to wrap fresh fruit or smoked 
salmon for a delicious breakfast or snack. Tis recipe can make large crepes, 
or the small blini that we use with caviar for special occasions. 
Serves: four
Prep time: 10 minutes
Total time: 30 minutes
Ingredients
buckwheat ﬂour | 1 cup
eggs | 3
whole milk | 2 cups
butter | 3 Tbsp, melted
agave syrup (or honey) | 1 Tbsp
salt | pinch
coconut oil | enough to just coat pan lightly
Directions
Mix the ingredients together until the consistency is like heavy cream. All ﬂours are diﬀerent, 
adjust if needed with a bit more ﬂour or water. Crepe batter should hold together, but is dis-
tinctly thinner than pancake batter (to allow a thin crepe). Gently stir the batter with the ladle 
before pouring each crepe to ensure consistency. 
Warm a wide, ﬂat pan over medium-high heat (use a crepe pan if you have one). Add just 
enough coconut oil to spread around the full surface. Ladle a measure of batter into the middle 
of the pan. Quickly pick the pan up to spread the batter using gravity by rolling the pan to 
guide the batter into shape. With a little practice, you’ll know how much batter will make a 
thin crepe in your pan. Return to heat, cook until the edges are just dry and the batter just set. 
With a spatula, gently lift and ﬂip the crepe. Te second side needs very little cooking time.

<!-- p.203 -->
Buckwheat Crepes       197
Remove the crepe to a plate to serve, or keep warm in a 175˚F oven. Cook each crepe in turn, 
adding more oil to the pan only when needed, if at all. 
Serve with fresh or preserved fruit for a sweet crepe. For a main meal, serve with eggs, sau-
sage, or smoked salmon. In our home, small buckwheat blini (same recipe) served with crème 
fraiche (French-style fermented cream) and sustainably farmed American caviar is a traditional 
birthday breakfast. 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Despite its name, buckwheat is gluten-free and not related to wheat. (It is not a true grain in 
its botanical classiﬁcation, but it is certainly used as a grain in the kitchen.)  
Crepes are fun food, not as beneﬁcial as a basic meal of steamed grains, greens and a protein, 
but if your diet is well-balanced and you don’t need a therapeutic approach, they are a remark-
ably easy special treat. Feel free to replace the milk with water. Be careful that crepes don’t 
become merely a vehicle for sugar; if using good ingredients, the natural ﬂavors and hint of 
sweetness provided by the agave can be wonderful enough.

<!-- p.204 -->
198        Breakfast Foods
Breakfast Eggs, Five Methods
Eggs are the reproductive eﬀorts of chickens. Tey connect with our own con-
stitutional level: kidneys, reproduction, bones, bone marrow, and brain. Eggs 
support hormones, moisten dryness, and are calming to the spirit, like drop-
ping an anchor to steady ourselves. Tey can be comforting in the morning at 
the same time that they strengthen and invigorate us for an active day. 
A newspaper interview with a celebrity chef caught my eye. He had closed his award-winning 
restaurant while a more ambitious one was being built. It was a busy time with investors, 
lawyers, building contractors and so forth. Now that he wasn’t working at night, the chef was 
enjoying cooking breakfast for his family. In a generous mood, he confessed feeling shocked to 
discover he wasn’t good at cooking eggs. He quickly developed skills, but the story points out
