the diﬀerences between restaurant chefs and home cooks, at least in America. 
In French cooking schools, training begins only with eggs. Since eggs visibly change as they 
cook, the aspiring chef can see cooking take place. When you understand eggs, you can then 
discern how far along a piece of ﬁsh or meat is in the center as it is cooking. To improve any 
cooking skill, master various methods of cooking eggs. 
Te major methods are frying (whole or scrambled), boiling, poaching, and baking. Salt-pre-
serving is the ﬁfth major method. While rare in the West, preserved eggs are daily fare for 
much of China. Each method connects with one of the Five Elemental Phases and their asso-
ciated internal organ system. Tis is described below. Brief instructions follow for eggs in each 
of these methods:
 » Wood Element cooking method: frying
 » Earth Element cooking method: boiling
 » Water Element cooking method: preserving
 » Fire Element cooking method: swirling or puﬃng
 » Metal Element cooking method: baking

<!-- p.205 -->
Breakfast Eggs, Five Methods       199
Fried Eggs  
Frying is the Wood Element cooking method that connects with the liver system.
For sunny side up, heat a heavy pan over medium heat (nonstick not necessary). Add 1 Tbsp 
butter for each egg to be cooked. Crack eggs with the back of a knife (not the side of the pan) 
and gently add each egg to the pan after the butter has sizzled but before it quiets and browns. 
Te goal is to allow the white to set while controlling just how runny the yolk remains. Here’s 
where techniques vary. 
 » For a short-order cook’s technique, use a butter knife to gently draw the white clos-
est to the yolk down toward the pan. Tis allows the white to cook more quickly, 
protecting some runny quality of the yolk while having all of the white set and 
become opaque. 
 » For French technique (we call it hand-cooking the egg), use 2 Tbsp butter for each egg 
(it won’t all be eaten). Tip the pan slightly, gather the hot butter with a long-handled 
spoon and baste (cover) the egg with it. Repeat the process until the egg is ready. Te 
egg will cook precisely where you aim the hot butter (olive oil works well, too). Clas-
sically, the hot butter is spooned over the white repeatedly but over the yolk only 
once or twice. Tis method allows the most control and the most sophisticated and 
luxurious egg. Serve with a pinch of ﬂaky salt and crack of fresh pepper as desired. 
 » If cooking more than two eggs at once, use a lid, with or without 1 Tbsp water for 
steaming. Check often, perhaps every 30 seconds, to catch the eggs as the whites set 
but before overcooking. Pour melted pan butter over the eggs as they are plated if 
you’d like a bit more luxury.
For over-easy eggs, begin as above, but gently turn the eggs when they have set enough to stay 
together. Again, the goal is to cook the white while leaving the yolk slightly runny. My family 
asks for a touch of crunchiness, so I cook them in butter that is quite hot, develop a bit of 
crunch, then turn them for only 5-10 seconds before serving. A gentler approach is also good: 
use lower heat and 1 minute on the second side before serving. 
If you prefer eggs cooked through (white and yolk fully set), your job is easy—use any of these 
methods and continue until there is no wiggle in any area.

<!-- p.206 -->
200      Breakfast Foods
Scrambled Eggs
Scrambled eggs are pan-cooked beaten eggs. You can cook them hot and fast or slowly with 
low heat. 
 »  Crack two or more eggs into a bowl. Season with salt and herbs at this point (I like 
to use thyme with eggs because the cooling property of thyme pairs well with the 
energetically warming eggs). Whip or whisk vigorously enough to add plenty of air 
to the eggs for ﬂuﬃness.  
 »  Heat a wide pan to medium-low heat. For a creamier result, use the lowest heat 
possible. Melt butter in the pan, add the eggs, allow them to set for just 1 minute 
(less if your pan is hotter). 
 »  With a wooden spoon or spatula, gently push the eggs from one side of the pan to 
the other, repeating only until all the visibly runny egg has touched heat. Advanced 
practitioners can melt the butter, add the eggs, turn the heat oﬀ. In less than a 
minute, as the eggs are setting, turn the heat back on, and move the eggs enough so 
the wet part now touches the pan, and again, turn the heat oﬀ. Move to the plate. 
 »  Plate when still underdone, after about two minutes in the pan (the eggs will con-
tinue to set from residual heat). As with nearly all egg dishes, the sooner they go 
from pan to being eaten the more special they will be. 
 »  Serve with ﬂaky salt and fresh cracked pepper if you like. Grain of paradise is won-
derful over eggs, as is rosemary, oregano or tarragon (fresh or dried), which can be 
added during beating or at the table. 
Eggs with Ginger, Tamari, Black Vinegar is a classic postpartum dish from the therapeutic cook-
ing tradition. Fantastic for helping new mothers recover after childbirth (too often taken for 
granted in modern culture), this dish can be enjoyed by anyone (use somewhat less vinegar 
and tamari if your life doesn’t include having given birth in the past month).  
 » Cut 3 slices of fresh ginger then sliver them into long, thin strips. Beat 2-3 organic 
eggs in a bowl. Add the Shanxi vinegar, 1 tsp for common use, 1-2 Tbsp for new 
mothers (Chinatown ingredient).  Add the same amount of tamari. Beat together. 
Tis dish is cooked hotter than most scrambled eggs; the heat develops ﬂavors with 
the tamari and vinegar. Heat a pan, add some butter (or oil), add the ginger, add 
the seasoned eggs. Let the ingredients sizzle for a few seconds, not long, then stir

<!-- p.207 -->
Breakfast Eggs, Five Methods       201
until nearly set. Serve while steaming. Pair with a good bread or rice and sautéed 
vegetables. (Look for true Shanxi vinegar; it is available for only pennies more than 
imitations that are merely poor vinegar with food coloring and preservatives.)  Chives 
or scallions can be included.
Omelet 
Te omelet (omelette in French) is the grand example of understanding egg cookery. It’s com-
mon for a young cook to make an omelet for the head chef when applying for a job; I’ve 
even known sommeliers to be required to cook an omelet for the chef in order to be hired as 
apprentice wine steward. Te perfect omelet is simply plain beaten eggs, chopped herbs and 
perhaps one delicate ﬁlling, but some cooks prefer to add milk and more elaborate ﬁllings to 
the beaten eggs. 
 » Beat 2-3 eggs (best to allow eggs to warm to room temperature ﬁrst).
 »  Add a generous amount of butter to your pan, heating until it sizzles and bubbles, 
but do not let it brown.
 »  Add the eggs to the pan. Tip the pan slightly so the egg spreads all around. Follow-
ing French technique, push the egg to the center as it sets, roll the pan slightly to 
spread out the still raw egg to so that all the runny part can touch the pan. Once 
the egg is set underneath but still silky and somewhat runny on top, add the herbs 
such as chopped parsley or tarragon and your choice of ﬁllings, perhaps grilled 
mushrooms that have been set aside. Spread the herbs and ﬁllings evenly on one 
half or one third of the pan. 
 »  With a spatula, lift the corner close to where you’ve added ﬁlling. Either turn over 
in half or use the rolling technique: roll the edge over the ﬁlling, continue to roll 
that over the rest of the egg until you reach the other edge of the pan. Gently tip the 
omelet onto a plate, add more parsley and plate the accompaniments: toasted wheat 
or gluten-free bread, potatoes, rice, or whatever you like. For a dinner omelet, try 
serving with peas or asparagus and sweet potato. 
Soft- or Hard-Boiled Eggs
Boiling is the Earth Element cooking method that connects with the stomach 
and pancreas-spleen systems.

<!-- p.208 -->
202       Breakfast Foods
Soft-boiled eggs are gentle to eat and much easier to digest than pan-fried eggs. 
 »  Allow eggs to warm to room temperature before cooking. Te initial temperature 
of the egg is very signiﬁcant for boiled eggs because we can’t see inside the shell to 
know how it is transforming with the heat. Since the boiling temperature of water 
is constant, the variables for the cook are only cooking duration, temperature of the 
egg before cooking, size of the egg, and of course, the quality of eggs you’ve selected. 
 » Bring water to a gentle boil, lower eggs into the hot bath with a spoon. 
 »  Cook medium-size room-temperature eggs for 3.5 minutes. With experience, you 
will quickly know your preference for the size eggs you like. Te goal of soft-boiled 
eggs is softly set whites with warm, runny yolks. How runny is up to personal pref-
erence. Use a kitchen timer (cell phone timer for most people these days) to keep 
track of your personal preferences. 
 » Serve with good bread, oat porridge, millet porridge or congee. 
 Hard-boiled eggs are a traditional high-protein, no-mess portable snack. And, they can be 
cooked with refrigerator-cold eggs if you have no time to allow them to come to room tem-
perature. 
 »  Place eggs in cold water, turn on heat, bring to a boil. Just when the pot comes to a 
boil, start your timer, cook for 8 minutes, a little less or more for desired ﬁrmness. 
Although there is some variation here, the eggs will be cooked through. 
 »  Stop further cooking from residual heat by dunking into cold water. Pour out the 
cooking water, ﬁll the pot with cool, wait a minute then repeat until the eggs no 
longer heat the cool water. Tis prevents the yolks from turning greenish. Eat them 
still warm or carry with you on your day.
Poached Eggs  
Water cooking is the method that most supports stomach and pancreas-spleen, the central 
organs of digestion, and poached eggs are the easiest to digest. Te softness of poached eggs 
and their rich fats are soothing for the intestines and supportive for the kidneys and hormones. 
Water cooking doesn’t stress the liver the way that pan-frying can. Te diﬀerence is important. 
Eggs have been controversial, considered nearly taboo for two generations for fear that they

<!-- p.209 -->
Breakfast Eggs, Five Methods       203
contributed to heart disease. Now eggs have been exonerated, arteriosclerosis being attributed 
to inﬂammation (cholesterol itself is known to be a necessary component of good health, cre-
ated by our own liver if not suﬃciently present in our diet). As research continues, poached 
eggs will be found to be signiﬁcantly less inﬂammatory than pan-fried eggs, even adjusting 
for the added fat used to cook fried eggs. Poached eggs don’t arouse protective wei qi (roughly 
analogous to the immune response) to the same degree that fried eggs are likely to do. (By the 
same token, fried eggs oﬀer more invigorating support for facing a big day, looking at this from 
another point of view.)  Both cooking methods have their time and place. For anyone with 
autoimmune issues, allergies, migraines, irritability, or hypertension, choosing poached rather 
than fried eggs will make a very positive diﬀerence.
 »  Bring 2 inches of water to boil. For a single egg, use a small saucepan, but for up to 
four at a time, use a lower, wider pan. Cast iron or enamel is good, as egg proteins 
tend to stick to stainless steel. Add 1 tsp salt and 2 tsp white wine vinegar to the 
poaching water. Although not absolutely necessary, the vinegar helps the egg white 
stay focused rather than feathering out. 
 »  Gently crack each egg into the water, holding it close so it can gently fall into place. 
Rather than spin the water as is sometimes recommended, simply place each egg 
in its own area. Maintain heat on medium, but avoid a rolling boil as that would 
move the eggs too much. 
 »  If the egg was room temperature, 2-3 minutes cooking will do; 3-4 if starting with a 
cold egg. In either case, you can watch the egg cook, knowing with some precision 
how loose or ﬁrm it is. 
 »  Remove gently with a slotted spoon, allowing the water to drip oﬀ, and place direct-
ly into a bowl of congee, millet porridge, soft polenta or on toasted bread. Season 
and garnish as desired. 
Baked Eggs 
Baking is the Metal Element cooking method that connects with the lung and 
large intestine systems.
Teoretically, any food can be cooked in any of the ﬁve principal cooking methods: boiling/
steaming, baking, preserving, frying/sautéing, and, for lack of an egg-cooking term, expand-

<!-- p.210 -->
204      Breakfast Foods
ing-puﬃng (more on that below). Just as French chef training begins with egg cookery, so we 
can use eggs to practice thinking in the ﬁve traditional cooking methods, each associated with 
an internal organ system in classical Five Phase or Five Element theory.

We’ve discussed frying (the Wood Elemental Phase method), and boiling (the Earth Elemental 
Phase method). Baking eggs (the Metal Elemental Phase) is common in some cultures while 
rare in others. Quiche is perhaps the most widespread baked egg dish. Recipes for many quiche 
types are easy to ﬁnd. As another example, here is how to make baked Spanish tortilla. Not to 
be confused with the corn tortilla ﬂatbread (or chips) from Mexico, this tortilla is a share-size 
baked omelet, a daily staple all over Spain. 
 »  In an ovenproof iron or enamel skillet, cook 2 pounds sliced potatoes and a large 
leek (white part only) with ample olive oil. Add a generous chef’s pinch of sea salt. 
Using medium heat, turn potatoes frequently to help cooking but avoid burning. 
When tender but not falling apart, remove from pan and set aside. (Potato is most 
traditional, but this is a family-style dish, feel free to improvise with cauliﬂower and 
chard, for example.)  
 »  Beat 8 eggs in a large bowl, add a bit of salt or tamari (1-2 tsp salt all told). Whisk 
the eggs vigorously to incorporate air. Add the cooked potatoes and leek, and your 
choice of herbs, mix gently. 
 »  Look at your skillet. If too much olive oil remains after cooking the potatoes, 
remove most of it, if too little, add enough to make 2-3 Tbsp. Heat the pan over 
medium-high ﬂame. When hot, add the egg-potato mixture. Let it begin to set,

<!-- p.211 -->
Breakfast Eggs, Five Methods       205
then stir to show the wet egg to the hot pan surface. Try not to break the potatoes. 
Spread contents fairly evenly then move the pan to a preheated 425˚F oven. Cook 
only until the eggs are set on top, do not allow the tortilla to brown (it will be 
turned over to expose the well-cooked underside when serving). Time in the oven 
depends upon how far along stovetop cooking progressed. If minimal time on the 
stove, 12-15 minutes in the oven; if nearly set on the stove, 2-4 minutes in the hot 
oven will be enough to set the top. 
 »  When eggs have set, remove the skillet from the oven. Using an oven mitt, carefully 
hold the skillet with one hand, while using your other hand to hold a plate ﬁrmly 
against the top of the pan. Flip with conﬁdence to release the tortilla to the serving 
plate. Allow to cool or nearly so; the Spanish tortilla is baked in advance and served 
at room temperature, often next to cooked greens, sliced avocado and a piece of 
local bread. 
 »  Options will call to you. Use mushrooms, herbs, tomatoes—your choice according 
to what you know. For those who aren’t attached to the customs of the Spanish 
tortilla, it is ﬁne to place the semi-cooked tortilla into the oven with the broiler on, 
allowing the top to brown, then serve directly from the skillet (once cooled a bit), 
using a wooden spatula or spoon. 
Souﬄé is a cooking method that opens the eggs to maximum ﬂuﬃness and therefore exem-
pliﬁes the Fire Elemental Phase cooking method. Fire Element cooking is not about direct ﬂame, 
it refers to the expansive, swirling, or explosive energetic of a dish. Popcorn is an example of 
Fire Element or Phase cooking method applied to a grain. Te Fire Element cooking method 
has special resonance for the heart. Imagine the special oohs and ahhs and other heart-type 
sounds people make when a successful souﬄé makes it from the oven to the table. Te emo-
tions of the heart combine relaxation and engaged excitement. Although a souﬄé can be said 
to satisfy these criteria, it’s not everyday home cooking. 
Instead, for Fire Phase egg cooking, make ﬂuﬀy scrambled eggs. Whisk the eggs to incorporate 
air, then cook with quick, circular movements in a hot pan. Move the ﬂuﬀy eggs to the plate 
right away for immediate enjoyment. 
Preserved eggs represent the ﬁfth method, the Water Elemental Phase method that resonates 
with kidney health. Preserving supports longevity, in other words, health of the deepest level

<!-- p.212 -->
206       Breakfast Foods
(bones, marrow, brain, reproductive system, and genetic unfolding). Since eggs can spoil (the 
famous and unforgettable smell of rotten eggs), egg preservation was as important historically 
as it is rare in today’s age of mechanical refrigeration. 
Westerners are often deeply skeptical about preserved eggs. It’s common for visitors in China 
to be given hard-boiled eggs for their congee and notice their Chinese hosts relishing preserved 
eggs. Ask for them if you’d like your tourist status raised to initiate level, or better yet, make 
them in your own home. (Note: Some commercial preserved eggs coming from China are 
made with toxic chemicals to shortcut the lengthy process. Tese should be avoided complete-
ly, but the traditionally made eggs are quite safe.)  
Te traditional process for century egg or thousand-year-old egg (same thing, both take 2-3 
months) works by covering raw eggs (in the shell) with a “mud” made of strong tea, ﬁreplace 
ash, pickling lime (calcium hydroxide, use great care), salt and rice chaﬀ (the outer hull re-
moved between harvesting and storing rice). Once covered, the eggs (usually duck or quail 
eggs, but often enough from chickens) are placed in an earthenware pot, covered with more 
pickling lime and clay, then buried in the backyard. After two or three months, dig up your 
eggs, rinse oﬀ the special mud, peel, cut into sections and add to congee. Tey are usually 
cooked brieﬂy or simply warmed by the congee, but once cured, preserved eggs can be eaten 
as is (due to the chemical and fermentation reactions taking place in high alkalinity during 
their time in the dark). 
Salt-cured egg yolks, in contrast, take only an hour and a half to make, and produce a lovely, 
semi-runny yolk that satisﬁes both the occasional desire for trendy preparations and the Water 
Element method without the complexity of thousand-year-old eggs (or the reasonable worries 
over contaminated commercially manufactured ones).   
 »  Prepare a bowl or shallow tray with 1 cup kosher salt and 1 cup granulated sugar 
(don’t worry, the salt and sugar draw moisture out of the yolk and will be washed 
away).
 »  Crack 2 eggs per person, separating the whites and yolks. Place each yolk in a 
slight hollow made into the salt-sugar mixture. Spoon just enough of the salt-sugar 
mixture over each yolk to lightly but completely cover it. (Cook’s tip: Use an un-
cracked egg to press the hollows into the salt-sugar mix.)

<!-- p.213 -->
Breakfast Eggs, Five Methods       207
 »  Allow to sit at room temperature for an hour and a half. Ten, reach in and remove 
the yolks with clean ﬁngers. Gently rinse oﬀ all the salt-sugar, drip oﬀ as much 
water as possible, then place the perfectly semi-runny yolks into porridge or onto 
crusty bread. Garnish with parsley, chives, dill or herbs of your choice. Te yolk is 
similar in consistency to a nicely poached egg. No cooking is necessary. 
 » For a solidly cured yolk, allow to sit in the salt-sugar mix for a week, refrigerated, 
then rinse carefully and place the yolks in a 170˚F oven to dry for one hour. Solidly 
cured yolks are chopped or grated to be used as condiments on pasta, risotto, green 
vegetables, ﬁsh or a Cooked Salad’ (see page 49). 
Note: Be sure to purchase high-quality eggs if consuming them less than 
thoroughly cooked. If uncomfortable with these traditional methods, 
follow the FDA advice to cook eggs until ﬁrm. 
Tere are countless egg recipes. What is important in order to understand any recipe is:
 » How cooking methods change the health inﬂuence of the ingredients
 » Not to overwhelm the properties of the principal food with too many additions 
A Note on the Food Energetics
Are eggs good for us or not?  Do they contribute to heart disease or are they an excellent source of 
protein that has been unduly maligned?  Medical science seems conﬂicted, reﬂected by diﬀering 
health reports in the media. Even in Chinese medicine, eggs have been debated. Foods with that 
much protein and fat are usually seen as warming, but the great master of digestive medicine Li 
Dong-Yuan (1180-1251 CE) used eggs as a cooling food.  
What is not debated is that eggs are the reproductive eﬀort of the chicken. As such, they 
connect wei qi level with yuan qi. Tis means that eggs resonate with the deep source level of 
health (yuan qi, reproductive health, genetics, bone health, cell division, etc.) and with the 
very active energy known as wei qi (protective, immune, defensive energy). Tis makes eggs 
a very powerful food. For some people this power can be grounding, calming and even cool-
ing, while for others, eggs are stimulating and warming (or can even arouse an inﬂammatory 
response). It’s important to know your own response, through experience. Pair that with the

<!-- p.214 -->
208       Breakfast Foods
cooking method that matches your personal health status best.
Well-prepared, eggs nourish deep reserves, helping to restore digestion and kidney health af-
ter periods of deﬁciency or exhaustion. If the liver is congested, too many eggs can make 
things worse. To prevent stagnation that will spread to the blood level (arteriosclerosis), in-
clude chives, scallions, dill, tarragon or ginger with eggs and avoid pairing of eggs with cheese 
or cream.
Fried eggs confer the most yang qi (and will stimulate wei/defensive qi the most). For deﬁciency 
of qi and blood, use eggs in Congee (see recipe page 180) or as Egg Drop Soup (see recipe page 93). 
A Note on Black Pepper:
I haven’t mentioned black pepper very often. As an herb, black pepper warms the mid-
dle, particularly the stomach, so it is useful if cold has penetrated to the stomach (signs 
would include fatigue, phlegm, brain fog, undigested food in the stool, etc.). Cold in 
the stomach is part of yang qi deﬁciency overall, usually due to stress and tiredness for 
long periods of time. Cold and raw food (sushi, salads, cold drinks, ice cream, smooth-
ies, juicing) also play an important role in developing cold in the stomach, but if yang 
qi is suﬃcient, cold foods can overheat the stomach as the body responds (perhaps too 
much) to the “insult” cold makes on this organ that only works well when it is warm. 
Terefore, the introduction of too much cold for the stomach can result in signs and 
symptoms of cold, heat, or a combination. Tis is a very common scenario in the clinic. 
Black pepper can easily dry and overheat a stressed stomach, making things worse. 
But with some care, it has its uses in a healthy diet. In the context of breakfast eggs, a 
moderate amount of freshly cracked black pepper helps arouse and activate yang qi and 
therefore can be nice in the morning. Black pepper should be avoided by people with 
hemorrhoids, thinning of the stomach yin mucosal linings, dehydration, and those with 
any bleeding disorder or other heat signs.

<!-- p.215 -->
Breakfast Eggs, Five Methods       209
Other Types of Eggs
Chicken eggs are by far the most common, but eggs from quail, duck, goose and even ostrich 
or emu are available. Each oﬀers a slightly diﬀerent energetic proﬁle. Duck and goose eggs 
help relax the nervous system and are much less likely to arouse inﬂammation. Quail eggs are 
traditionally used for children with weak constitutional health. 
Dietary cholesterol is necessary to build and restore hormones that help us manage stress, 
including the soothing hormone oxytocin. Without enough good quality fat in the diet, stress 
can be unmanageable. Eggs are an excellent source of the richness needed to build these hor-
mones. Have some eggs cooked with butter to provide the support you need, but include 
kitchen herbs and skillful cooking methods to ensure the most beneﬁt.

<!-- p.216 -->
Afterword 
Tere is a legend in Chinese medicine of a mythic master who received information on the use 
of food and herbs directly from nature. His name was Shen Nong, which means Divine Farmer. 
He is said to have had a transparent stomach—in other words, he would eat something and 
then look inside to watch things work. According to the story, Shen Nong would try one hun-
dred plants each day, and when one poisoned him, the next would enact the cure. It’s a story 
of how humanity learned to identify some of nature’s oﬀerings as food for daily nourishment, 
some as gentle medicines to get back on track, and others best reserved as emergency medicines. 
Tese teachings were organized further into a coded manual for personal evolution by the 
Daoist sage Tao Hong-Jing in the ﬁfth to sixth century CE. (Several modern translations of 
the Shen Nong Ben Cao are available.)  
Imagine you can see into your own digestion, like Shen Nong. What dialogue would you have 
with your belly on an ordinary day?  
Mind: I woke feeling a bit tired, so let’s eat especially well today. 
Belly: Always a good idea. 
Mind: I’m wondering, do we need something strong, some animal food to feel grounded and 
more energized?  Or do we need a vegetarian day instead, with some soup, so that nourish-
ment is as easy as possible to receive and assimilate?  
Belly: I can send signals, but I don’t decide what I have to digest.
Mind: Right. I’ll use my intention and look inside. 
Digestion is the central hub that supports all other functions. We return to diet again and 
again because we can use food to reach everywhere in the body. If, for example, the lower back 
feels achy, indicating the adrenal glands and kidney yang aren’t well rested, the transformations 
directed by pancreas-spleen won’t have full power. Tat would be a good day to eat fairly light-
ly or have clear meals. Starting there (before consistently adding foods with kidney resonance, 
along with more calm and better sleep) can greatly improve lower back pain and achy knees. 
Or, if well rested and experiencing cravings with poor digestion of basic foods, honest looking

<!-- p.217 -->
Afterword        211
within will reveal some disorder of the intestinal microbiota. I’ve oﬀered tools to help you 
understand these and many other scenarios from a dietary point of view so you know how to 
eat well while enacting repair. 
We all have tendencies; we must know them. Be personal with yourself. Do you tend to have 
reﬂux, bloating, tightness, pain, mild discomfort, burping?  Or, do you tend to have elimina-
tion problems such as constipation, loose stool, or an alternating scenario, perhaps with hem-
orrhoids?  Or, are your tendencies toward immune system issues, such as hay fever, airborne 
or food allergies, asthma, or more severe autoimmune conditions including lupus, multiple 
sclerosis, rheumatoid arthritis, and more?  Or, do you get kidney or gall stones, or ﬁbroids, a 
metabolic disease such as diabetes, or simply gain weight (dampness) along with sinus prob-
lems, sleep problems, and food cravings?  
Genuine, sustained health starts when the mind and belly are in a real dialogue. We need our 
minds to learn to listen to the wisdom that is inherent in the body, and that means we need 
to learn to understand the language the body uses to speak to us. Tere is wisdom in the belly, 
and the heart, liver, pancreas-spleen, kidneys, lungs, blood, glands, bones, everything, and very 
much also in the microbes that live within us. Teir language is subtle, often only heard when 
symptoms can’t be ignored. But in truth, the messages are always coming. We start to really 
care about ourselves when we listen to the quiet messages from within and not only the pains 
and discomforts. We know how to eat when we identify what we truly want within a world of 
abundant, complicated, and market-driven choices. It’s a message that everyone can learn to 
understand. A bit like the legendary Shen Nong, we, too, can become aware of our deep selves 
and the way diﬀerent foods inﬂuence us.

<!-- p.218 -->
212       Afterword
Look inside; insight is one of the gifts of our wondrous human intelligence. Combine 
knowledge, mental intention, and the calm clarity of mind that exists within, even when 
we may feel like we have little access to it. All eating needs to be individualized, with a real 
knowledge of our state of health and how diﬀerent foods inﬂuence us (and why). 
Tere are so many ways to relate to food. We eat to fuel our bodies, to have a rhythm in 
the day, to socialize and bond with others, to feel our cultural roots, to comfort ourselves or 
dream of a new direction. Hidden within all the economics, entertainment, health advice 
and cultural complexities is an open secret: food is a gift and eating is for life. Nothing 
grows without the gifts of sunshine and rain from above and the rich earth below. Some-
thing relaxes at a very deep level within us when we give in and recognize this. Skillful food 
choices protect our deepest life—what we now know can be called prenatal qi—by gather-
ing well from what nature oﬀers. Digestion is the center—from there, all our organs con-
tribute to transforming food into what we now can understand as postnatal qi, responsible 
for health maintenance, growth, daily energy, and clarity of mind and spirit. 
And while this may seem esoteric or grand, it is our personal and practical part of the eso-
teric and grand. It is our individual participation in the wheel of life, combining the earthy 
routines of shopping, cooking and eating with an awareness that we are nourishing life and 
vitality. Tis, and so much more, is what we mean by welcoming food.

<!-- p.219 -->
Sauces and Condiments
I have included a variety of sauces within these recipes to make everyday cooking special. 
Crafting sauces can be a long training in the culinary arts and is its own career in formal restau-
rants. What interests me here diﬀers in two ways: 
 »  Sauces must actively aid digestion rather than add heaviness or contribute to dietary 
excess (as is sometimes the case when sauces are crafted merely for pleasure or tradi-
tion).
 »  Tey must be easy to make for busy people with varying levels of kitchen skill. 
Sauces, then, are similar to appetizers and the use of spices—to be truly successful, they must 
facilitate digestion of the foods they accompany, not merely be delicious. Sauces achieve this 
through the use of the fermented and acidic note of good vinegars and suitable use of wines, 
along with fruit peels, spices, oils, and sometimes thickening agents. Great cooking is no lon-
ger about surprise, expensive ingredients, or dazzling technique. Great cooking brings together 
these factors: 
 » An understanding of the personal health of the people for whom you are cooking
 » Te use of good quality ingredients selected for season and individual health status 
(not necessarily expensive or in any way exotic)
 » Te choices of cooking methods (baking, sautéing, steaming, roasting, preserving, 
etc.) that are well matched to the individuals and the speciﬁc foods 
 » Te use of appetizers, side dishes, kitchen herbs, spices, and sauces to aid digestion 
of the meals they are part of 
In some cases, sauces are very speciﬁc to the dishes they accompany, but with small adjust-
ments, many can be creatively repurposed. In this way, a small set of sauces can enliven many 
diﬀerent dishes, which is, of course, exactly how ﬁne restaurants work. At home, most of us 
don’t keep multiple sauces at the ready as restaurant kitchens do, so feel free to mix and match 
as you cook, as long as you honor the principles described above. A meal needs to be digested 
well by the individual or it is not good food for them, no matter how tasty (or how highly

<!-- p.220 -->
214       Sauces and Condiments
recommended it is in the press or by nutrition experts). 
I am repeating sauces from the recipes in this appendix for easy reference. Te energetics of 
these sauces are explained where they ﬁrst appear (those page numbers are included here). Feel 
free to use these sauces in diﬀerent and creative ways.   
Basic Vinaigrette 
Tis is the dressing on the Arugula Salad with Paprika Almonds in the Ap-
petizer section on page 16. A basic vinaigrette like this can also be used on 
steamed green vegetables, steamed root vegetables, ﬁsh or meat (and on a 
garden salad, of course, during warm weather).
fresh lemon juice | 2 tsp
balsamic vinegar | 1 Tbsp
dijon mustard | 1/2 tsp
olive oil | 4 Tbsp
sea salt | chef’s pinch
Prepare the dressing by whisking together the lemon juice, balsamic, and mustard, then driz-
zling in the olive oil while continuing to whisk. Add a pinch of salt.  Red wine vinegar is often 
used in place of balsamic, and the lemon juice is optional.

<!-- p.221 -->
Sauces and Condiments        215
Dressing for Asian-Style Black Rice Salad
Tis is the dressing for Rice of Original Qi in the Grains section, page 35. 
Here I have halved the amounts to be more appropriate for smaller dishes. See 
the original recipe for amounts for the grain salad.
scallion | 1/4 cup, finely sliced (raw)
grapeseed oil | 1/2 cup
tamari | 2.5 Tbsp
black vinegar |  1 Tbsp (Shanxi black vinegar is a barley-sorghum vinegar from cen-
tral China. Substitute rice vinegar and balsamic, 1/2 Tbsp each, if 
Shanxi vinegar is hard to find.)
toasted sesame oil | 1 Tbsp
sea salt | to taste
Whisk the ingredients together in a mixing bowl. Pour the dressing over the vegetables, stir to 
mix. Gently mix in the rice. Taste for balance, adjust as needed.
Olive Oil and Sea Salt  (see page 51)
It hardly rises to the level of a sauce, but in my kitchen a quick splash of extra virgin olive oil 
and a generous pinch of ﬁne salt is the everyday dressing for steamed greens of all kinds. 
Don’t be afraid of oil or salt. Oil is an essential nutrient; it’s needed to build hormones and 
healthy tissues (including brain cells). 
Salt, too, is an essential nutrient. Te lower small intestine includes intricate salt-absorption 
mechanisms to ensure we aren’t deﬁcient. To reduce excess sodium without speciﬁcally trying, 
cook your own food, salt it well so it is delicious and satisfying, eat no junk food, fewer snacks, 
and fewer restaurant meals.

<!-- p.222 -->
216        Sauces and Condiments
Homemade ponzu Sauce (for dipping or dressing) (page 51)
tamari | ½ cup
water | ¼ cup
rice wine vinegar | 2 Tbsp (a low acid vinegar—if replacing with another, use a bit 
less)
fresh ginger | 3 slices, slivered
shallots | 2 medium, trimmed, sliced
lime | juice of 1 lime or ½ meyer lemon (use Japanese yuzu citrus if available)
Tahini Dressing  (page 51)
Whisk to combine:
tahini | 3 Tbsp
tamari | 1 tsp
mirin | 1 tsp
rice vinegar or lemon juice | 1 tsp
toasted sesame seed oil | 1 Tbsp
water | 3 Tbsp (use warm water, add more gradually if needed)
optional: 
honey | 1 tsp
Shanxi vinegar | ½ tsp
A simpler tahini sauce can be made in moments with: 
tahini | ½ cup
lemon | juice of 2 fresh lemons

<!-- p.223 -->
Sauces and Condiments       217
Butter, Balsamic and Red Wine Glaze (see page 132)
olive oil | 1 Tbsp
butter | 2 Tbsp
balsamic vinegar | 3 Tbsp
red wine (optional) | 1 Tbsp
Heat a heavy pan. Add the olive oil, then the butter. Add the balsamic and the red wine. 
Add herbs if desired, such as rosemary, oregano, basil, chives, thyme. Salt is not necessary—
enough may be included in the butter, and in any case the rich tart and sweet notes of the 
balsamic vinegar are suﬃcient. 
If the pan sauce is thin, raise heat to reduce and add another bit of butter to tighten the sauce. 
Use with mushrooms, vegetables, over meats or ﬁsh. 
Mustard Cream Sauce
butter | 1 tsp
prepared French mustard | 2 Tbsp
heavy cream | 1 cup
nutmeg | several scrapes, perhaps 1/8 tsp
parsley | half a handful, trimmed and chopped
sea salt | pinch
fresh black pepper | a few grinds
Add the butter to a heavy pan, and then the mustard (grainy or Dijon style) and the heavy cream. 
Whisk gently, cooking hot enough to bubble and reduce for 2-3 minutes. Add the parsley leaves. 
Add a few scrapes of fresh nutmeg, just enough to notice as a mysterious hint of complexity. Test 
for salt (none will be needed if salted butter was used). Crack a few grinds of pepper on top. 
Serve as a bed for ﬁsh or lean meat or as sauce for rice and lentils.

<!-- p.224 -->
218        Sauces and Condiments
BEURRE BLANC  
(appears as an option for poached salmon on page 108) 
I generally don’t recommend dairy or this amount of animal fat; however, 
with the spices and herbs it’s easy to understand that the calories and animal 
fat here will digest much better than equal calories and animal fat from other 
common dishes (for example, ice cream). Again: allow yourself occasional 
indulgences at home to avoid cravings and bingeing. 
shallots | 2 medium, minced
white wine | ¼ cup
white wine vinegar | ¼ cup
unsalted butter | 10 or more Tbsp, added gradually
fresh dill | 2 tsp, chopped
In a small saucepan, add 2 minced shallots, 1/4 cup dry white wine, 1/4 cup white wine vine-
gar. Reduce over medium heat until nearly dry. 
Add an enormous amount of good quality unsalted butter—begin with 2 Tbsp. 
Whisk into the shallot reduction until nearly melted, then add 2 more Tbsp at a time until the 
sauce is creamy and fragrant (10-12 Tbsp butter all told). Add 2 tsp chopped fresh dill, whisk. 
Taste for salt, add if needed. Spoon a bit of beurre blanc over each piece of poached ﬁsh. Finish 
with a few fronds of fresh dill. It’s a lot of butter, but only a spoon or so of the beurre blanc is 
used per portion.

<!-- p.225 -->
Sauces and Condiments       219
Ginger and Scallion Sauce 
(for Fish with Ginger and Scallion, page 111)
grapeseed oil (or other high quality oil) | 1 Tbsp
fresh ginger | 3-6 slices, slivered
scallions | 3-5, coarsely chopped
sake or dry white wine | 3/4 cup (substitute water if you wish)
mirin | 1 Tbsp (or 1/2 tsp sweetener)
tamari | 1 tsp
cilantro | handful (half a bunch)
toasted sesame oil | a few drops (1/2 tsp)
salt and fresh pepper | to taste
Add the oil to a large, hot pan. Add the ginger and scallions. Add the ﬁsh to the pan. Working 
quickly, add the sake, mirin and tamari. Cover, and allow to steam on medium high heat for 
8-10 minutes. In the last minute of cooking, add the chopped cilantro, the toasted sesame oil, 
and salt and pepper to taste. 
Use your creativity to apply the ginger with scallion sauce to dishes beyond ﬁsh. 
Fermented Black Bean Sauce  (see page 124)
scallions | 2-3, sliced coarsely
fresh ginger | 3 slices, finely slivered
ﬁve-spice powder | 1/4 tsp
grapeseed oil (or organic peanut oil) | 2 Tbsp
fermented black beans | 2 Tbsp, soaked & rinsed, whole or chopped
tamari | 1/4 cup
rice wine | 1 cup

<!-- p.226 -->
220       Sauces and Condiments
organic corn starch (or kuzu powder) | 1 Tbsp, mixed with cool water
fresh cilantro | a few sprigs per person as garnish
Soak the dried fermented beans in a bowl of water (this can be done well in advance of cooking 
to save time). In a hot pan or wok, add the cooking oil, ginger slivers, the scallion and the ﬁve-
spice powder. Add a pinch of salt (optional). Add the soaked fermented black beans (whole, 
mashed or chopped, as you like, but do not add the soaking liquid). 
After a moment, splash in the tamari and the cooking wine. As soon as the dish is hot and most 
of the alcohol has evaporated (4-5 minutes), add the dissolved cornstarch (or kuzu) and stir. 
Cornstarch thickens when it reaches bubbling temperature. As soon as it does, the dish is ready. 
Adjust with more dissolved cornstarch if thin, or open with a splash of water if too thick. Add the 
cilantro, then serve immediately over ﬁsh, scallops, tofu, meats, steamed rice, or green vegetable.
Master Sauce 
(used to make Braised Chicken with Soy and Ginger, see page 129) 
Tis sauce also works well with ﬁsh and tofu. 
tamari | 1 cup
sake or white wine | 1 cup
barley malt or other sweetener | 1/3 cup
water | 1.5 cups
scallions | 4, cut into 4-6 pieces each
fresh ginger | a chunk the size of your thumb, cut into 5 pieces
star anise | 2-3
allspice | 4-5 whole
peppercorns | up to 1 tsp
organic citrus peel (optional) | 2 strips

<!-- p.227 -->
Sauces and Condiments        221
Add the wet ingredients to the pan: the tamari, sake, barley malt and water.  Add the scallions 
and spices, and citrus peel if desired. Swirl gently to combine ingredients. 
Bring the liquids to a boil, then reduce immediately to the lowest simmer. Add the chicken, 
ﬁsh, or tofu. Allow the dish to simmer, partially covered until the chicken or ﬁsh is done (30-
40 minutes for chicken, 8-10 for ﬁsh, 5 minutes for tofu). Heat should be low but enough for 
occasional bubbling. 
Serve the chicken, ﬁsh or tofu over rice with a generous spoonful of the sauce. 
Balsamic Sauce with Apricot and Citrus Peel 
( for duck, beef, lamb, pork, salmon or roast vegetables such 
as parsnip, see page 132)
balsamic vinegar | 3/4 cup
red wine | 1/2 cup
dried prunes or apricots | 3
dried tangerine peel (or orange zest) | 1 medium strip or 1/4 tsp
butter (optional) | 1 Tbsp
Add the balsamic and wine to a saucepan, bring to a boil, reduce to simmer. Cut the apricots 
and/or prunes into small bits, add to the pan. Add the citrus peel (ﬁnely chopped, or a single 
piece to be removed before serving). Reduce to nearly a syrup consistency (timing depends 
upon heat—be careful to prevent burning!)  Just before serving, whisk in the butter (optional). 
Tamarind Sauce 
( Found in the sautéed ground meat recipes on page 140, but could be 
used for many dishes) 
scallions | 3-5
balsamic vinegar | 2 Tbsp

<!-- p.228 -->
222       Sauces and Condiments
tamarind paste | 1 Tbsp
tamari | 1 Tbsp
cilantro | 1/4 cup, chopped
parsley | 1/4 cup, chopped
Tis is very forgiving cooking. Add the ingredients to the pan, stir often enough, add liquid if 
it seems too dry, turn heat higher if it seems too wet. Tis type of sauce works easily and very 
well with chickpea or other vegetarian dishes. 
Marinade or Grilling Sauce 
for beef or other meats (see page 143)
olive oil | 1/4 cup
lime juice | 1/2 lime
vinegar (Shanxi is my preference but any good will do) | 2 Tbsp 
tamari | 2 Tbsp 
Use this for a quick marinade for pan-searing meats. For outdoor grilling, use a high-heat oil 
like grapeseed in place of olive. 
Tamari-Mirin Glaze (for grilled fish, see page 147)
tamari (or soy sauce) | 1/4 cup
mirin | 2 Tbsp
rice vinegar | 1 Tbsp
fresh ginger | 2 slices, slivered
In a saucepan, bring ingredients to a boil then quickly lower to a simmer. Cook to reduce to 
about half, or until consistency is thickened almost to a syrup (about 5 minutes). 
Serve as a sauce for grilled meat, ﬁsh, shrimp or tofu.

<!-- p.229 -->
Sauces and Condiments       223
Condiments 
Condiments are preparations that are typically added right at the table, including mustard, 
ketchup, hot sauce, relishes, and so forth. Like sauces and spices, condiments are often mis-
used, missing their potential to add signiﬁcant beneﬁts while also appealing to taste. 
Two sesame seed condiments are included just after the Grains chapter, one from the Medi-
terranean and the other from Asia. Make them periodically. Keep a dish of one or the other 
on the table to sprinkle over grains, steamed or sautéed vegetables, beans, seaweeds, or ﬁsh. 
Or, for a healthy and delicious snack, simply dip carrot sticks or some bread in olive oil before 
touching them to the sesame seed mix. 
Gomasio  (see page 41)
sesame seeds (unhulled is best) | 1 cup
sea salt | 1/2-1 tsp
Heat the sesame seeds in a dry pan, shaking to manage toasting without burning.   Add the 
salt. Stir to roast over medium heat for 5-10 minutes. A beautiful toasted sesame seed aroma 
will let you know that you can move them to a bowl to cool (leaving them in the pan will allow 
seeds to burn from residual heat). Grind the seed-salt mixture in a suribachi or standard mortar 
and pestle. Gomasio stays fresh on the table for a few days or a few weeks if refrigerated. 
Za’atar   (see page 39)
sesame seeds | 2 Tbsp
sumac (ground) | 4 Tbsp (if you prefer, 1/4 cup)
thyme | 2 Tbsp 
oregano | 1 Tbsp
marjoram | 2 Tbsp 
salt | 1/2-1 tsp

<!-- p.230 -->
224       Sauces and Condiments
Heat the sesame seeds in a dry pan, shaking to manage toasting without burning.   Add the 
other ingredients, continue to shake while heating, only 3-5 minutes in total. Set za’atar in a 
shallow dish, ﬁll another with olive oil. Dip good bread into oil then into za’atar for snack. For 
complete meal, sprinkle over grains, bean dishes, etc., or accompany the dipping method with 
Greek style yogurt or spiced mashed fava beans.

<!-- p.231 -->
A Well-Stocked Kitchen:  
Tings to Have on Hand
In the fourth century CE, the famous Daoist herbalist/alchemist known as Ge Hong wrote a 
book called Zhou Hou Jiu Zu Fang, or Remedies to Have Up Your Sleeve. Te term zhou means 
elbow, which is up the sleeve of a traditional gentleman’s loose ﬂowing robe. It’s a charming-
ly colloquial title for such a classic text. What Ge Hong oﬀered are formulas to make with 
what any household is likely to have on hand, at your elbow. I’ve referred to one of his most 
famous remedies on page 125 here, the idea of using fermented black beans and the whites 
of scallion to treat the onset of common cold. Get it at the right time and it’s very eﬀective. 
More than that, it isn’t just a remedy, it is the implementation of clear and practical theory 
(the salty, fermented black soybeans nourish stomach ﬂuids while the particular spiciness of 
the scallion whites stimulate the qi to spread those ﬂuids where they are needed). In Chinese 
homes, scallions and fermented black beans could be expected to be always “on hand”. Every-
one can stock those ingredients, but that’s not the point. Ge Hong is saying that if we know 
what we’re doing, we can  stay healthy with a well-stocked cupboard or pantry in any culture. 
Good cooking is always personal. 
And good cooking depends on good shopping. Good shopping has two main branches. You 
can plan meals in advance and shop to a list, or arrive at a good market and build meals on 
what looks freshest. As cooks we should be skilled in both ways of thinking. Either way, some 
ingredients keep well and are perfect for the pantry or cupboard, things like oils, vinegars, 
salt and spices, soy sauce, noodles, rice, millet, oats, dried beans, dried fruits, seaweeds, and 
so forth. Shopping lists are always incomplete and should always remain ﬂexible. In this list, I 
have tried to include what you’ll need to cook the recipes in this book, shopping only for fresh 
ingredients and to replenish cupboard supplies as they run low. 
In these recipes I have tried to strike a balance between common ingredients familiar to most 
everyone and a slightly widened list. I have also tried to use the less familiar ingredients often 
enough that you won’t buy a bottle of mirin, for example, only to use it for one recipe and 
discover it in the back of the cupboard years later. It’s a balancing act.  What is most important

<!-- p.232 -->
226       A Well-Stocked Kitchen: Things to Have on Hand
throughout this book is that classical Chinese medicine dietary principles can be applied for 
daily cooking with any foods that are available and appealing to you. If you are inspired to 
explore more than I’ve included, that’s wonderful, but no one should be left thinking that the 
precious value of the Chinese medicine dietary tradition can only (or best) be enacted with 
foods from Asia, or anything that is unfamiliar. 
With this in mind, here is a suggested cupboard or pantry list to help with cooking the recipes 
included here. A well-stocked pantry supplements shopping for fresh foods in an essential way. 
When the cupboard is full, going to the market is focused on vegetables, eggs, ﬁsh or meat (if 
included in the meal plan). And, of course, before shopping for fresh foods, take a look in the 
cupboard for what pantry items are running low.  Feel free to add favorites to this list, cross 
out some that I have included that you won’t use, and above all, make your cooking your own.

<!-- p.236 -->
Frequent Conversions
I do most of my cooking in New York, but it’s a big world, and cooks from most other coun-
tries use the metric system rather than the antiquated system of ounces, pounds, cups, and 
Fahrenheit measurements that we maintain in the States. Te history of these diﬀerences or 
the relative superiority of the metric system isn’t our focus here, but a quick conversion guide 
for the measurements included often in these recipes may be useful. 
Approximation is welcome—it’s my hope that once experienced with a recipe, more cooks will 
abandon measuring tools and return to human measures: half a handful of this, pinch or two 
of that, enough water to rise to the ﬁrst knuckle, and so forth. Tink of the way a recipe gets 
written. First you just know how to cook the dish, then you must measure things and write the 
recipe, correct it a bit and ﬁnalize, then hope that the reader will reverse the process, measuring 
once or twice then no longer needing to, as you just know how to cook the dish. 
Weights  
American  
Custom
Metric  
Equivalent
How to Simplify
1 pound
454 grams
In America, things like tofu are often packaged by the pound. 
In the rest of the world, 500g is common. 
2.2 pounds
1 kilo
In most cases, think of half a kilo as a bit more than a pound. 
8 ounces 
(weight)
227 grams
A standard ﬁsh or meat portion is often 8 ounces, or 1/4 kilo.
6 ounces
170 grams
Fish rich in oils (salmon, for example) are best in smaller por-
tions, 6 ounces or just 4, in other words, 170 or 115 grams, re-
spectively. High quality meats are also good in smaller portions 
such as 4-6 ounces/115-170g.

<!-- p.237 -->

Temperature
Fahrenheit
Celsius
How to Simplify
375˚F
190˚C
Tis is a standard setting for most baking. 
400˚F
204˚C
A “hot” oven (at least for home cooking). 200˚C will do.
325˚F
160˚C
A “moderate” oven, used for slower baking such as granola.
170˚F
77˚C
A “coolest setting” oven, used for drying soaked nuts, etc.  
 
Cups, Teaspoons & Tablespoons
American  
Custom
Metric  
Equivalent
How to Simplify
1 cup  
(dry ingredients)
240 milliliter 
(ml)
I often measure dry ﬂour in a liquid measuring cup. It’s 
imprecise but more sensible than weight, because a cup of 
cornmeal weighs a quarter more than the same of wheat 
ﬂour and nearly twice that of rolled oats, as examples. Pre-
cise weight measurements would infer a speciﬁcity beyond 
what’s necessary. Keep it simple.
8 cups  
(or 2 quarts)  
(liquid ingredients)
1.9 liters
Fairly large measures of water are used for congee or stock 
recipes. Tink of 8 cups as 2 liters, no problem. A pot of 
congee for a family of 4-6 will take 3 liters of water, perhaps 
more, by the time it is ﬁnished. 
1 tablespoon 
(Tbsp) 
(dry or liquid 
ingredients)
15 ml
Most of my international cooking colleagues use teaspoons 
and tablespoons rather than 5 or 15 ml. 
1 teaspoon (tsp) 
(dry or liquid 
ingredients)
5 ml
Tablespoons and teaspoons as measures are deﬁnitely not 
tablespoons or teaspoons used as cutlery (which can be of 
any size). But estimating is ﬁne, too. A tsp is one-third of a 
measuring Tbsp, and fractions of a tsp that I use for strong 
spices such as clove or nutmeg are the same as big pinch, 
couple of pinches, etc. Cook to your taste. 
Chef’s pinch
Chef’s pinch
Tis is, of course, not an oﬃcial measure. It is a way of 
saying that professional chefs season more aggressively than 
home cooks tend to. A chef’s pinch means a big pinch or 
two, more if needed. Use two ﬁngers and your thumb. 
I’ve heard ﬁne chefs speak in awe about another who has 
a perfect sense with the salt, just knowing by looking in 
the pan and touching the salt how much is needed. It’s a 
knack developed by creative attention, beyond the limits of 
measurement.

<!-- p.238 -->
Glossary of Selected Terms
All specialties are ﬁlled with a culture of words, their teachings populated with a specialized 
terminology, and while this can add precision (and sometimes beauty) to the discussion, ex-
pert jargon also acts (sometimes even deliberately) as a way to exclude interested outsiders. In 
my opinion, precision is admirable but exclusion distinctly less so.  
In both books of Welcoming Food, I have “translated” most technical terms from the Chinese 
and Western medical traditions into plain language (I highly recommend this exercise for 
anyone trained in any technical terminology). Tere are, however, some terms that are best 
used from classical Chinese because they have either entered English (e.g., yin and yang) or 
have nearly entered (e.g., qi). Additionally, some English words (such as dampness) are used 
here in special ways. Tese are explained when they appear in the text, and in the chapter on 
Preliminaries in Book 1. For convenience, some key terms are brieﬂy included here as well. 
Deﬁnitions are invitations, not an end to inquiry.  
k
Warming refers to foods that, regardless of their physical temperature, have a warming 
inﬂuence upon us after we eat them. An example is cinnamon.  
Cooling refers to foods that, regardless of their physical temperature, have a cooling inﬂuence 
within us. An example is celery; it has a cold inﬂuence when raw which is tempered to only 
cooling when cooked.  
Yang qi is the active, or moving energy of a living thing, and also the activating energy of any 
digestive aspect that may be considered chemical. Young children waking at sunrise to dance 
spontaneously on top of their beds is an example of yang qi, as are the way the enzymes in 
beansprouts stimulate micronutrient functions in digestion that otherwise would occur at a 
slower pace.  
Yin is the complement of yang (but not its opposite). Yin in this context is our bodily substance: 
our bones, muscles, tendons, anatomical organs, blood, hormones and any other aspect of our 
bodies that has substance and can actually be weighed. (Yang qi can’t be weighed.)

<!-- p.239 -->
Glossary of Selected Terms       233
Nourishing yin is a process of ﬁrst taking care not to further deplete, and then to skillfully 
support or “resupply” our healthy yin resources, especially blood and hormones, but also 
sometimes bones and suﬃcient muscle or ﬂesh. A person lacking healthy yin can be subject 
to weight gain, cravings, and various illnesses as the body seeks to restore yin without positive 
foods or suﬃcient rest. Examples of foods that can provide healthy yin vary to some degree for 
individuals, but can include nuts, seeds, eggs, certain vegetables, some types of animal foods, 
and well-chosen grains (as well as improved sleeping habits).  
Dampness describes a wide variety of problems symbolized by a kind of creeping ﬂood inside 
the body. Visualize the body not keeping up with internal housekeeping, allowing old ﬂuids 
to linger and stagnate, without providing fresh ﬂuids to fulﬁll necessary cleaning roles. Tis 
is water weight, swelling, or edema (in the lower limbs, hips or belly, neck or face, internal 
organs, or extremities such as ﬁngers or toes). Dampness can be obvious or hidden, and 
classically includes the kind of stickiness that is experienced as confusion, lethargy, hesitation, 
or brain fog.  
Cold (along with dampness) is another of the “climatic factors” described brieﬂy in the 
beginning of Book 1. Here, cold is not simply cold weather or cold drinks; it refers to the 
situation where cold somehow penetrates the body and takes up residence. At this point, cold 
is a pathogenic factor as real as any other. Illness comes not only from the cold itself, but from 
the inﬂammation that results as our bodies attempt to respond. Simply put, a cold inﬂuence 
is anything that slows our internal functions, including cold drinks and food, as well as too 
much raw food.  
Heat, similarly, can be a direct inﬂuence (too much fried or hot spicy food) or can be an 
internal response to cold or internal stagnation (slow digestion or constipation, for example). 
Internal heat can arise as the body sends yang qi to an area for healing purposes. If this healing 
response is repeated too often, or if the heating signals are steadily called for, this heat will 
eventually damage our own tissues. Heat can be another way of saying inﬂammation. Foods 
that tend to cause heat are the stimulants; foods that help clear heat are most vegetables, some 
more than others.

<!-- p.240 -->
234       Glossary of Selected Terms
Wind is a term meaning disruptive ﬂow of internal energy, such as the continuum from 
mild unease, ﬁdgetiness, restless leg syndrome, emotional outbursts, to tics, tremors, or even 
seizures. Wind is also about change; we need the ability to handle change well to stay happy 
and healthy.  
Kidney yang is the deep spark within that is said to reside in the kidneys. Kidney yang energizes 
digestion and metabolism, and therefore all our living functions. Kidney yang includes the role 
of the adrenal glands and their secretions.  
Kidney yin is the reserve beneath the “ﬁre” of kidney yang, like the wax beneath the ﬂame. 
Kidney yin includes the idea of hormone status. Although kidneys are seen as part of the lower 
torso, all hormones are in very complex interactions, and issues such as thyroid hormone 
deﬁciency are in close relation to the status of kidney yin.  
Kidney essence refers to the deepest level of kidney yin. Although sometimes interchangeable, 
when we say kidney essence we mean to indicate a very deep level of constitutional health. 
When the constitution is strong a lot of lifestyle or dietary stress can be tolerated, but the 
gradual decline of kidney essence is inevitable. Kidney essence is like a crystal at the center of 
our innermost engine; problems at this level can show as reduced fertility, tiredness, lack of 
ambition or will, and so forth.  
Lung yin is the moistness and ﬂexibility of the lungs. Te lung’s moistness relates to the 
moistness of the skin, the ability to sweat to relieve heat, to the stomach’s protective lining, 
and our immune integrity. Stagnation of lung yin manifests as sticky coughing or wheezing, 
dryness of the skin or premature wrinkling, and problems of the sinuses. Healthy lung yin 
status is one of a host of internal health aspects.  
Qi is the animating life force, related to breath and warmth. In practical terms, it refers to a 
subtle energy within, a potential to get something done or make a special connection. For 
example, if our digestive qi is weak, we will have trouble digesting even very “healthy” foods. 
Tis is an important concept. Like so much in classical thinking, qi is a collective term that is 
often used in various speciﬁc ways.

<!-- p.241 -->
Glossary of Selected Terms       235
Lung qi refers to the ability of the lungs to fulﬁl their functions, in conjunction with the other 
organs, in a complete way.  
Stomach ﬁre can be a positive or negative term depending on context. Enough stomach acid to 
digest foods (particularly proteins) is suﬃcient stomach ﬁre. Excess acid that sometimes rises above 
the safety of the stomach to cause reﬂux or GERD is excess stomach ﬁre (stomach directionality is 
an essential component here). Cold in the stomach or the inability to feel appetite or digest food 
is stomach ﬁre deﬁciency. Excess stomach ﬁre can manifest as dry lips or acne.  
Stomach yin is the protective capacity of the stomach, including the mucosal lining of the 
stomach and the duodenum.  
Blood deﬁciency is the sense within the body that blood is not rich enough or not full enough. 
Various internal compensations will be made. Blood-building foods are important to help 
those compensations relax and reverse.  
Qi deﬁciency is the sense that even though physical nutrition may be suﬃcient, a person still 
lacks a feeling of energy and enthusiasm.  
Wei qi is the energy behind our immune capability, from the details of the immune responses 
to the outward manifestations of “ﬁght or ﬂight” responses. Wei qi is a type of yang qi.  
k
More on these and other helpful terms can be found in chapter 3 of Welcoming Food, Book 1. 
Refer also to the index listings in both volumes.

<!-- p.255 -->
About the Cover
One morning I quickly tapped out some grains and small legumes into a pattern on our dining 
table, as monks do to create sand mandalas.  My son Ravi photographed the result, and then 
we did another.  I liked it, and with the encouragement of artist and design friends, we decid-
ed to use this personal art for the two covers of Welcoming Food (Cody Dodo did his magic 
turning the photographs into design).  Te evening after arranging these “grain paintings” we 
hosted a dinner of practitioners who had gathered in New York from many parts of the world. 
I cooked the cover art as part of our dinner—nothing wasted.

<!-- p.256 -->
About the Author
Andrew Sterman’s dietary work integrates Chinese medicine with modern cooking. He teach-
es cooking for health in classes and private sessions throughout North America, Europe and 
Australia, and his articles and online Food Chats have introduced classical food energetics to a 
widening circle of health professionals and food lovers. 
As a musician and composer heard on ﬁlm scores, recordings, live theater and concert stages 
around the world, Andrew has had unique opportunities to learn from chefs and family cooks 
of many cultures.  Tis integration of traditional food wisdom collected from decades of trav-
eling informs his special way of incorporating food and healing into daily cooking routines. 
Andrew lives in Manhattan with his wife, acupuncturist and author Ann Cecil-Sterman, and 
their two children.
